# **Part I — Orientation**

*Overview, the job-market case, the build-in-public system, and quick-start logistics — unchanged from the source document.*

## **▌ OVERVIEW**

**What This Document Is**

This is a 16-month, 290-topic, phase-based software engineering curriculum totaling 2700 hours, designed to take you from a beginner to a job-ready full-stack engineer earning ₹35-50 LPA. It's structured as a self-study roadmap with built-in "Exit Points" — specific milestones where you're ready to start applying for jobs at different salary levels.

## 

## **▌ Who Is This For?**

| CATEGORY | DETAILS |
| :---- | :---- |
| Primary audience | Aspiring software engineers in India, especially those with a BCA degree (addressed explicitly in the doc) |
| Career stage | Complete beginners to those wanting to level up to senior/staff roles |
| Time commitment | Flexible: 25-70 hrs/week depending on how fast you want to finish |
| Goal | Fastest path to employment at product companies, startups, and scale-ups |
| Companies targeted | Early-stage startups → Series A/B → Unicorns → Global remote (Vercel, Supabase, etc.) |

**KEY NOTE:**

The document explicitly addresses BCA graduates. It says BCA is fine for most target companies (startups, product companies, dev agencies) but becomes a hard filter at FAANG India and large IT services. It suggests MCA as a path if FAANG is a long-term goal.

# 

# **How This Will Get You a Job in a Fierce Market**

*Especially if you're a BCA graduate from a Tier‑2 college with zero projects and no work experience.*

**THE HONEST TRUTH – AND THE PLAN**

The market is tough, but it's not closed. Companies still hire – they hire people who can ship code, not people with fancy degrees. This curriculum is designed to turn you into that person.

Here's how it works for you:

**—** You build a real, deployable project (ClientSync) in the first 6 months — this becomes your portfolio, your proof of skill, and your conversation starter in interviews.

**—** You publish your work publicly — every phase gives you something to show on GitHub, dev.to, or LinkedIn. Recruiters see your progress, not your college name.

**—** You don't wait until the end to apply — the 'Exit Points' let you start applying as early as Phase 06, when you have a live app and a CI/CD pipeline. That's often enough for early-stage startups.

**—** The curriculum explicitly addresses the BCA degree filter — it tells you which companies don't care about your degree (most startups, product companies, dev agencies) and which do (FAANG, large IT services). You focus your energy where it matters.

**—** You learn exactly what's needed for the job — no fluff, no outdated theory. Every topic is chosen because it's used in production at top startups. You're job-ready, not just 'certified'.

**—** You build in public — by the time you finish, you have a network, a trail of content, and often inbound interest from founders. That's how you beat the 'no experience' trap.

**THE BOTTOM LINE**

Your BCA degree is not a blocker – it's a starting point. What gets you hired is your ability to build and ship. This roadmap gives you the exact path to do that, with clear milestones to keep you on track.

If you follow it consistently, you will have a portfolio, a GitHub history, and the confidence to walk into any interview. The jobs are there – you just need to be visible and credible. This document shows you how.

## **▌ How to Build in Public**

"Build in public" gets said so often it's stopped meaning anything. Here's what it actually means for this roadmap: at the end of most phases (not every day, not every commit), you post one specific, concrete thing you built or learned — not a status update, not "day 47 of my coding journey." Each phase in this document has a compact BUILD IN PUBLIC callout telling you exactly what to post, what to commit, and where. This section is the reasoning behind that system.

**WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN YOUR DEGREE**

A recruiter or hiring manager cannot verify "I know React." They can verify a GitHub repo with real commit history, a deployed URL, a green CI badge, and a dev.to post that explains a real decision you made. Visibility isn't vanity here — it's the only evidence a stranger can check in 90 seconds. Silence is the default; everyone in this roadmap is building the same 19 phases. The difference between two people with identical skills is which one has proof.

**GITHUB**

* Commit as you go, not in one dump at the end of a phase. A commit history with 3 commits on the last day of every phase reads as fabricated. It probably is.

* Use Conventional Commits from Phase 04 onward (feat:, fix:, refactor:, etc.) — it's a topic in the curriculum for a reason; a recruiter who opens your commit log should see discipline, not noise.

* Every repo gets a real README: what it does, how to run it, what you learned. "TODO: add README" sitting untouched for months is a worse signal than no README at all.

* Pin your 2–3 best repos on your GitHub profile. Don't make someone dig through 40 repos to find the one that matters.

**LINKEDIN**

* Post when you ship something visible — a deployed feature, a working demo, a hard bug you fixed. Not "studying TypeScript today," which nobody can evaluate.

* Lead with what it does or looks like (a screenshot, a short screen-recording), not with the tech stack. Recruiters skim; a visual gets 2 seconds of attention, a paragraph of jargon gets zero.

* One post per phase is enough. A LinkedIn feed with 40 low-effort posts is worse than one with 15 real ones — frequency without substance reads as noise, not progress.

**DEV.TO / HASHNODE**

* Write about a specific decision or a specific bug, never a tutorial recap of something already covered better elsewhere ("What is React?" gets you nothing — 500,000 of those already exist).

* The BUILD IN PUBLIC callouts flag which phases are worth a dev.to post and which aren't (Phase 08's DSA grind and Phase 15's build tooling aren't — skip posting, keep building).

* A post that says "here's a mistake I made and how I fixed it" outperforms one that says "here's how to do X correctly" — the former is credible because it's specific and honest; the latter reads as copied.

*The rule underneath all of it: post less than you think you should, and make sure what you do post could survive someone actually clicking through. A thin GitHub history with real commits beats a loud one with none.*

## **▌ Summary: Quick Start Checklist**

**01**   Decide your weekly hours (30 hrs/week recommended for full-time job)

**02**   Set up Phase 01 dev environment (VS Code, nvm, Git, SSH)

**03**   Start Phase 02 → Build Next.js app, deploy to Vercel

**04**   Continue phases sequentially

**05**   At Exit A (Phase 06\\) → Start applying to startups

**06**   At Exit ★1 (Phase 08\\) → Pass DSA rounds, get SDE-1 roles

**07**   After Phase 12 → Build an AI project

**08**   After Phase 17 → Senior-level roles

**09**   After Phase 19 → Complete profile, publish npm package

**10**   Choose an advanced project from the 10 listed

**CRITICAL ADVICE FROM THE DOCUMENT:**

"The gap between Exit 3 and Exit E is not technical — it's visibility and credibility. If you've been posting on dev.to, building in public, and engaging with the community throughout the 16 months, Exit E is a natural inbound moment. If you've done the curriculum silently with no public presence, the salary range is the same but you'll have to work harder." Build in public. Document your decisions. Ship consistently. Apply early (Exit A/B/1), not just at the end.

**STEP 1**

**Choose Your Timeline**

Pick a weekly commitment and stick with it.

| WEEKLY HOURS | REALISTIC TIMELINE | BEST FIT FOR |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 85 hrs/week | \~7.3 months | All-in sprint, no other commitments |
| 70 hrs/week | \~9 months | No job, no college |
| 40 hrs/week | \~16 months | Serious part-time |
| 40 → 70 hrs/week (ramp after 6 mo) | \~11.5 months | Start part-time, ramp later |
| 30 hrs/week | \~21 months | Full-time job \+ study |
| 25 hrs/week | \~25 months (\~2.1 yrs) | Casual evenings/weekends |

*Note: every month figure in this document — in this table, the Exit Point Ladder, the phase-by-phase breakdown, and the cover page — is calculated on a 40 hrs/week baseline (2,700 total hours ÷ 40 hrs/week ≈ 16 months). Rows above at a different weekly pace are converted from that same 2,700-hour total; your actual timeline scales directly with the hours/week you put in.*

*At 30 hrs/week, \~780 hours clear in 6 months — that lands mid-Phase 05 (Backend+DB+Auth), not Exit Point ★1. Exit ★1 needs 1,290 cumulative hours (end of Phase 08, \~32 weeks / \~7.4 months at 40 hrs/week). If the target is a December 2026 offer, the lever is weekly hours, not phase order.*

**STEP 2**

**The Exit Point Ladder**

Don't wait until the end to apply — each exit point is a real job-readiness milestone with its own target roles and resume highlights.

| EXIT | AFTER PHASE | MONTH (@40 HRS/WK) | JOB LEVEL | SALARY RANGE (INDIA) |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| A | Phase 06 | \~6.1 | Junior Full-Stack (deployed) | ₹6–10 LPA |
| B | Phase 07 | \~6.5 | Junior-to-Mid (API-literate) | ₹8–12 LPA |
| ★1 | Phase 08 | \~7.4 | Junior-to-Mid (interview-ready \+ DSA) | ₹8–15 LPA |
| C | Phase 10 | \~9.0 | Mid (production-grade \+ observability) | ₹12–18 LPA |
| ★2 | Phase 11 | \~9.9 | Mid-Level (real-time \+ search) | ₹15–25 LPA |
| D | Phase 12 | \~10.9 | Mid-Senior (AI-capable) | ₹20–30 LPA |
| 3 | Phase 17 | \~14.4 | Senior (distributed systems \+ infra) | ₹25–40 LPA |
| E | Phase 19 | \~15.6 | Complete Profile | ₹35–50 LPA |

**KEY INSIGHT**

Apply at Exit A, B, or ★1 — don't wait for the end.

**STEP 3**

**The 19 Phases**

**Phase 01–01b — Developer Environment & TypeScript Mastery**

**WEEKS 1–5**

HTML, CSS, JavaScript, TypeScript, Git, GitHub, Bash, SSH, SQL fundamentals; TypeScript: Generics, Discriminated Unions, Utility Types, Zod

**Phase 02 — React \+ Next.js Core**

**WEEKS 6–9**

React 18, Next.js 15 App Router, Route Groups, Parallel Routes, Vercel deployment

**Phase 03 — UI System & Styling**

**WEEKS 10–12**

Tailwind CSS, shadcn/ui, react-hook-form, Zod, BEM, Style Dictionary

**Phase 04 — State & Data Fetching**

**WEEKS 13–16**

Zustand, TanStack Query v5, tRPC v11, XState v5, React.lazy, Suspense, Intersection Observer

**Phase 05 — Backend \+ Database \+ Auth (Major Phase)**

**WEEKS 17–22**

Node.js/Express, REST APIs, PostgreSQL, Prisma, Supabase, bcrypt, JWT, httpOnly Cookies

**Phase 06 — Testing \+ CI/CD \+ Deployment**

**WEEKS 23–27**

Vitest, React Testing Library, Playwright, Docker, GitHub Actions, Render

**Exit A — Junior Full-Stack**

**TARGET:**

Early-stage startups, dev agencies

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Live URL, green CI badge, Docker deployment

**Phase 07 — API Documentation & Developer Tooling**

**WEEK 28**

OpenAPI 3.0, Swagger UI, Postman, GraphQL, Mermaid

**Exit B — Junior-to-Mid Full-Stack**

**TARGET:**

API-first companies (Postman, Hasura), fintech startups

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Swagger UI link, OpenAPI spec, GraphQL knowledge

**Phase 08 — DSA & Interview Preparation**

**WEEKS 29–32**

Two Pointers, Sliding Window, Binary Search, BFS/DFS, DP, HashMap patterns, Tree Traversal, Heap

**Exit ★1 — Interview-Ready Junior-to-Mid**

**TARGET:**

Series A/B startups, product companies

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Portfolio \+ DSA readiness \+ system design basics

**Phase 09 — Caching \+ Email \+ Payments**

**WEEKS 33–34**

Redis, Upstash, rate limiting, Resend, React Email, Razorpay

**Phase 10 — Monitoring \+ Product Analytics**

**WEEKS 35–39**

pino, Sentry, UptimeRobot, PostHog, feature flags, A/B testing

**Exit C — Mid Full-Stack (Production-Ready)**

**TARGET:**

Fintech, e-commerce, B2B SaaS

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Razorpay integration, Sentry dashboard, Redis caching

**Phase 11 — Real-Time \+ Search \+ PostgreSQL Internals**

**WEEKS 40–43**

WebSockets, SSE, WebRTC, B-Tree/Composite/Partial Indexes, MVCC, WAL, Read Replicas; PostgreSQL Full Text Search, Meilisearch, Typesense

**Exit ★2 — Mid-Level Full-Stack**

**TARGET:**

High-traffic platforms, real-time heavy companies

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

WebSocket feature, search benchmarks, query optimization

**Phase 12 — AI/RAG \+ Production AI Patterns**

**WEEKS 44–47**

OpenAI API, GPT-4o, pgvector, IVFFlat/HNSW, RAG Pattern, BullMQ; Anthropic Claude, prompt caching, tool calling, multi-model routing, cost tracking, evals

**Exit D — AI-Capable Mid-Senior**

**TARGET:**

AI-native startups, LLM-powered products

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

RAG pipeline, multi-model routing, eval framework

**Phase 13 — Advanced Browser APIs \+ Collaboration**

**WEEKS 48–51**

IndexedDB, Web Workers, Broadcast Channel API, Yjs, Hocuspocus, Tiptap

**Phase 14 — Load Testing \+ Security Deep Dive**

**WEEKS 52–54**

k6 load testing, OWASP Top 10, OWASP ZAP, JWT algorithm pinning, trufflehog, Dependabot

**Phase 15 — Build Tooling \+ CSS-in-JS**

**WEEKS 55–56**

Vite, tsup, bundle analyzers, styled-components, Emotion

**Phase 16 — Infrastructure**

**WEEKS 57–58**

AWS S3, Terraform, Kubernetes, kind, kubectl, Helm, HPA

**Phase 17 — Architectural Patterns**

**WEEKS 59–62**

Saga Pattern, Outbox Pattern, Circuit Breaker, CQRS, Multi-Tenancy, Blue-Green Deployments

**Exit 3 — Senior Full-Stack**

**TARGET:**

Late-stage unicorns, FAANG India, high-growth scale-ups

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Kubernetes, Terraform, distributed systems patterns

**Phase 18 — Engineering Craft \+ Package Publishing**

**WEEKS 63–66**

Changesets, ADRs, RFCs, DORA Metrics, Blameless Post-Mortems, technical debt tracking

**Phase 19 — Career & Community**

**WEEKS 67–68**

Wellfound, Instahyre, LinkedIn, Product Hunt, dev.to, Hashnode

**Exit E — Complete Profile**

**TARGET:**

Staff/Principal Engineer, Founding Engineer roles, global remote

**HIGHLIGHTS:**

Published npm package, ADRs, DORA metrics, 16 months of public learning

**STEP 4**

**Build the Portfolio Project**

Build ClientSync — the core full-stack portfolio project referenced throughout the curriculum.

* Build it in public

* Document decisions as ADRs (Architectural Decision Records)

**STEP 5**

**After Completing the Curriculum — Advanced Projects**

10 advanced project ideas are included (pages 30–45), each with:

* A real-world Indian problem to solve (TiffinOS, BatchIQ, TaxStack, etc.)

* Specific features

* ZTD skills exercised (mapped back to phases)

* Monetization strategy

* First 5 users (exact, actionable advice)

### **Decision Matrix — Which Project to Build**

| IF YOU WANT... | BUILD THIS |
| :---- | :---- |
| Highest number of potential users | TiffinOS or BatchIQ |
| Dev audience that converts fast | LLMeter or VaultEnv |
| Most technically interesting | MeetMind or SocietyOS |
| Easiest path to paying customers | OfferLens or TaxStack |
| Best interview story | HireKit |
| Solve a problem you personally faced | OfferLens |

**The Rule: Build the project where you can find 5 real users within 48 hours of launching.**

# **Part II — Why This Works**

*Every roadmap fails the same handful of ways. Here is the specific mechanism already built into this curriculum that prevents each one.*

| COMMON FAILURE MODE | WHAT THIS CURRICULUM DOES ABOUT IT |
| :---- | :---- |
| Silent grinding with no public presence | Build in Public system — a required post/commit/README artifact at the end of every phase, not optional polish. |
| Waiting until the very end to apply for jobs | Exit Points (A, B, ★1, C, ★2, D, 3, E) — real job-readiness milestones seeded from Phase 06 onward, each with its own target roles and salary band. |
| Learning topics before their prerequisites are solid | Dependency-ordered resequencing — explicitly reasoned in-document (e.g. Type Narrowing before Discriminated Unions, HMAC-SHA256 before JWT, curl before Postman) rather than following the original curriculum’s grouping. |
| Burnout from unsustainable pace | Weekly-hours-based timeline options (25–85 hrs/wk) with an explicit daily schedule and "track hours weekly, not daily" rule — a rough day doesn’t derail the plan. |
| A portfolio with no depth — one demo, no real product | ClientSync — a single real, deployable, two-sided SaaS product built feature-by-feature across every phase, rather than 19 disconnected toy projects. |
| Degree/college filtering you out before anyone sees your work | The document explicitly separates companies that gate on degree (FAANG India, large IT services) from those that don’t (most startups, product companies, dev agencies) — so effort is targeted, not wasted. |
| GitHub history that looks fabricated (a dump of commits at the last minute) | Conventional Commits from Phase 04 onward \+ explicit guidance to commit as you go, not in one end-of-phase dump. |

# **Part VI — The 19 Phases**

*Full topic-by-topic detail, in original dependency order. This is the reference layer everything above points to.*

**A SELF-STUDY ENGINEERING CURRICULUM**

**Zero to Elite**

*Phase-wise Topic Index*

**290**  TOPICS   ·   **19**  PHASES   ·   **2700**  HOURS

\~16 MONTHS AT 40 HOURS / WEEK

**OVERVIEW**

**What This Document Is**

This is a 16-month, 290-topic, phase-based software engineering curriculum totaling 2700 hours, designed to take you from a beginner to a job-ready full-stack engineer earning ₹35-50 LPA. It's structured as a self-study roadmap with built-in "Exit Points" — specific milestones where you're ready to start applying for jobs at different salary levels.

## **▌** 

## **▌ Who Is This For?**

| CATEGORY | DETAILS |
| :---- | :---- |
| Primary audience | Aspiring software engineers in India, especially those with a BCA degree (addressed explicitly in the doc) |
| Career stage | Complete beginners to those wanting to level up to senior/staff roles |
| Time commitment | Flexible: 25-70 hrs/week depending on how fast you want to finish |
| Goal | Fastest path to employment at product companies, startups, and scale-ups |
| Companies targeted | Early-stage startups → Series A/B → Unicorns → Global remote (Vercel, Supabase, etc.) |

**KEY NOTE:**

The document explicitly addresses BCA graduates. It says BCA is fine for most target companies (startups, product companies, dev agencies) but becomes a hard filter at FAANG India and large IT services. It suggests MCA as a path if FAANG is a long-term goal.

# 

# **How This Will Get You a Job in a Fierce Market**

*Especially if you're a BCA graduate from a Tier‑2 college with zero projects and no work experience.*

**THE HONEST TRUTH – AND THE PLAN**

The market is tough, but it's not closed. Companies still hire – they hire people who can ship code, not people with fancy degrees. This curriculum is designed to turn you into that person.

Here's how it works for you:

**—** You build a real, deployable project (ClientSync) in the first 6 months — this becomes your portfolio, your proof of skill, and your conversation starter in interviews.

**—** You publish your work publicly — every phase gives you something to show on GitHub, dev.to, or LinkedIn. Recruiters see your progress, not your college name.

**—** You don't wait until the end to apply — the 'Exit Points' let you start applying as early as Phase 06, when you have a live app and a CI/CD pipeline. That's often enough for early-stage startups.

**—** The curriculum explicitly addresses the BCA degree filter — it tells you which companies don't care about your degree (most startups, product companies, dev agencies) and which do (FAANG, large IT services). You focus your energy where it matters.

**—** You learn exactly what's needed for the job — no fluff, no outdated theory. Every topic is chosen because it's used in production at top startups. You're job-ready, not just 'certified'.

**—** You build in public — by the time you finish, you have a network, a trail of content, and often inbound interest from founders. That's how you beat the 'no experience' trap.

**THE BOTTOM LINE**

Your BCA degree is not a blocker – it's a starting point. What gets you hired is your ability to build and ship. This roadmap gives you the exact path to do that, with clear milestones to keep you on track.

If you follow it consistently, you will have a portfolio, a GitHub history, and the confidence to walk into any interview. The jobs are there – you just need to be visible and credible. This document shows you how.

## **▌ How to Build in Public**

"Build in public" gets said so often it's stopped meaning anything. Here's what it actually means for this roadmap: at the end of most phases (not every day, not every commit), you post one specific, concrete thing you built or learned — not a status update, not "day 47 of my coding journey." Each phase in this document has a compact BUILD IN PUBLIC callout telling you exactly what to post, what to commit, and where. This section is the reasoning behind that system.

**WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN YOUR DEGREE**

A recruiter or hiring manager cannot verify "I know React." They can verify a GitHub repo with real commit history, a deployed URL, a green CI badge, and a dev.to post that explains a real decision you made. Visibility isn't vanity here — it's the only evidence a stranger can check in 90 seconds. Silence is the default; everyone in this roadmap is building the same 19 phases. The difference between two people with identical skills is which one has proof.

**GITHUB**

* Commit as you go, not in one dump at the end of a phase. A commit history with 3 commits on the last day of every phase reads as fabricated. It probably is.

* Use Conventional Commits from Phase 04 onward (feat:, fix:, refactor:, etc.) — it's a topic in the curriculum for a reason; a recruiter who opens your commit log should see discipline, not noise.

* Every repo gets a real README: what it does, how to run it, what you learned. "TODO: add README" sitting untouched for months is a worse signal than no README at all.

* Pin your 2–3 best repos on your GitHub profile. Don't make someone dig through 40 repos to find the one that matters.

**LINKEDIN**

* Post when you ship something visible — a deployed feature, a working demo, a hard bug you fixed. Not "studying TypeScript today," which nobody can evaluate.

* Lead with what it does or looks like (a screenshot, a short screen-recording), not with the tech stack. Recruiters skim; a visual gets 2 seconds of attention, a paragraph of jargon gets zero.

* One post per phase is enough. A LinkedIn feed with 40 low-effort posts is worse than one with 15 real ones — frequency without substance reads as noise, not progress.

**DEV.TO / HASHNODE**

* Write about a specific decision or a specific bug, never a tutorial recap of something already covered better elsewhere ("What is React?" gets you nothing — 500,000 of those already exist).

* The BUILD IN PUBLIC callouts flag which phases are worth a dev.to post and which aren't (Phase 08's DSA grind and Phase 15's build tooling aren't — skip posting, keep building).

* A post that says "here's a mistake I made and how I fixed it" outperforms one that says "here's how to do X correctly" — the former is credible because it's specific and honest; the latter reads as copied.

*The rule underneath all of it: post less than you think you should, and make sure what you do post could survive someone actually clicking through. A thin GitHub history with real commits beats a loud one with none.*

## **▌ Summary: Quick Start Checklist**

**01**   Decide your weekly hours (30 hrs/week recommended for full-time job)

**02**   Set up Phase 01 dev environment (VS Code, nvm, Git, SSH)

**03**   Start Phase 02 → Build Next.js app, deploy to Vercel

**04**   Continue phases sequentially

**05**   At Exit A (Phase 06\\) → Start applying to startups

**06**   At Exit ★1 (Phase 08\\) → Pass DSA rounds, get SDE-1 roles

**07**   After Phase 12 → Build an AI project

**08**   After Phase 17 → Senior-level roles

**09**   After Phase 19 → Complete profile, publish npm package

**10**   Choose an advanced project from the 10 listed

**CRITICAL ADVICE FROM THE DOCUMENT:**

"The gap between Exit 3 and Exit E is not technical — it's visibility and credibility. If you've been posting on dev.to, building in public, and engaging with the community throughout the 16 months, Exit E is a natural inbound moment. If you've done the curriculum silently with no public presence, the salary range is the same but you'll have to work harder." Build in public. Document your decisions. Ship consistently. Apply early (Exit A/B/1), not just at the end.

### **Decision Matrix — Which Project to Build**

| IF YOU WANT... | BUILD THIS |
| :---- | :---- |
| Highest number of potential users | TiffinOS or BatchIQ |
| Dev audience that converts fast | LLMeter or VaultEnv |
| Most technically interesting | MeetMind or SocietyOS |
| Easiest path to paying customers | OfferLens or TaxStack |
| Best interview story | HireKit |
| Solve a problem you personally faced | OfferLens |

**The Rule: Build the project where you can find 5 real users within 48 hours of launching.**

# **2,700-Hour Plan — Full Distribution**

*2,700 hours treated as a fixed budget and distributed across every layer of the plan: learning all 290 topics, drilling problems, building standalone mini-projects, building ClientSync feature-by-feature, and building the 10 advanced projects. Every number below adds up to exactly 2,700 — nothing floats free.*

## **▌ Methodology**

This is not a fresh independent estimate at 2,700 — it's the validated relative structure from the topic-by-topic hour validation (learn 1,364 / problems 346 / mini-projects 257 \\= 1,967), plus ClientSync's phase-by-phase build cost (268 hrs, reasoned) and the 10 advanced projects (308 hrs, reasoned), scaled up proportionally to hit exactly 2,700 and rounded with a largest-remainder method so the distribution stays clean — no topic absorbs a random leftover chunk just to make the total match.

**FIVE LAYERS, EVERY TOPIC (WHERE APPLICABLE)**

* **Learn —** concept understood, enough hands-on practice to use it correctly.

* **Problems —** focused drilling on that topic alone. Zero for topics where this doesn't apply (config, accounts, docs, career/community).

* **Mini-project —** a small standalone build proving you can use it. Zero where a project doesn't make sense on its own (e.g. Prettier, SSH Keys, LinkedIn).

* **ClientSync —** folding that phase's skills into the real product. Priced per phase, not per topic — features don't map 1:1 to topics.

* **10 Advanced Projects —** priced individually (see the project-hours table earlier in this document).

*If you want any single layer re-derived independently rather than scaled from the validated baseline, say which one — that's real additional work, not a settings toggle.*

## **▌ Headline Numbers**

| LAYER | HOURS | % OF TOTAL | NOTES |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| Learn (290 topics) | 1454 | 54% | validated, topic-by-topic |
| Problems (290 topics) | 363 | 13% | validated, topic-by-topic |
| Mini-projects (290 topics) | 269 | 10% | validated, topic-by-topic |
| ClientSync build | 286 | 11% | reasoned, per-phase |
| 10 Advanced Projects | 328 | 12% | reasoned, per-project |
| **TOTAL** | **2700** | **100%** |  |

*Note: 290 vs. the roadmap's own claimed 271 — same discrepancy flagged in the original validation, still unresolved in the source document.*

## **▌ Daily Schedule — 85 hrs/wk Reference**

*6 focused days \+ 1 light day. Structured so it survives real fatigue, not just a number on paper.*

| TIME | BLOCK | WHAT HAPPENS |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 06:00 – 06:30 | Wake-up | No phone, water, light movement. Starting foggy costs the whole first block. |
| 06:30 – 10:30 | Block 1 (4 hrs) | Hardest thing of the day — new concept, DSA, or heavy backend/infra topic. Peak-brain window. |
| 10:30 – 11:00 | Break | Away from the screen. Not “5 more minutes.” |
| 11:00 – 13:00 | Problems (2 hrs) | Drill what you just learned, while it's fresh. |
| 13:00 – 14:00 | Lunch \+ rest | Away from the desk. |
| 14:00 – 18:00 | Block 2 (4 hrs) | Building — mini-project or ClientSync feature work. Mechanical, good fit for the post-lunch dip. |
| 18:00 – 18:30 | Break | Outside if possible. |
| 18:30 – 20:30 | Block 3 (2 hrs) | Light work: notes, docs, commits, review, tomorrow's plan. Survives a bad day. |
| 20:30 – 21:00 | Buffer | Overflow from earlier, or genuinely done. |
| 21:00 onward | Off | Real off — not tutorials in bed. |

7th day: \~7 hrs, lighter — catch-up, writing, no new hard concepts. This is the slack valve.

**RULES THAT MAKE IT SURVIVE MONTHS, NOT WEEKS**

* If a block isn't landing, stop it — don't force hour 4 of Block 1 if nothing's absorbing. Swap to Block 2\.

* Track hours weekly, not daily. One rough day doesn't matter; a rough month does.

* First sign of declining output, dread, or worse sleep — that's an early 7-hr day, not a discipline failure.

*Weeks and month labels in every phase below assume a 40 hrs/week pace. Hours per phase are fixed; if you're working at a different weekly pace, divide each phase's hour total by your own weekly hours to get your personal week count (see Step 1 for common paces).*

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 01 & 01b — Reordered by Dependency, Not by Original Curriculum Grouping*

Every topic from Phase 01 (Developer Environment & Foundations) and Phase 01b (TypeScript Mastery), resequenced so nothing is introduced before its prerequisite. Original phase groupings ignored — this is pure dependency order.

# **STAGE 1 — Your Workspace (before writing a single line of real code)**

*You cannot learn to code without a place to write it, run it, and save it. This stage exists so every topic after it has a working environment underneath it.*

## **▌ 1\. VS Code**

**Interface Basics**

* Command Palette  

* File Explorer, Search, Source Control, Extensions panels  

* Integrated terminal

**Editing Features**

* Multi-cursor editing  

* IntelliSense and autocomplete  

* Code snippets  

* Go to definition / Find references

**Configuration**

* settings.json (user vs workspace settings)  

* keybindings.json  

* Extensions (ESLint, Prettier, GitLens, etc.)

**Debugging in VS Code**

* launch.json configuration  

* Breakpoints, watch panel, call stack view

**Productivity Features**

* Workspaces  

* Integrated Git support  

* Task automation (tasks.json)

## **▌ 2\. Bash**

*You need the terminal before nvm, npm, Git, or SSH make any sense — all of them are run from here.*

**Shell Basics**

* Navigating the filesystem (cd, ls, pwd)  

* File operations (cp, mv, rm, mkdir, touch)  

* Viewing files (cat, less, head, tail)  

* Permissions (chmod, chown, ls \-l interpretation)

**Text Processing**

* grep  

* sed (basic substitution)  

* awk (basic field processing)  

* Pipes (|) and redirection (\\\>, \\\>\\\>, \\\<)

**Scripting**

* Shebang line (\#\!/bin/bash)  

* Variables in bash  

* Conditionals (if/then/else)  

* Loops (for, while)  

* Functions in bash scripts  

* Exit codes and $?

**Process Management**

* ps, top  

* kill, background jobs (&, jobs, fg, bg)

**Environment**

* Environment variables (export, echo $VAR)  

* .bashrc / .bash\\\_profile  

* PATH variable

## **▌ 3\. nvm \+ npm**

*You need Node/npm installed via the terminal before you can run any JavaScript file outside a browser, or install any package (including tools you'll use later like ESLint, Prettier, Zod).*

**nvm (Node Version Manager)**

* Installing nvm  

* nvm install / nvm use  

* Managing multiple Node versions  

* .nvmrc files

**npm Basics**

* npm init  

* Installing packages (dependencies vs devDependencies)  

* npm install, npm uninstall, npm update  

* package.json anatomy  

* package-lock.json purpose  

* Semantic versioning (^, \~, exact versions)  

* npm scripts (custom run scripts)  

* Global vs local installs  

* npx usage

## **▌ 4\. SSH Keys**

*Needed now so you can authenticate to GitHub before Topic 5 (Git \+ GitHub) requires pushing code remotely.*

**Concepts**

* Public/private key cryptography basics  

* Symmetric vs asymmetric encryption (conceptual)

**Generating & Using Keys**

* ssh-keygen  

* Key types (RSA, Ed25519)  

* Adding keys to ssh-agent  

* Copying public key to GitHub/servers (ssh-copy-id, manual paste)

**SSH Usage**

* ssh user@host  

* SSH config file (\~/.ssh/config)  

* Connecting to remote servers  

* Using SSH keys with GitHub for authentication

## **▌ 5\. Git \+ GitHub (Foundations Only — local \+ remote basics)**

*You need enough Git now to save your work as you learn HTML/CSS/JS. Deeper collaboration workflows (rebase, PRs, conflict resolution at scale) are deliberately deferred to Stage 7, once you have real multi-file projects worth versioning properly.*

**Git Fundamentals**

* What is version control  

* git init, git status, git log  

* Working directory, staging area, repository (three trees)  

* git add, git commit  

* .gitignore

**Remote Repositories (basic push/pull only)**

* git remote add/remove  

* git push, git pull, git fetch  

* Creating repositories on GitHub  

* Cloning a repository

*(Branching, merging, rebasing, PRs, undoing changes, and collaboration patterns resume in Stage 7.)*

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Your Workspace  (16 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| VS Code | 3 |
| Bash | 5 |
| nvm \+ npm | 2 |
| SSH Keys | 2 |
| Git \+ GitHub (Foundations) | 4 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Dotfiles Time Capsule" (Easy)**

Build a personal dotfiles repository that configures your entire terminal \+ editor environment from scratch on a fresh machine. Includes a bootstrap.sh script that installs nvm, sets Node version, configures VS Code settings.json, keybindings, and a curated extension list via "code \--install-extension", and sets up SSH key generation with a checklist prompt. Domain: developer tooling / DevOps. Why it’s real: every professional engineer eventually builds this, and you’ll actually reuse it.

**"New Machine, Ten Minutes" Challenge (Medium)**

Extend the dotfiles project into a single idempotent shell script that provisions a brand-new Ubuntu/macOS machine for full-stack development in under 10 minutes — installs Git, nvm, correct Node LTS, global CLI tools (pnpm, gh CLI), configures a global .gitignore, sets up commit signing with SSH keys, and verifies everything with a self-test script that prints pass/fail per component. Why it’s real: this is what onboarding scripts at real companies look like.

**"Team Onboarding Kit" (Hard)**

Package the above into a shareable CLI tool ("npx create-dev-environment") that asks interactive questions (shell preference, editor, GitHub username) and generates a personalized setup repo plus a rendered onboarding README with screenshots. Publish it as an npm package others could install. Why it’s real: this is a genuine open-source-worthy tool — portfolio-impressive because it is meta (a tool that builds tools).

**Practice Exercises**

* Configure SSH keys for two different GitHub accounts (personal \+ "work") on one machine using SSH config Host aliases.  

* Write a Bash function "mkcd" that creates a directory and cd’s into it; add it to your shell profile.  

* Use nvm to install 3 different Node versions and write a script that prints which projects (via .nvmrc) need which version.  

* Diagnose and fix a broken PATH variable (intentionally corrupt yours, then repair it).  

* Write a .gitignore from scratch for a Node+Python monorepo without copying a template.  

* Create a Git alias ("git lg") for a readable one-line log with graph view.  

* Time yourself resolving a merge conflict created by two branches editing the same line — do it via VS Code’s merge editor and via raw CLI markers.

# **STAGE 2 — Structure: HTML**

*HTML comes before CSS because you cannot style what doesn't exist yet. HTML comes before JavaScript because you cannot manipulate a DOM that hasn't been defined.*

## **▌ 6\. HTML5**

**Document Structure**

* DOCTYPE declaration  

* html, head, body elements  

* Meta tags (charset, viewport, description)  

* Title element  

* Comments

**Text Content Elements**

* Headings (h1–h6) and hierarchy  

* Paragraphs  

* Lists (ul, ol, li, dl, dt, dd)  

* Inline elements (span, strong, em, small, sub, sup, abbr, cite)  

* Line breaks and horizontal rules

**Semantic Elements**

* header, footer, nav, main, article, section, aside  

* figure, figcaption  

* time, mark, details, summary  

* Why semantics matter (accessibility, SEO)

**Links & Navigation**

* Anchor tag and href  

* Relative vs absolute URLs  

* target attribute  

* rel attribute (noopener, noreferrer, nofollow)  

* Fragment identifiers

**Images & Media**

* img tag, src, alt, loading="lazy"  

* picture element and source  

* srcset and sizes (responsive images)  

* audio and video elements  

* track element for captions  

* iframe

**Tables**

* table, thead, tbody, tfoot  

* tr, th, td  

* colspan, rowspan  

* caption

**Forms**

* form element, action, method  

* Input types (text, email, password, number, date, checkbox, radio, file, etc.)  

* label and for attribute  

* select, option, optgroup  

* textarea  

* fieldset and legend  

* button types (submit, reset, button)  

* Form attributes (required, placeholder, pattern, min, max, autocomplete)  

* Native form validation

**HTML5 APIs & Attributes**

* data-\\\* attributes  

* contenteditable  

* draggable  

* hidden attribute  

* tabindex

**Accessibility (a11y) Basics**

* ARIA roles and attributes  

* Alt text best practices  

* Keyboard navigability  

* Landmark roles

**Document Metadata & Performance**

* link tag (stylesheet, preload, prefetch, icon)  

* script tag (async, defer, type=module)  

* Character encoding  

* Viewport meta for responsive design

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Structure: HTML  (10 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| HTML5 | 10 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Recipe Card, Zero CSS" (Easy)**

Build a single recipe page using only semantic HTML5 (article, section, figure, time, details) — no CSS at all. It must still be readable, accessible, and make logical sense structurally. Domain: food/lifestyle.

**"Accessible Government Notice Board" (Medium)**

Recreate a public notice/announcements page (like a city council site) with correct heading hierarchy, ARIA landmarks, a data table of meeting minutes using proper caption/th-scope, and a downloadable-document list using semantically correct link text (no "click here"). Must pass a Lighthouse accessibility audit at 100\. Domain: civic tech.

**"Structured Résumé That’s Also a Sitemap" (Hard)**

Build a résumé page using HTML microdata / Schema.org (itemscope, itemprop for Person, JobPosting history) so it’s machine-readable by search engines and could populate a rich search result. Validate with Google’s Rich Results Test. Domain: personal branding / SEO engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Mark up a 5-item nested navigation menu using only correct list/landmark semantics.  

* Take a "div soup" page and refactor every div into the correct semantic element.  

* Build an HTML-only (no CSS) multi-step form using fieldset/legend for grouping.  

* Validate 3 real-world websites’ HTML using the W3C validator and log every error found.  

* Write an HTML email template’s structure (tables-based) and understand why email HTML differs from web HTML.

# **STAGE 3 — Presentation: CSS**

*CSS depends entirely on HTML existing first. Ordered internally from syntax → box model → layout → responsiveness → visual polish, since each builds on the one before.*

* line-height, letter-spacing, word-spacing  

* text-align, text-decoration, text-transform  

* @font-face and web fonts

**Layout — Flexbox**

**Layout — Grid**

* grid-template-columns / rows  

* grid-gap / gap

* Implicit vs explicit grid  

* minmax(), repeat(), auto-fill, auto-fit

**Responsive Design**

* Media queries (min-width, max-width, orientation)

**Overflow & Visibility**

* overflow (visible, hidden, scroll, auto)

**CSS Architecture Basics**

* Specificity management strategy

* Reusability considerations

**Print & Miscellaneous**

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Presentation: CSS  (18 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| CSS3 | 18 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Print-Perfect Invoice" (Easy)**

Design an invoice that looks correct both on-screen and when printed (@media print), with page-break control, so a small business could literally print/PDF it for clients. Domain: business/finance tooling.

**"CSS-Only Data Dashboard Skeleton" (Medium)**

Build a responsive analytics dashboard layout (sidebar, KPI cards, chart placeholders, table) using CSS Grid \+ Flexbox combined, fully responsive from 320px to 4K, with no JavaScript — including a CSS-only collapsible sidebar using the checkbox hack or :has(). Domain: SaaS/analytics.

**"Theatre Seat Map" (Hard)**

Build a fully CSS-driven interactive seat-selection map (like a cinema/concert booking UI) — hover states, sold/available/selected visual states, using CSS Grid for the seat layout and pure CSS (:checked, sibling selectors) for selection logic, no JS. Domain: ticketing/events. Why it’s real: real booking sites need exactly this pattern; doing it CSS-only proves deep selector mastery.

**Practice Exercises**

* Recreate a well-known website’s hero section pixel-for-pixel using only CSS.  

* Build a responsive 12-column grid system from scratch without a framework.  

* Debug 5 intentionally broken CSS specificity conflicts and explain the cascade resolution for each.  

* Center a div 5 different ways and explain the tradeoffs of each.  

* Build a CSS-only tooltip system using ::before/::after and attr().  

* Create a print stylesheet for a blog article that hides nav/ads/comments.

# **STAGE 4 — Behavior: JavaScript (Full Sequence, dependency-ordered)**

*JavaScript comes after HTML/CSS because its most motivating use case (manipulating the page) needs a page to exist. Ordered in true dependency order — language core → functions → scope/execution → closures/this/memory → data structures → strings/numbers/dates → error handling → regex → OOP → iteration → meta-programming → modules → async → HTTP → DOM/browser → performance/security → functional patterns → architecture → algorithms → tooling/testing/debugging → engine internals/spec. Topics that could otherwise appear in more than one place (e.g. spread/rest appearing under both Functions and Advanced Operators) are merged at first occurrence.*

## **▌ 8\. JavaScript — Language Core**

**8.1 — Fundamentals**

* What is JavaScript / JS Engine / JS Runtime / How JavaScript Runs  

* Variables: var, let, const and their differences  

* Data types: Number, String, Boolean, Null, Undefined, Symbol, BigInt  

* Dynamic typing, type conversion, type coercion  

* Operators: arithmetic, assignment, comparison, logical, bitwise, ternary  

* Nullish coalescing (??) and optional chaining (?.)  

* Comments, Strict Mode (and strict vs sloppy mode details)  

* eval — what it is and why to avoid it

**8.2 — Control Flow**

* if / else / else if / switch  

* for, while, do-while loops  

* break, continue, labels

**8.3 — Functions**

* Function declarations vs expressions vs anonymous functions  

* Arrow functions  

* Parameters: default, rest, spread operator  

* Return values  

* Higher-order functions and callback functions  

* IIFE (Immediately Invoked Function Expressions)  

* Recursive and pure functions

**8.4 — Scope**

*Requires functions from 8.3 to make function scope concrete.*

* Global, function, block, and lexical scope  

* Scope chain

**8.5 — Execution Context**

*Requires scope from 8.4 — execution context is what scope chains live inside.*

* Global and function execution context  

* Creation phase and execution phase  

* Variable environment and lexical environment

**8.6 — Hoisting**

*Direct consequence of the creation phase in 8.5.*

* Variable hoisting  

* Function hoisting  

* Temporal Dead Zone

**8.7 — Closures**

*Requires scope (8.4) and execution context (8.5) — a closure is a function retaining its lexical environment.*

* Closure concept and lexical environment  

* Practical uses (data privacy, memoization, currying)  

* Closures in depth

**8.8 — this Keyword**

*Requires functions (8.3) — this is a per-call binding on function invocation.*

* Global this, object this, function this, arrow-function this  

* call(), apply(), bind()  

* Function binding internals

**8.9 — Memory Management**

*Requires closures (8.7) — closures are the main reason to understand what stays referenced in memory.*

* Stack memory vs heap memory  

* Primitive vs reference types  

* Garbage collection

## **▌ 9\. Core Data Structures**

**9.1 — Objects**

* Creating objects, object literals, nested objects  

* Property access: dot notation, bracket notation  

* Methods  

* Object.keys() / values() / entries() / assign() / freeze() / seal()  

* Object destructuring  

* Object.getPrototypeOf / setPrototypeOf  

* Object.hasOwn, Object.fromEntries  

* Object.getOwnPropertyNames / getOwnPropertySymbols / getOwnPropertyDescriptors  

* Object.defineProperty / defineProperties

**9.2 — Arrays**

* Creating arrays  

* Mutating methods: push, pop, shift, unshift, splice, sort, reverse, fill  

* Non-mutating methods: slice, concat, join, flat, flatMap  

* Higher-order methods: map, filter, reduce, forEach, find, findIndex, some, every, includes

**9.3 — Strings**

* String methods, template literals, escape characters  

* String search, replace, split, trim, padding

**9.4 — Numbers & Math**

* Number object, Math object  

* Random numbers, rounding, precision, parsing numbers

**9.5 — Dates**

* Date object, current date  

* Formatting dates, timestamps, time calculations

**9.6 — Advanced Operators & Destructuring (consolidated)**

*Spread/rest/destructuring were introduced narrowly in 8.3 and 9.1; this section is the full generalized treatment across arrays, objects, and function calls.*

* Spread operator across arrays/objects/function calls  

* Rest operator  

* Full destructuring patterns (nested, default values, swapping)  

* Optional chaining and nullish coalescing recap in destructuring contexts

## **▌ 10\. Error Handling**

*Requires functions (8.3) and objects (9.1) — custom errors are classes/objects, thrown from function call stacks.*

* try / catch / finally / throw  

* Error object, custom errors, error subclasses  

* Stack traces  

* Error propagation  

* Global error handlers

## **▌ 11\. Regular Expressions**

*Requires strings (9.3) — regex operates on string matching.*

* Regex syntax, character classes, quantifiers, groups, flags  

* String matching (test/match/replace)  

* Lookahead and lookbehind  

* Named capture groups  

* Unicode regex  

* Backreferences

## **▌ 12\. Prototypes & Inheritance**

*Requires objects (9.1) and this (8.8) — prototypes are how objects share behavior via this-bound method lookup.*

* Prototype object and prototype chain  

* Object.create()  

* Constructor functions and the new keyword  

* Instance creation  

* Prototype internals

## **▌ 13\. Classes**

*Classes are sugar over the prototype system in 12 — cannot be understood without it.*

* Class syntax, constructor, methods  

* Getters and setters  

* Static methods  

* Inheritance and super

## **▌ 14\. Iterators & Generators**

*Requires functions (8.3) — generators are a special function type.*

* Iterable protocol, iterator protocol  

* Generator functions and yield  

* Tail calls (concept)

## **▌ 15\. Symbols**

*Symbol was introduced as a primitive type in 8.1; this goes deeper now that objects (9.1) exist as a context for unique keys.*

* Symbol primitive  

* Unique keys  

* Global symbol registry

## **▌ 16\. Collections**

*Requires objects (9.1) for Map/WeakMap semantics to make sense by comparison.*

* Set, WeakSet  

* Map, WeakMap

## **▌ 17\. Property Descriptors & Object Internals**

*Requires Object.defineProperty from 9.1 — this section goes underneath it into descriptor mechanics.*

* Property descriptors: writable, enumerable, configurable  

* Getters and setters (descriptor-based)  

* Internal slots and internal methods (conceptual)  

* Property attributes  

* Equality algorithms (==, \\===, Object.is, SameValueZero)  

* Primitive wrapper objects

## **▌ 18\. Reflect API**

*Requires prototypes (12) and property descriptors (17) — Reflect operates on both.*

* Reflect.get()  

* Reflect.set()  

* Reflect.apply()  

* Reflect.construct()

## **▌ 19\. Proxy**

*Requires Reflect (18) — Proxy traps are typically implemented using Reflect internally.*

* Proxy object and traps  

* Validation  

* Reactivity  

* Intercepting operations

## **▌ 20\. Meta Programming**

*Synthesis of Symbols (15), Proxy (19), and Reflect (18) — placed last among these since it combines all three.*

* Symbols, Proxy, Reflect in combination  

* Decorator proposal (concept)

## **▌ 21\. Modules**

*Independent of the object/class system — placed here because real multi-file code (which motivates modules) assumes classes/objects already exist.*

* import / export, named and default exports  

* Module scope  

* ES Modules vs CommonJS vs UMD vs AMD  

* Dynamic import  

* Import maps

## **▌ 22\. Event Loop**

*Requires functions, closures, and execution context (8.3–8.7) — the call stack is the execution-context stack in motion.*

* Call stack, Web APIs, callback queue  

* Microtask queue, macrotask queue  

* Event loop mechanics, job queue

## **▌ 23\. Asynchronous JavaScript**

*Requires the event loop (22) — promises and async/await are built on it.*

* Synchronous vs asynchronous execution  

* Callbacks and callback hell  

* Promises: states, chaining, methods (all, race, any, allSettled)  

* async/await  

* Fetch API basics, AbortController

## **▌ 24\. Advanced Promises**

*Requires 23 — this is the internal specification-level view of what was used practically above.*

* Promise internals and resolution procedure  

* Thenables  

* Promise executor  

* Error propagation in promise chains  

* Promise specification concepts

## **▌ 25\. Advanced Async**

*Requires generators (14) and async/await (23) — async iterators combine both.*

* Async iterators, for await...of  

* Async generators  

* Streams, AbortSignal

## **▌ 26\. HTTP & APIs**

*Conceptual grounding needed before Fetch (23) is used meaningfully in real requests.*

* HTTP methods, REST APIs, JSON  

* Headers, request body, response, status codes  

* Authentication basics  

* CRUD operations

## **▌ 27\. DOM**

*Now that HTML exists (Stage 2\\) and async/fetch exist (23/26), DOM manipulation has real purpose and real data to render.*

* DOM tree, BOM  

* Selecting, creating, removing, updating elements  

* innerHTML, textContent, innerText  

* Attributes, dataset, classList  

* Styling with JS, DOM traversal

## **▌ 28\. Events**

*Requires DOM (27) — events fire on DOM nodes.*

* Event listeners, event object  

* Mouse, keyboard, form, clipboard, focus, window events  

* Event bubbling, capturing, delegation  

* preventDefault(), stopPropagation()

## **▌ 29\. Forms (JS side)**

*Requires HTML forms (Stage 2\\) and events (28).*

* Form validation, input/submit/reset events  

* Constraint Validation API

## **▌ 30\. Browser Storage**

* localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies (incl. secure cookies)  

* JSON serialization  

* IndexedDB, Cache Storage, StorageManager

## **▌ 31\. Timing Functions**

* setTimeout / clearTimeout  

* setInterval / clearInterval  

* requestAnimationFrame, requestIdleCallback

## **▌ 32\. Browser APIs**

*Broad grab-bag of Web Platform APIs — placed after DOM/events/storage since most either manipulate the DOM or extend the storage/network model already covered.*

* Navigator, Geolocation, History, Clipboard, Drag and Drop, Fullscreen, Notifications, Speech, Web Share APIs  

* Fetch, XMLHttpRequest, URL API, URLSearchParams  

* File API, Blob, FileReader  

* Canvas, SVG, Web Audio, WebRTC, WebSocket, Server-Sent Events  

* Permissions, Screen Orientation, Battery API (concept), Vibration API  

* BroadcastChannel, Web Locks, Idle Detection (concept), Wake Lock API, Credential Management API

## **▌ 33\. Advanced Browser APIs (Observers & Performance)**

*Requires DOM (27) — these observe DOM/render state changes.*

* Intersection Observer, Mutation Observer, Resize Observer  

* Performance API, PerformanceObserver  

* Web Animations API

## **▌ 34\. Networking (Realtime)**

*WebSockets/SSE were introduced under Browser APIs (32); this is the dedicated deep dive.*

* WebSockets deep dive  

* Server-Sent Events (SSE) deep dive  

* Streams API

## **▌ 35\. Binary Data**

*Requires Blob/File API from 32 as a starting point.*

* ArrayBuffer, SharedArrayBuffer  

* Typed Arrays, DataView  

* Blob, File API deep dive

## **▌ 36\. Workers**

*Requires the event loop (22) and messaging concepts from Browser APIs (32) — workers run parallel event loops.*

* Web Workers, Shared Workers  

* Service Workers  

* Background Sync  

* Worklets

## **▌ 37\. Internationalization**

* Intl.NumberFormat, Intl.DateTimeFormat  

* Intl.Collator, Intl.Locale  

* Intl.RelativeTimeFormat, Intl.PluralRules

## **▌ 38\. Security**

*Requires DOM (27), forms (29), and HTTP (26) — most web security issues are attacks on those surfaces.*

* XSS, CSRF, CORS, Same-Origin Policy  

* Content Security Policy  

* Sanitization, DOM sanitization, Trusted Types (concept)

## **▌ 39\. Browser Rendering & Performance**

*Requires DOM (27) and CSS (Stage 3\\) — this is what happens after both exist together on a page.*

* Critical rendering path  

* Reflow, repaint, layout thrashing  

* Rendering pipeline: layout, paint, composite

## **▌ 40\. Frontend Performance Techniques**

*Requires functions/closures (8.3/8.7) for debouncing/throttling/memoization, and modules (21) for code splitting.*

* Debouncing, throttling, memoization  

* Lazy loading, code splitting, tree shaking

## **▌ 41\. Memory Leaks**

*Requires memory management (8.9), closures (8.7), DOM (27), and events (28) — leaks arise from all four interacting.*

* Detached DOM nodes  

* Closures causing leaks  

* Timers and event listeners as leak sources  

* Garbage collection pitfalls  

* WeakRef, FinalizationRegistry

## **▌ 42\. Functional Programming**

*Requires functions (8.3) and closures (8.7) — currying and composition are closure-based patterns.*

* Pure functions, immutability  

* Composition, currying, partial application

## **▌ 43\. Advanced Functional Programming**

*Builds directly on 42\.*

* Monads (basic understanding), functors  

* Pipelines, function composition, point-free style

## **▌ 44\. Design Patterns**

*Requires classes (13), closures (8.7), and modules (21) — most classic JS patterns are implemented using one of these.*

* Module, Factory, Singleton, Observer, Pub/Sub patterns  

* MVC basics, Strategy, Adapter, Decorator, Facade, Command, Mediator  

* Dependency Injection (concept)

## **▌ 45\. JavaScript Architecture**

*Requires design patterns (44) — architecture is patterns applied at application scale.*

* State management, component-based thinking  

* Separation of concerns: MVC, MVVM, MVP  

* Flux architecture, event-driven architecture

## **▌ 46\. Algorithms & Problem Solving in JavaScript**

*Requires arrays (9.2), objects (9.1), and recursion (8.3) as building blocks — placed after core language, independent of async/DOM.*

* Time complexity (Big O), space complexity  

* Searching, sorting, recursion  

* Two pointers, sliding window  

* Hash maps, stacks, queues, trees, graphs  

* Dynamic programming (basics)

## **▌ 47\. Advanced Algorithms**

*Builds directly on 46\.*

* Trie, heap, segment tree, Fenwick tree, union find  

* Topological sort, BFS, DFS  

* Backtracking, greedy algorithms

## **▌ 48\. Testing**

*Requires functions (8.3), async (23), and DOM (27) — there needs to be real code with those shapes to test.*

* Unit testing concepts, assertions, mocking  

* Integration testing, end-to-end testing basics  

* Test runners

## **▌ 49\. Debugging**

* Chrome DevTools, breakpoints, watch expressions  

* Performance profiling, memory profiling  

* Network debugging, source maps

## **▌ 50\. Package Ecosystem & Tooling**

*Revisits/reinforces nvm+npm from Stage 1, now applied to JS specifically, and introduces the build tools that assume real multi-file JS projects exist.*

* npm, package.json, package-lock.json, semantic versioning, npm scripts  

* Babel, Vite, Webpack, Rollup, esbuild  

* ESLint, Prettier (introduced here; full deep dive in Stage 7\\)

## **▌ 51\. JavaScript Best Practices**

* Clean code, SOLID (as applicable to JS)  

* DRY, KISS, YAGNI  

* Code organization

## **▌ 52\. JavaScript Engine Internals**

*Placed near the end deliberately — internals are far more meaningful once you've written enough real code (functions, objects, async, DOM) to have intuitions worth correcting.*

* Parsing, AST (Abstract Syntax Tree)  

* Bytecode, JIT compilation  

* Hidden classes, inline caching, deoptimization

## **▌ 53\. JavaScript Specifications**

*Last in the JS stage — reading the spec and understanding TC39 process is most useful once you already know what the language does in practice.*

* Reading the ECMAScript spec  

* TC39 proposal stages and process  

* Language evolution

## **▌ 54\. Modern JavaScript Features (ES6+) — Consolidation Checkpoint**

*Not new material — this is a deliberate recap checkpoint pulling together let/const, arrow functions, template literals, destructuring, modules, classes, promises, async/await, optional chaining, nullish coalescing, BigInt, and dynamic import, all of which were taught individually above. Placed last in the JS stage as a consolidation pass before moving to TypeScript.*

*This is the largest stage in the roadmap (46 topics). Projects are grouped into progressive milestone clusters spanning multiple related topics rather than one disconnected toy project per topic.*

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**Cluster A — "Command-Line Expense Tracker" (Easy)**

A Node.js CLI tool that tracks expenses in a local JSON file, using Maps/Sets for category dedup, robust try/catch with custom Error subclasses, and regex-based input validation. Domain: personal finance. Covers: language core, data structures, error handling, regex.

**Cluster A — "Text Adventure Engine" (Easy–Medium)**

A choose-your-own-adventure engine driven by a JSON story graph, using classes for Room/Player/Item, prototypal inheritance for enemy types, and regex command parsing ("go north", "take sword"). Domain: games/interactive fiction.

**Cluster A — "Log File Forensics Tool" (Medium)**

A Node CLI that ingests raw server log files, uses regex to extract structured events, aggregates with Maps, and flags anomalies using custom error classes for malformed lines. Domain: SRE/observability.

**Cluster B — "Custom ORM-lite Query Builder" (Medium–Hard)**

A mini fluent query builder (db.users.where("age").gt(18).select("name")) using Proxy to intercept property access, Reflect for meta-operations, and generators for lazy result iteration. Domain: backend tooling. Covers: prototypes, classes, iterators/generators, symbols, meta-programming, Proxy, Reflect.

**Cluster B — "Reactive State Store from Scratch" (Hard)**

Build a tiny Redux/Zustand-like state management library using Proxy for reactivity, Symbols for private internal keys, and a plugin system using meta-programming. Ship as an npm-installable package with tests. Domain: frontend infrastructure. Why it’s real: this is literally how real state libraries are built internally.

**Cluster B — "Iterable Deck of Cards / Card Game Engine" (Medium)**

Model a deck of cards as a custom iterable (Symbol.iterator), build a generator-based shuffle/deal system, and implement Blackjack or War fully in the terminal. Domain: games.

**Cluster C — "Weather-Aware Commute Planner" (Medium)**

A Node script that calls a real weather API and a geocoding API concurrently using Promise.all, handles partial failures with Promise.allSettled, retries with exponential backoff, and outputs a commute recommendation. Domain: productivity/logistics. Covers: modules, event loop, async, promises, HTTP/APIs.

**Cluster C — "Rate-Limited API Aggregator" (Hard)**

A tool that fetches data from 3+ public APIs while respecting each one’s rate limit using a hand-written async queue/throttler, with correct microtask/macrotask ordering, and caches results. Domain: data engineering.

**Cluster C — "Live Order Status Simulator" (Medium)**

Simulate an e-commerce order pipeline (payment → packing → shipping → delivered) as async stages with realistic delays, custom event emitters, and retry logic on failure. Domain: e-commerce/logistics.

**Cluster D — "Kanban Board with Drag-and-Drop, Zero Frameworks" (Medium–Hard)**

A fully vanilla-JS Trello-style kanban board: native Drag and Drop API, localStorage persistence, keyboard accessibility, IntersectionObserver-based lazy rendering. Domain: productivity tools. Covers: DOM, events, forms, storage, browser APIs, observers, workers, binary data.

**Cluster D — "Client-Side Image Compressor" (Hard)**

Users drag in images, compression/resizing happens client-side via Canvas \+ binary data (ArrayBuffer/Blob), the compression runs in a Web Worker so the UI never freezes, and results are downloadable — zero backend. Domain: media tooling / privacy-first apps. Why it’s real: this is how tools like Squoosh.app actually work.

**Cluster D — "Multiplayer Cursor Presence Demo" (Hard)**

Using the BroadcastChannel API, multiple browser tabs of the "same user" see each other’s mouse cursors move in real time — groundwork for the real-time collaboration phase later in the roadmap. Domain: collaborative software.

**Cluster E (Capstone) — "JS Performance Profiler Playground" (Hard)**

Build a page with 5 intentional performance bugs (memory leaks, unthrottled scroll listeners, layout thrashing, closure leaks, unbounded cache) then a fixed version, documenting before/after with Chrome DevTools Performance & Memory tabs. Domain: web performance engineering.

**Cluster E — "Functional Data Pipeline Library" (Hard)**

Build your own functional utility library (pipe, compose, curry, memoize, immutable update helpers) from scratch, fully unit-tested, then use it to process a real CSV dataset end-to-end. Domain: data processing / dev tools.

**Cluster E (Stage Capstone) — "Design Patterns Museum" (Medium–Hard)**

A single documented codebase implementing 8 classic design patterns (Observer, Factory, Singleton, Strategy, Decorator, Command, Module, Facade), each solving a real mini-problem, with tests and a written doc explaining when to use each. Domain: software architecture. Why it’s real: genuinely useful as a portfolio artifact demonstrating architectural maturity.

**Practice Exercises**

* Implement Array.prototype.flat, .reduce, and .map from scratch without using the built-ins.  

* Given a large nested object, write a function to deep-freeze it recursively.  

* Implement an LRU cache using a Map — no library.  

* Build a custom iterable Range class usable in a for-of loop.  

* Implement a generator-based infinite Fibonacci sequence with a take(n) helper.  

* Recreate class inheritance manually using Object.create and prototype chains, then compare.  

* Implement Promise.all, Promise.race, and Promise.allSettled from scratch using only the Promise constructor.  

* Write debounce and throttle from scratch and prove correctness with a fake-timer test harness.  

* Diagram the exact call-stack/microtask/macrotask order of a script mixing setTimeout, Promise.then, and async/await, then verify by running it.  

* Build a custom event delegation system for a 1000-row table using a single listener.  

* Implement infinite scroll using IntersectionObserver without any library.  

* Write a vanilla JS form validator supporting async validation with correct race-condition handling (ignore stale requests).  

* Find and fix a memory leak in a provided buggy vanilla JS app (detached DOM references).  

* Write unit tests for your functional utility library.  

* Refactor a 200-line "spaghetti" script into clean architecture using at least 2 design patterns, and justify the choices in writing.

# **STAGE 5 — Adding Types: TypeScript**

*TypeScript only makes sense once JavaScript is solid — it's a typed layer on top of JS, not a separate language. Intro concepts come first, then the full Mastery set from Phase 01b, each ordered so foundational type mechanics (generics, narrowing) precede the advanced techniques that combine them (conditional/mapped types, template literals).*

## **▌ 55\. TypeScript — Introduction**

**Why TypeScript**

* Static typing vs dynamic typing  

* Compile-time error catching  

* TypeScript vs JavaScript relationship (superset)

**Basic Types**

* number, string, boolean, null, undefined  

* any, unknown, never, void  

* Arrays and tuples  

* Enums

**Type Annotations**

* Variable type annotations  

* Function parameter and return type annotations  

* Object type annotations (inline)

**Interfaces & Type Aliases**

* interface declarations  

* type aliases  

* Optional properties (?)  

* Readonly properties  

* Extending interfaces

**Functions in TypeScript**

* Typed function parameters and return types  

* Optional and default parameters  

* Function overloads (intro)

**Union & Intersection Types (intro)**

* Union types (|)  

* Intersection types (&)  

* Basic type narrowing with typeof/instanceof

**Classes in TypeScript**

* Access modifiers (public, private, protected)  

* readonly properties  

* Implementing interfaces  

* Abstract classes (intro)

**Compiler Basics**

* tsc compiler  

* Basic tsconfig.json options (target, module, strict)

## **▌ 56\. tsconfig.json (Deep Dive)**

*Full configuration reference, now that basic tsconfig options are familiar from Topic 55\.*

* strict family flags individually (strictNullChecks, noImplicitAny, etc.)  

* Module resolution options  

* Path mapping (paths, baseUrl)  

* Project references

## **▌ 57\. Type Narrowing (Deep Dive)**

*Narrowing was touched briefly in the intro (typeof/instanceof) — now goes deep, and must come before Discriminated Unions since discriminated unions are a narrowing pattern.*

* in operator narrowing  

* Truthiness narrowing  

* Equality narrowing  

* Control flow analysis

## **▌ 58\. Discriminated Unions**

*Depends directly on Type Narrowing (Stage 5.57).*

* Tagged/discriminated unions  

* Exhaustive switch statements over discriminated unions  

* Type narrowing via discriminant checks

## **▌ 59\. Generics**

*Independent of narrowing/unions, but needed before Conditional Types and Mapped Types, both of which build on generic type parameters.*

* Generic functions  

* Generic interfaces and type aliases  

* Generic constraints (extends)  

* Default generic parameters

## **▌ 60\. as const & satisfies**

* const assertions (as const)  

* satisfies operator

## **▌ 61\. Utility Types**

*Requires generics (59) — utility types are generic type functions.*

* Partial, Required, Readonly, Record  

* Pick, Omit, Exclude, Extract  

* ReturnType, Parameters, InstanceType

## **▌ 62\. Mapped Types**

*Requires generics (59) and a sense of utility types (61), since utility types are themselves implemented as mapped types.*

* Basic mapped type syntax  

* Key remapping (as clause)  

* Mapped type modifiers (+/- readonly, \+/- ?)

## **▌ 63\. Conditional Types**

*Requires generics (59) — conditional types branch on generic type parameters.*

* Basic conditional type syntax  

* infer keyword  

* Distributive conditional types

## **▌ 64\. Template Literal Types**

*Requires conditional and mapped types (62, 63\\) — template literal types are typically combined with both.*

* Template literal type syntax  

* String manipulation types (Uppercase, Lowercase, etc.)  

* Combining with unions for string pattern generation

## **▌ 65\. Declaration Files**

* Writing .d.ts files  

* declare keyword  

* Ambient declarations  

* Using @types packages

## **▌ 66\. Zod**

*Applies the full type system (generics, unions, inference) to runtime schema validation — placed last as the practical synthesis.*

* Schema definition basics  

* Type inference from schemas (z.infer)  

* Unions and discriminated unions in Zod  

* Using Zod for API request/response validation

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Type-Safe Config Loader" (Easy)**

A small library that loads and validates environment-variable-based app config, using discriminated unions for different deploy environments and strict TypeScript to make invalid states unrepresentable. Domain: dev tooling.

**"Generic Data Table Library" (Medium)**

Build a fully generic, reusable data-table utility (sort/filter/paginate) typed with generics so it works correctly for any row shape (DataTable\\\<User\\\>, DataTable\\\<Order\\\>) with full autocomplete and compile-time safety on column keys. Domain: frontend infrastructure.

**"Typed API Client Generator" (Hard)**

Given an OpenAPI-like JSON schema, generate fully-typed TypeScript API client functions using mapped types, conditional types, and template literal types (e.g. typed route strings), plus a Zod runtime validator layer so types are enforced at compile time and runtime. Domain: API tooling / codegen. Why it’s real: this is essentially what openapi-typescript and tRPC do internally.

**Practice Exercises**

* Convert Stage 4’s "Design Patterns Museum" JS project fully to strict TypeScript.  

* Write a type-level "Tuple to Union" and "Union to Intersection" utility type from scratch.  

* Use satisfies to type a config object while preserving literal types, and explain why as const alone wasn’t enough.  

* Write 5 custom Utility Types (DeepPartial\\\<T\\\>, DeepReadonly\\\<T\\\>, PickByType\\\<T, ValueType\\\>) from scratch.  

* Author a .d.ts declaration file for a small untyped npm package following DefinitelyTyped conventions.  

* Build a Zod schema and derive its TypeScript type with z.infer, then intentionally break the schema and observe how type errors cascade.

# **STAGE 6 — Data: SQL Fundamentals**

*SQL is intentionally placed after the full JS/TS language stack rather than earlier — it's conceptually independent of JavaScript, and interleaving it earlier would split focus mid-language. By this point you also have enough programming maturity (data structures, objects) to map naturally onto relational concepts (tables ≈ arrays of objects, rows ≈ objects, columns ≈ keys).*

## **▌ 67\. SQL Fundamentals**

**Relational Database Concepts**

* Tables, rows, columns  

* Primary keys and foreign keys  

* Relationships (one-to-one, one-to-many, many-to-many)

**Schema Basics**

* CREATE TABLE  

* Data types (INT, VARCHAR, TEXT, DATE, BOOLEAN, etc.)  

* Constraints (NOT NULL, UNIQUE, DEFAULT, CHECK)  

* ALTER TABLE

**Basic Queries**

* SELECT statement  

* WHERE clause and comparison operators  

* ORDER BY  

* LIMIT / OFFSET  

* DISTINCT

**Filtering & Logic**

* AND, OR, NOT  

* IN, BETWEEN, LIKE  

* IS NULL / IS NOT NULL

**Data Modification**

* INSERT INTO  

* UPDATE  

* DELETE  

* Basic transactions (COMMIT, ROLLBACK concept)

**Joins**

*(Requires multiple tables to exist — placed after basic CRUD so there's something to join)*

* INNER JOIN  

* LEFT JOIN / RIGHT JOIN  

* FULL OUTER JOIN  

* Self joins (intro)

**Aggregation**

*(Builds on joins — real aggregation usually spans joined tables)*

* GROUP BY  

* HAVING  

* Aggregate functions (COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX)

**Indexes (intro)**

* What an index is and why it speeds up reads  

* Basic CREATE INDEX

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — Data: SQL Fundamentals  (12 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| SQL Fundamentals | 12 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Library Fine Calculator, In Pure SQL" (Easy)**

Design a normalized schema (Books, Members, Loans) and write pure SQL queries (joins, aggregates) to calculate overdue fines, most-borrowed books, and member activity reports. Domain: civic/library systems.

**"E-commerce Analytics Query Pack" (Medium)**

Given a realistic multi-table e-commerce schema, write 15 real analytical queries a business would actually ask for: cohort retention, top products by margin, customers at risk of churn via window functions/RFM logic. Domain: e-commerce analytics.

**"Query Optimization Challenge" (Hard)**

Given a deliberately slow, badly-indexed schema and slow queries, rewrite/optimize each (correct indexes, restructuring subqueries into joins/CTEs) and document before/after EXPLAIN plans and execution time. Domain: database performance.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a self-join query to find employees who report to the same manager.  

* Write a query using window functions (RANK, LAG) to find each customer’s first vs. most recent order.  

* Convert 5 correlated subqueries into equivalent JOIN-based queries.  

* Design and normalize a schema (to 3NF) for a hospital appointment system from a messy flat spreadsheet.  

* Write a recursive CTE to compute an org chart’s reporting hierarchy depth.

# **STAGE 7 — Collaboration & Code Quality**

*Everything in this final stage assumes you now have real, multi-file projects worth versioning, linting, and formatting properly — HTML/CSS pages, JS/TS scripts, and SQL schemas from Stages 2–6. This is why deeper Git workflows, commit hygiene, and tooling are saved for last rather than front-loaded in Stage 1, where you had nothing substantial to collaborate on yet.*

* Feature branch workflow  

* Git flow (concept)

* .gitattributes basics

**Inspecting History**

* git diff  

* git log formatting and filtering  

* git blame  

* Tags (git tag)

### **Stage 7 Projects & Exercises — Collaboration & Code Quality  (13 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Git \+ GitHub (Collaboration Deep Dive) | 6 |
| Conventional Commits | 2 |
| ESLint | 3 |
| Prettier | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Open-Source-Style Contribution Simulation" (Easy)**

Take any earlier stage project and simulate a full open-source contribution workflow solo: feature branch, Conventional Commits, a PR description template, a CHANGELOG.md generated from commit history. Domain: OSS practice.

**"Team Code Quality Gate" (Medium)**

Configure a shared ESLint \+ Prettier config as its own publishable npm package (@yourname/eslint-config) that multiple projects could extend, including at least one custom lint rule for a project-specific convention. Domain: engineering tooling.

**"Git Archaeology Report" (Hard)**

Pick a real open-source repo, use git log, git blame, and git bisect to trace the history of a specific bug fix or feature through its evolution, and write a technical report, including reconstructing a rebase/merge scenario locally. Domain: software forensics.

**Practice Exercises**

* Rewrite 10 messy commit messages from a provided bad-history repo into Conventional Commits format via interactive rebase.  

* Deliberately create a 3-way merge conflict and resolve it manually via CLI.  

* Set up a pre-commit hook (Husky or plain Git hook) that runs ESLint \+ Prettier before allowing a commit.  

* Use git bisect to find which of 20 commits introduced a bug in a provided broken repo.  

* Squash 5 WIP commits into one clean commit via interactive rebase without losing any changes.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "DevScribe" — A Personal Developer Journal & Snippet Manager**

Build a complete, deployable application that: is scaffolded with a proper dotfiles-provisioned dev environment (Stage 1); has full semantic, accessible HTML structure (Stage 2); is responsively styled with hand-written CSS Grid/Flexbox, no framework (Stage 3); implements all core logic in strict TypeScript compiled from scratch, covering async data fetching from a raw Node http server, custom iterators for browsing entries, a Proxy-based reactive UI updater, Web Worker-based full-text search indexing, and IndexedDB or a JSON file store for persistence (Stages 4–5); uses a real SQL database (SQLite is fine) with a normalized schema for entries/tags/snippets and at least 3 non-trivial analytical queries (Stage 6); and is version-controlled with clean Conventional Commit history, a CONTRIBUTING.md, linting, and a documented Git workflow (Stage 7). Deliverable: a working local-first journal/snippet app plus a written architecture doc explaining every technical decision. Domain: developer productivity tools. Why it matters: this project alone demonstrates command of the entire JS/TS/SQL/Git foundation without leaning on any framework crutch.

# **Summary: The Full Sequence at a Glance**

| Stage | Focus | Topics |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | Workspace | VS Code → Bash → nvm/npm → SSH Keys → Git (basics) |
| 2 | Structure | HTML5 |
| 3 | Presentation | CSS3 |
| 4 | Behavior | JavaScript — full dependency-ordered sequence (Language Core → Data Structures → Error Handling/Regex → OOP → Meta-Programming → Modules → Async → HTTP/DOM/Browser → Performance/Security → Functional Patterns → Architecture → Algorithms → Tooling/Testing/Debugging → Engine Internals/Spec) |
| 5 | Types | TypeScript Intro → tsconfig → Narrowing → Discriminated Unions → Generics → as const → satisfies → Utility Types → Mapped Types → Conditional Types → Template Literal Types → Declaration Files → Zod |
| 6 | Data | SQL Fundamentals |
| 7 | Collaboration | Git (deep) → Conventional Commits → ESLint → Prettier |

This is the order in which each concept's prerequisites are guaranteed to already exist. No topic here references something you haven't learned yet.
