# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 02 — React \+ Next.js Core, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 02 budget (146 core hours), the same way the Phase 01 JavaScript stage expanded beyond its 50h line item. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the actual sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports. If you want a version that fits 146h, ask for the trimmed version; this doc is the deep one by design.**

Every topic from Phase 02, resequenced by real dependency and expanded to the depth a working React/Next.js engineer actually needs — not just the topic list, but the sub-mechanics underneath each one.

# **STAGE 1 — React 18 (Foundation)**

*Everything else in Phase 02 sits on top of React. Next.js is a framework around React, not a replacement for it — so React must be solid, and deep, first.*

## **▌ 1\. React Fundamentals**

**JSX & Rendering**

* JSX syntax and compilation to React.createElement  

* Expressions in JSX  

* Fragments (\\\<\\\>, React.Fragment)  

* Conditional rendering (&&, ternaries, early returns)  

* Rendering lists and the role of key  

* Why array index as key is usually wrong

**Components & Props**

* Function components  

* Props: passing, destructuring, default values  

* children prop  

* Prop drilling and why it becomes a problem  

* PropTypes vs TypeScript for prop validation (conceptual)

**State**

* useState mechanics  

* State updates are asynchronous/batched  

* Functional updates (setState(prev \\=\\\> ...))  

* State colocation principle  

* Lifting state up  

* Derived state vs redundant state

## **▌ 2\. Component Lifecycle & Effects**

*Requires state (1) — effects exist to synchronize with state changes.*

* useEffect: mount, update, cleanup phases  

* Dependency array semantics (missing deps, exhaustive-deps rule)  

* Cleanup functions and when they run  

* Effect timing vs layout effects (useLayoutEffect)  

* Common pitfalls: stale closures, infinite loops, effects that should be event handlers instead  

* Data fetching in effects vs modern alternatives (deferred to Stage 5\\)

## **▌ 3\. The Full Hook Set**

*Requires state and effects (1, 2\\) — every other hook either builds on state or exists to solve a problem effects introduce.*

**Ref & DOM Access**

* useRef for mutable values that don't trigger re-render  

* useRef for DOM node access  

* forwardRef  

* useImperativeHandle (advanced ref patterns)

**Performance Hooks**

* useMemo — memoizing expensive computations  

* useCallback — memoizing function identities  

* React.memo — skipping re-renders on unchanged props  

* When memoization helps vs when it's premature optimization  

* Referential equality pitfalls (objects/arrays recreated each render)

**Context**

* createContext, Provider, useContext  

* Avoiding prop drilling with context  

* Context re-render implications (all consumers re-render on change)  

* Splitting contexts to limit re-render scope  

* When to use Context vs a state management library

**Reducer Pattern**

* useReducer for complex state logic  

* Action/dispatch pattern  

* useReducer \+ Context as a lightweight state management combo

**React 18 Concurrent Hooks**

* useTransition — marking updates as non-urgent  

* useDeferredValue — deferring expensive re-renders  

* Concurrent rendering conceptually: interruptible rendering  

* Automatic batching (all updates, not just event handlers)

**Custom Hooks**

* Extracting reusable stateful logic  

* Naming conventions (use prefix)  

* Composing hooks from other hooks  

* Common custom hook patterns: useLocalStorage, useDebounce, useToggle, useFetch (conceptual precursor to Stage 5\\)

## **▌ 4\. Forms & Events**

*Requires state (1) — forms are a direct application of controlled state.*

* Controlled vs uncontrolled inputs  

* Event handling — SyntheticEvent system  

* Form submission patterns, preventDefault  

* Multi-field form state management (single object vs multiple useState)  

* Validation patterns (inline vs on-submit vs library-based, conceptual)

## **▌ 5\. Component Design Patterns**

*Requires props, children, and hooks (1–3) — these patterns are built from those primitives.*

* Composition over inheritance  

* children as a composition mechanism  

* Render props pattern  

* Compound components pattern (e.g. \\\<Tabs\\\>\\\<Tab/\\\>\\\</Tabs\\\>)  

* Container/presentational component split (conceptual, historically significant)  

* Controlled component pattern at the component-API level (not just inputs)

## **▌ 6\. Error Boundaries & Portals**

*Requires component lifecycle understanding (2) — error boundaries are class components that hook into lifecycle error methods.*

* Error boundaries (why they must be class components)  

* getDerivedStateFromError, componentDidCatch  

* Where to place error boundaries in a tree  

* createPortal — rendering outside the parent DOM hierarchy  

* Portal use cases: modals, tooltips, toasts

## **▌ 7\. Suspense & Concurrent Rendering (Client-Side)**

*Requires transitions (3) and error boundaries (6) — Suspense is conceptually similar to an error boundary but for loading states.*

* Suspense boundaries and fallback UI  

* Suspense for lazy-loaded components (React.lazy)  

* Suspense for data (conceptual bridge to Server Components in Stage 2\\)  

* How Suspense interacts with transitions

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — React 18 (Foundation)  (37 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| React Fundamentals | 8 |
| Component Lifecycle & Effects | 4 |
| The Full Hook Set | 10 |
| Forms & Events | 3 |
| Component Design Patterns | 5 |
| Error Boundaries & Portals | 3 |
| Suspense & Concurrent Rendering (Client-Side) | 4 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Habit Tracker with Streaks" (Easy)**

A single-page habit tracker using function components, hooks (useState/useEffect), controlled forms, and localStorage persistence. Domain: personal wellness.

**"Multi-Step Job Application Wizard" (Medium)**

A polished multi-step form (resume upload, work history, review) using component composition patterns (compound components), error boundaries per step, and portals for a confirmation modal. Domain: HR tech / recruiting.

**"Concurrent Rendering Image Gallery" (Hard)**

A masonry image gallery that uses Suspense for image loading, startTransition for non-blocking filter/search updates, and an error boundary fallback per image tile. Includes a deliberate slow-network simulation to demonstrate concurrent rendering benefits visibly. Domain: media/photography platforms.

**Practice Exercises**

* Convert 5 class-based lifecycle patterns (componentDidMount, componentDidUpdate, componentWillUnmount) into equivalent useEffect hooks and explain the differences in timing.  

* Build a custom hook useDebouncedValue and use it in a live search box.  

* Build 2 compound components (Tabs, Accordion) from scratch without a library.  

* Trigger and catch a rendering error using an Error Boundary, with a "Try again" reset button.  

* Use createPortal to build a toast notification system rendered outside the main DOM tree.

# **STAGE 2 — Next.js 15 App Router (Core Framework)**

*Requires all of Stage 1 — the App Router is React Server Components plus a file-system router, so nothing here works without React fundamentals, hooks, and Suspense already understood.*

## **▌ 8\. Routing Fundamentals**

* File-system based routing (the app/ directory)  

* page.tsx as a route's UI  

* layout.tsx and nested layouts (persisted across navigation)  

* loading.tsx — automatic Suspense boundary per route segment  

* error.tsx — automatic error boundary per route segment  

* template.tsx vs layout.tsx (re-mounting behavior)  

* Dynamic segments (\\\[id\\\]) and catch-all segments (\\\[...slug\\\], \\\[\\\[...slug\\\]\\\])  

* not-found.tsx and the not-found convention

## **▌ 9\. Server Components vs Client Components**

*The central conceptual shift of the App Router — requires understanding React rendering (Stage 1\\) to appreciate what's different.*

* Server Components by default — what runs where  

* "use client" boundary and where to draw it  

* Why Server Components can't use hooks or browser APIs  

* Passing serializable props from Server to Client Components  

* Composition pattern: passing Client Components as children to Server Components  

* Third-party component libraries and the client boundary problem

## **▌ 10\. Data Fetching & the Caching Model**

*Requires Server Components (9) — this is the App Router's signature feature, fetching directly in components without client-side effects.*

* fetch() directly in Server Components (async components)  

* Request Memoization — deduping identical fetches in a single render pass  

* Data Cache — persistent cache across requests/deploys  

* Full Route Cache — cached rendered output for static routes  

* Router Cache — client-side cache of visited route segments  

* Static vs dynamic rendering — what triggers each  

* Route segment config: dynamic, revalidate, fetchCache options  

* Streaming with Suspense — progressively rendering as data resolves

## **▌ 11\. Server Actions & Mutations**

*Requires Server/Client boundary (9) and forms (Stage 1, Topic 4\\) — Server Actions are the App Router's answer to form mutations.*

* Defining Server Actions ("use server")  

* Calling Server Actions from forms (progressive enhancement — works without JS)  

* Calling Server Actions from event handlers  

* useFormStatus, useFormState for pending/error UI  

* Revalidation after mutation (bridges to Stage 3 revalidatePath/Tag)  

* Security considerations: Server Actions are public endpoints

## **▌ 12\. Route Handlers (API Routes)**

*Requires routing fundamentals (8) — Route Handlers are the App Router's file-based API endpoint convention, parallel to page.tsx.*

* route.ts convention  

* Supported HTTP methods (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, etc.)  

* Request and Response objects (Web standard APIs)  

* When to use a Route Handler vs a Server Action

## **▌ 13\. Static Generation & ISR**

*Requires the caching model (10) — static generation is one of the rendering strategies that model supports.*

* generateStaticParams — pre-rendering dynamic routes at build time  

* Incremental Static Regeneration (ISR) via the revalidate option  

* On-demand revalidation vs time-based revalidation  

* Build-time vs request-time tradeoffs

## **▌ 14\. Metadata, SEO & Optimization APIs**

* Metadata API — static metadata export and generateMetadata()  

* Open Graph and Twitter card metadata  

* next/image — automatic image optimization, layout shift prevention  

* next/font — font loading and optimization  

* next/script — third-party script loading strategies

## **▌ 15\. Middleware**

*Requires the Edge Runtime concept, previewed here and formalized in Stage 6 — Middleware runs on every matched request before routing resolves.*

* middleware.ts convention and matcher config  

* Use cases: auth redirects, A/B testing, geolocation-based routing  

* NextRequest/NextResponse APIs  

* Middleware runs on the Edge Runtime — implications for what APIs are available

## **▌ 16\. Fetch API (Next.js-Extended Semantics)**

*Fetch itself is a Web/JS standard, but placed here rather than in Phase 01 because Next.js extends it with its own caching options — learning it in isolation first would teach the wrong mental model for this framework.*

* Extended fetch() options: cache, next.revalidate, next.tags  

* How extended fetch integrates with the four-cache model (Topic 10\\)  

* Error handling for fetch failures in Server Components

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Next.js 15 App Router (Core Framework)  (35 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Routing Fundamentals | 4 |
| Server Components vs Client Components | 5 |
| Data Fetching & the Caching Model | 6 |
| Server Actions & Mutations | 5 |
| Route Handlers (API Routes) | 3 |
| Static Generation & ISR | 4 |
| Metadata, SEO & Optimization APIs | 3 |
| Middleware | 3 |
| Fetch API (Next.js-Extended Semantics) | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Local Business Directory" (Easy)**

A statically generated directory of local businesses (seeded JSON/CSV data) using file-based routing, dynamic segments, and generateStaticParams. Domain: local commerce.

**"Newsroom CMS Front-End" (Medium)**

A news site pulling articles from a headless data source, mixing Server and Client Components correctly (interactive comment section as client, article body as server), using Server Actions for comment submission, and ISR for article freshness. Domain: media/publishing.

**"Multi-Tenant SaaS Marketing Site \+ Dashboard Shell" (Hard)**

A realistic SaaS product shell: public marketing pages (fully static, optimized metadata/SEO), an authenticated dashboard area using middleware for route protection, Route Handlers acting as a lightweight BFF, and a caching strategy documented per route (static / ISR / dynamic). Domain: B2B SaaS.

**Practice Exercises**

* Diagram (in writing) which of your project’s routes are Server vs Client Components and justify each choice.  

* Build a Server Action that mutates data and triggers revalidation without a full page reload.  

* Implement middleware that redirects unauthenticated users away from a protected route group.  

* Compare and document the actual caching behavior (via network tab) of a static, ISR, and fully dynamic route in the same app.  

* Add structured metadata (Open Graph, Twitter cards) to 3 different route types and validate with a social preview debugger.

# **STAGE 3 — App Router: Advanced Routing Features**

*All topics in this stage are App Router features layered on top of Stage 2's routing model — none make sense without layouts, segments, and the caching model already understood.*

## **▌ 17\. Route Groups**

*Simplest advanced routing feature — pure organizational syntax, no new rendering behavior.*

* (folderName) syntax — grouping routes without affecting the URL  

* Applying different root layouts to route subsets  

* Organizing by feature/team without URL nesting

## **▌ 18\. notFound()**

*Depends on knowing how not-found.tsx works in the layout tree (Stage 2, Topic 8).*

* Programmatically triggering the nearest not-found.tsx  

* Difference from redirect() and throwing an error  

* Where in the component tree the boundary resolves

## **▌ 19\. Parallel Routes**

*Requires Route Groups (17) as an organizational prerequisite and layouts (Stage 2\\) — parallel routes render multiple pages into named slots within one layout.*

* @slot naming convention  

* default.tsx for unmatched slots on navigation  

* Independent loading.tsx / error.tsx per slot  

* Use cases: dashboards with independent panels, split views

## **▌ 20\. Intercepting Routes**

*Builds directly on Parallel Routes (19) — most commonly combined with them to implement the modal-over-page pattern.*

* (.), (..), (..)(..), (...) interception path conventions  

* Combining with Parallel Routes for the modal pattern (e.g. photo grid → modal, direct link → full page)  

* Soft navigation (intercepted) vs hard reload/direct link behavior

## **▌ 21\. revalidatePath()**

*Requires the caching model (Stage 2, Topic 10\\) — revalidation only makes sense once you understand what's being cached and why.*

* On-demand revalidation for a specific path  

* Typical use: calling after a Server Action mutation  

* Path matching behavior (exact vs layout revalidation)

## **▌ 22\. revalidateTag()**

*Same family as revalidatePath (21), placed immediately after — tag-based revalidation is the more granular alternative once path-based revalidation is understood.*

* Tagging fetch requests via next.tags (Stage 2, Topic 16\\)  

* Invalidating by tag instead of path  

* When tags are preferable to paths (e.g. one tag spanning multiple routes)

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — App Router: Advanced Routing Features  (11.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Route Groups | 1.5 |
| notFound() | 1 |
| Parallel Routes | 3 |
| Intercepting Routes | 3 |
| revalidatePath() | 1.5 |
| revalidateTag() | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Photo App with Modal Routes" (Medium)**

An Instagram-style photo grid where clicking a photo opens it in a modal (intercepting routes) but a direct URL visit renders the full page — correct back/forward browser behavior included. Domain: social/media.

**"Admin Panel with Parallel Dashboards" (Medium–Hard)**

An admin panel using parallel routes to simultaneously render independent panels (e.g., analytics \+ recent activity \+ alerts) that load and error independently, plus route groups to separate authenticated vs public layouts. Domain: internal tools/admin.

**"Selective Cache-Busting Content Platform" (Hard)**

A content platform where editors can trigger revalidatePath for a single article or revalidateTag for an entire content category, with a small internal "publish" UI that demonstrates surgical cache invalidation and a notFound() fallback for unpublished/deleted content. Domain: publishing/CMS.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build an intercepting route for a modal and verify it degrades correctly to a full page on refresh.  

* Implement 2 independent parallel route slots with independent loading states.  

* Trigger notFound() conditionally based on a database lookup and design a helpful 404 UI.  

* Call revalidateTag from a Server Action and prove (via timestamps) that only tagged data refreshes.  

* Organize a project into at least 2 route groups with different layouts and explain the folder structure decision.

# **STAGE 4 — Client-Side Concerns**

*Independent of the App Router internals in Stage 3 — these are client-side/browser concerns that fit better once you have real components and pages to apply them to.*

## **▌ 23\. Browser Storage**

* localStorage — persisting data across sessions  

* sessionStorage — per-tab, cleared on tab close  

* Reading storage safely in a Server-Component-first app (client-only APIs, hydration mismatch risk)  

* Wrapping storage access in a custom hook (ties back to Stage 1, Topic 3\\)

## **▌ 24\. React Router v6 (Non-Next.js Context)**

*Placed deliberately after the App Router (Stage 2–3), not before — learning Next's router first prevents confusing the two routing models. Relevant for plain Vite/CRA React apps where App Router isn't available.*

* \\\<BrowserRouter\\\>, \\\<Routes\\\>, \\\<Route\\\>  

* Nested routes and layouts (React Router's own version, contrasted with Next.js layouts)  

* useNavigate, useParams, useLocation, useSearchParams  

* Protected/private route patterns  

* Data loading patterns (loader functions) as a contrast to Server Component fetching

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Client-Side Concerns  (5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Browser Storage | 2 |
| React Router v6 (Non-Next.js Context) | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Persistent Shopping Cart" (Easy)**

A shopping cart that survives page reloads and tab closes using browser storage with a clean abstraction layer (not raw localStorage calls scattered everywhere). Domain: e-commerce.

**"Non-Next.js SPA Router Migration Demo" (Medium)**

Take a small existing feature and implement it twice: once with Next.js file-based routing, once as a standalone SPA using React Router v6, documenting the API and mental-model differences (loaders, nested routes, navigation guards). Domain: educational/comparative engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a storage abstraction that gracefully falls back to in-memory storage when localStorage is unavailable (e.g., private browsing).  

* Implement nested routes with a shared layout and an active-link indicator using React Router v6.  

* Implement a protected-route wrapper in React Router v6 and compare it to Next.js middleware-based protection.

# **STAGE 5 — Data Layer Integration**

## **▌ 25\. Client-Side Data Fetching Libraries (Conceptual Prerequisite)**

*Not an explicit line item in the original topic list, but required conceptual grounding before Hydration (26) makes sense — added here rather than assumed.*

* Why client-side fetching libraries exist alongside Server Components (client-triggered mutations, polling, optimistic updates)  

* Core concepts: query keys, caching, stale-while-revalidate  

* Where React Query / TanStack Query fits relative to Server Components

## **▌ 26\. HydrationBoundary and dehydrate()**

*Requires Server/Client boundaries (Stage 2, Topic 9\\) and the data-fetching library context (25) — this is the mechanism for passing server-fetched data into client-side cache without a refetch.*

* Server-side prefetching pattern in a Server Component  

* dehydrate() on the server, HydrationBoundary on the client  

* Avoiding request waterfalls and duplicate fetches on initial load  

* Combining with Server Actions for post-mutation cache updates

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Data Layer Integration  (5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Client-Side Data Fetching Libraries (Conceptual Prerequisite) | 2 |
| HydrationBoundary and dehydrate() | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Server-to-Client Hydration Demo Dashboard" (Medium)**

A dashboard that prefetches data on the server, dehydrates it, and hydrates a client-side query cache (HydrationBoundary) so the first paint is instant and subsequent interactions are fully client-cached. Domain: analytics/SaaS.

**"Offline-Tolerant Notes App Prerequisite Layer" (Medium–Hard)**

Build the client-side data-fetching foundation (before the full offline sync work happens later in the roadmap) for a notes app: a documented conceptual comparison of fetch-based, SWR-style, and query-cache-based approaches, implemented as 3 small parallel prototypes of the same feature. Domain: productivity tools.

**Practice Exercises**

* Prefetch a data query on the server and confirm zero client-side loading spinner on first render.  

* Deliberately mismatch server and client dehydrated state and diagnose the hydration warning.  

* Write a one-page comparison document of 3 client data-fetching approaches with tradeoffs.

# **STAGE 6 — Deployment**

*Deliberately last. Deployment only matters once there's a real, working app — learning Vercel configuration before pages, data fetching, and routing exist would be abstract and unmotivated.*

## **▌ 27\. Vercel**

* Connecting a GitHub repo to Vercel  

* Build and output settings for Next.js (auto-detected, and manual overrides)  

* Environment variables — build-time vs runtime, per-environment (dev/preview/prod)  

* Preview deployments per branch/PR  

* Vercel Analytics and Speed Insights (conceptual overview)

## **▌ 28\. Vercel Edge Functions**

*Requires Vercel basics (27) — Edge Functions are a deployment target within Vercel's platform.*

* Edge Function basics and use cases (geolocation, auth checks, personalization)  

* Cold start differences vs traditional serverless functions  

* Limitations — no full Node.js API surface

## **▌ 29\. Vercel Edge Runtime**

*Builds on Edge Functions (28) — the Edge Runtime is the execution environment those functions and Middleware (Stage 2, Topic 15\\) run in.*

* Edge Runtime vs Node.js runtime differences (available APIs, execution model)  

* Setting runtime \\= 'edge' in route segment config  

* Connecting this back to Middleware's runtime constraints

## **▌ 30\. Image Optimization Utilities: plaiceholder**

*Small, isolated topic — an image blur-placeholder utility, most relevant once real images are being served from a deployed app.*

* Generating blur data URLs at build time  

* Using with next/image's placeholder prop  

* Relationship to next/image's built-in optimization (Stage 2, Topic 14\\)

## **▌ 31\. GitHub Pages (Alternative Static Deployment)**

*Placed last as an alternative, static-only deployment target — learned after Vercel so the contrast (static export vs full Next.js server features) is meaningful rather than the first thing seen.*

* next export / output: 'export' — static export limitations (no Server Actions, no ISR, no Middleware)  

* GitHub Pages deployment via GitHub Actions  

* Deciding when static export is and isn't appropriate for a given app

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — Deployment  (8.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Vercel | 2 |
| Vercel Edge Functions | 2 |
| Vercel Edge Runtime | 1.5 |
| Image Optimization Utilities: plaiceholder | 1.5 |
| GitHub Pages (Alternative Static Deployment) | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Portfolio Site, Two Ways" (Easy)**

Deploy the same static portfolio site to both Vercel and GitHub Pages, documenting configuration differences (base path, environment variables, build commands). Domain: personal branding.

**"Edge-Personalized Landing Page" (Medium)**

A marketing landing page that uses a Vercel Edge Function to personalize content based on geolocation/headers (e.g., currency or language hint) with near-zero latency, benchmarked against a non-edge equivalent. Domain: marketing/growth.

**"Image-Heavy Blog with Optimized Delivery" (Medium–Hard)**

A photography/blog site using blur placeholders (plaiceholder) generated at build time, deployed on Vercel with documented Core Web Vitals before/after optimization. Domain: media/publishing.

**Practice Exercises**

* Deploy the same app to Vercel and GitHub Pages and document every configuration difference required.  

* Write and deploy an Edge Function that reads a request header and returns a personalized response.  

* Measure and record Largest Contentful Paint before and after adding blur placeholders to an image-heavy page.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "CivicBoard" — A Server-Rendered Local Government Issue Tracker**

A full Next.js 15 App Router application where citizens report local issues (potholes, broken streetlights) and track status. Combines: static public pages for browsing resolved/open issues (ISR), a Server Action-powered report submission form, an authenticated admin dashboard behind middleware with parallel routes (queue / map view / stats), intercepting routes for a "quick view" issue modal, edge-personalized location defaults, and a deployed, benchmarked production build on Vercel with documented caching strategy per route. Domain: civic tech. Why it matters: this is a realistic, socially useful full-stack Next.js app that exercises nearly every App Router feature in one coherent product rather than in isolation.

# **Summary: The Full Sequence at a Glance**

| Stage | Focus | Topics |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | React Foundation | Fundamentals → Lifecycle/Effects → Full Hook Set → Forms/Events → Design Patterns → Error Boundaries/Portals → Suspense |
| 2 | Next.js Core | Routing → Server/Client Components → Data Fetching/Caching → Server Actions → Route Handlers → Static Gen/ISR → Metadata/Optimization → Middleware → Fetch API |
| 3 | Advanced Routing | Route Groups → notFound() → Parallel Routes → Intercepting Routes → revalidatePath() → revalidateTag() |
| 4 | Client-Side Concerns | Browser Storage → React Router v6 |
| 5 | Data Layer | Client Data-Fetching Concepts → HydrationBoundary/dehydrate() |
| 6 | Deployment | Vercel → Edge Functions → Edge Runtime → plaiceholder → GitHub Pages |

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 03 — UI System & Styling, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 03 budget (141 core hours), the same way the Phase 01 JavaScript stage and Phase 02 both expanded beyond their official hour budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports. If you want a version that fits 141h, ask for the trimmed version; this doc is the deep one by design.**

**OVERLAP NOTICE: Zod (Topic 13 here) was already covered in Phase 01, Stage 5, Topic 66, as the synthesis of TypeScript's type system applied to runtime validation. This phase's Zod coverage is scoped differently — specifically its integration with react-hook-form — and is not a full re-teach. Treat it as a targeted extension, not a duplicate lesson.**

Every topic from Phase 03, resequenced by real dependency: vanilla CSS foundations → Tailwind → class-composition utilities → component libraries built on top of those utilities → forms/validation → general-purpose utilities → design-token/methodology topics, each expanded to the depth actually needed to use it correctly rather than just recognize its name.

# **STAGE 1 — CSS Foundations (Beyond Phase 01's CSS3 Basics)**

*Phase 01's CSS3 stage covered the box model, flexbox, grid, and responsive basics. This stage picks up where that left off — the modern CSS features that Tailwind, theming, and design-token tooling all build on.*

## **▌ 1\. CSS Custom Properties**

* Declaring custom properties (--variable-name) at :root and scoped to selectors  

* var() with fallback values  

* Custom properties vs Sass/Less variables — computed at runtime, not compile time  

* Using custom properties for theming (light/dark values swapped at a scope)  

* Cascading and inheritance behavior of custom properties  

* Custom properties combined with calc()

## **▌ 2\. CSS @layer (Cascade Layers)**

*Requires understanding the cascade/specificity from Phase 01's CSS3 stage — @layer is a tool for controlling cascade order explicitly.*

* @layer syntax — declaring and ordering layers  

* Layer precedence: later-declared layers win regardless of selector specificity  

* Unlayered styles vs layered styles (unlayered always wins)  

* Why component libraries (Tailwind, shadcn) use @layer internally to avoid specificity wars  

* Practical use: layering reset → base → components → utilities

## **▌ 3\. CSS Modules**

*Independent of custom properties/@layer — a different scoping strategy (build-time class name hashing) worth understanding before comparing it to Tailwind's utility-first approach.*

* \\\*.module.css file convention and automatic scoping  

* Composing classes (composes: keyword)  

* CSS Modules with dynamic class names in JS/JSX  

* When CSS Modules are preferable to utility-first CSS (complex one-off components, gradual migration)  

* Limitations: no true dynamic values without JS interpolation

## **▌ 4\. BEM (Block Element Modifier)**

*A naming methodology, not a tool — placed here because it's most relevant when writing vanilla CSS or CSS Modules class names, and understanding it clarifies why utility-first CSS (next stage) was a reaction against this approach.*

* Block, Element, Modifier naming convention (.block\\\_\\\_element--modifier)  

* Why BEM exists — solving specificity and naming collisions at scale  

* BEM's tradeoffs: verbosity vs predictability  

* How BEM relates to (and contrasts with) utility-first CSS philosophy

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — CSS Foundations  (7.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| CSS Custom Properties | 2 |
| CSS @layer (Cascade Layers) | 2 |
| CSS Modules | 2 |
| BEM (Block Element Modifier) | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Themeable Component Sandbox" (Easy)**

A small component gallery (buttons, cards, alerts) fully re-themeable at runtime via CSS Custom Properties, with a light/dark and a "high contrast" theme. Domain: design systems.

**"Legacy CSS Layering Refactor" (Medium)**

Take a deliberately messy, specificity-war-ridden stylesheet (provided or self-authored) and refactor it using @layer (reset, base, components, utilities, overrides) so cascade order becomes predictable and documented. Domain: engineering/refactoring.

**"BEM Design System Starter Kit" (Medium–Hard)**

A small component library authored with strict BEM naming and CSS Modules for scoping, published as a usable style guide page (a mini Storybook-style catalog page built by hand) with documented naming conventions. Domain: design systems.

**Practice Exercises**

* Convert a hardcoded color palette across 10 components into CSS Custom Properties with a single theme-switch point.  

* Reorganize an unlayered stylesheet into 4 explicit @layer buckets and prove specificity conflicts disappear.  

* Name 15 UI elements using strict BEM and get peer/self-review on 3 ambiguous cases.  

* Scope two components with clashing class names using CSS Modules and verify no leakage.

# **STAGE 2 — Tailwind CSS (Utility-First Foundation)**

*Requires the cascade/@layer understanding from Stage 1 — Tailwind is built on cascade layers internally, and its config system uses CSS custom properties for theming.*

## **▌ 5\. Tailwind CSS**

**Core Utility Model**

* Utility-first philosophy — styling via composed classes, not custom CSS  

* Spacing, sizing, color, typography utility scales  

* Responsive prefixes (sm:, md:, lg:, etc.) and mobile-first breakpoints  

* State variants (hover:, focus:, active:, disabled:, group-hover:, peer-\\\*)  

* Dark mode variant (dark:)

**Layout Utilities**

* Flexbox and Grid utility classes (mapping directly to Phase 01's CSS3 concepts)  

* Container queries (@container, if using Tailwind v4/CSS container query utilities)  

* Arbitrary value syntax (\\\[value\\\]) for one-off cases

**Configuration & Theming**

* tailwind.config.js/ts — extending vs overriding the default theme  

* Custom color palettes, spacing scales, font families  

* Integrating CSS Custom Properties (Stage 1, Topic 1\\) into the Tailwind theme for runtime-switchable values  

* @layer usage inside Tailwind (base, components, utilities layers) — direct application of Stage 1, Topic 2

**Build & Optimization**

* Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation model  

* Purging unused styles for production builds  

* @apply — extracting repeated utility combinations into custom classes (and why it's used sparingly)

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Tailwind CSS (Utility-First Foundation)  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Tailwind CSS | 8 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Pricing Page, Pixel-Faithful" (Easy)**

Recreate a real SaaS pricing page layout using only Tailwind utilities, responsive at 3 breakpoints. Domain: SaaS marketing.

**"Custom Tailwind Design Token Theme" (Medium)**

Extend Tailwind’s config with a fully custom design token set (brand colors, spacing scale, typography scale) for a fictional client brand, then rebuild 5 components using only the custom tokens (no arbitrary values). Domain: agency/freelance work.

**"Dashboard UI Kit, Utility-First" (Hard)**

A full admin dashboard UI (nav, cards, tables, forms, charts placeholders) built at production polish level using only Tailwind, with a documented approach for avoiding class-name bloat (extracted components, not @apply-everything). Domain: SaaS/enterprise tooling.

**Practice Exercises**

* Rebuild a component from Stage 1 using pure Tailwind and compare line count/readability.  

* Extend tailwind.config with 5 custom design tokens and use them across 3 components.  

* Audit a Tailwind-heavy component for unnecessary arbitrary values and refactor them into config tokens.

# **STAGE 3 — Class Composition Utilities**

*These three tools exist specifically to manage Tailwind utility classes at scale — none of them are useful without Tailwind (Stage 2\\) already in place, and each builds on the previous.*

## **▌ 6\. clsx**

*Simplest of the three — a conditional classname joiner. Entry point into this stage.*

* Conditional class application via objects and arrays  

* Replacing manual template-literal string concatenation for classNames  

* Common pattern: clsx(baseClasses, condition && conditionalClasses)

## **▌ 7\. tailwind-merge**

*Requires clsx (6) conceptually — solves a problem clsx alone doesn't: conflicting Tailwind utilities (e.g. two different padding classes) don't override each other predictably by string concatenation alone.*

* Why conflicting Tailwind classes need special merging (CSS specificity ties, not overrides)  

* twMerge() resolving conflicting utility classes by keeping the last one  

* Common pattern: combining clsx \+ tailwind-merge into a cn() helper function

## **▌ 8\. CVA (Class Variance Authority)**

*Requires clsx and tailwind-merge (6, 7\\) — CVA composes both internally to manage variant-based component styling (e.g. a Button with variant='primary'|'secondary' and size='sm'|'lg' props).*

* Defining a base class set plus variant definitions  

* variants, defaultVariants, compoundVariants configuration  

* Type-safe variant props when combined with TypeScript (VariantProps\\\<\\\>)  

* Why CVA exists — replacing manual conditional class logic for multi-axis variant components

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Class Composition Utilities  (5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| clsx | 1 |
| tailwind-merge | 1 |
| CVA (Class Variance Authority) | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Conditional Class Playground" (Easy)**

A small set of components (badge, alert, button) with multiple visual states driven entirely by clsx-based conditional logic. Domain: design systems.

**"Merge-Safe Themeable Button" (Medium)**

A Button component that accepts a className override prop and correctly merges/overrides Tailwind classes using tailwind-merge (no visual bugs from conflicting utilities), tested against 10 tricky override cases.

**"CVA-Powered Component Library Core" (Hard)**

Build 6 components (Button, Badge, Alert, Input, Card, Tag) using Class Variance Authority for variant/size/state management, with full TypeScript prop inference from the CVA config. Domain: design systems / component libraries.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a component with 4 boolean-driven visual states using clsx, no ternary soup.  

* Break tailwind-merge intentionally with a conflicting override and explain why it resolves the way it does.  

* Convert a manually-written variant Button (if/else classes) into a CVA-based one and compare maintainability.

# **STAGE 4 — Component Libraries**

*Both libraries in this stage assume Tailwind (Stage 2\\) and the class-composition utilities (Stage 3\\) are already understood — shadcn/ui in particular is built directly from both.*

## **▌ 9\. Radix UI**

*Placed before shadcn/ui because shadcn/ui's components are Radix primitives with Tailwind styling applied on top.*

* Unstyled, accessible component primitives — what "headless" UI means  

* Compound component API pattern (ties back to Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 5 — Component Design Patterns)  

* Built-in accessibility: focus management, keyboard navigation, ARIA attributes handled automatically  

* Common primitives: Dialog, DropdownMenu, Popover, Tooltip, Select, Tabs  

* Controlled vs uncontrolled usage of Radix primitives

## **▌ 10\. shadcn/ui**

*Directly depends on Radix UI (9), Tailwind (Stage 2), and CVA (Stage 3, Topic 8\\) — shadcn/ui components are literally Radix primitives styled with Tailwind and CVA, copied into your own codebase rather than installed as an opaque package.*

* The "copy, don't install" philosophy — components live in your codebase, fully editable  

* CLI usage — adding components on demand  

* Anatomy of a shadcn component: Radix primitive \+ CVA variants \+ Tailwind classes  

* Customizing theme tokens (ties back to Stage 1, Topic 1 — CSS Custom Properties, which shadcn uses for its color system)  

* Composing shadcn components into larger patterns

## **▌ 11\. next-themes**

*Requires CSS Custom Properties (Stage 1, Topic 1\\) and Tailwind's dark: variant (Stage 2\\) — next-themes is the mechanism that toggles which set of custom property values is active.*

* ThemeProvider setup in a Next.js app  

* System theme detection vs manual toggle  

* Avoiding flash-of-incorrect-theme on page load (script injection strategy)  

* useTheme hook for building a theme toggle UI

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Component Libraries  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Radix UI | 3 |
| shadcn/ui | 3 |
| next-themes | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Accessible Custom Select" (Medium)**

Build a fully accessible custom dropdown/select using Radix UI primitives (keyboard nav, ARIA, focus trapping handled correctly) styled to match a custom brand, not default Radix look. Domain: accessibility engineering.

**"shadcn/ui-Based Admin Starter" (Medium)**

Assemble a small admin panel using shadcn/ui components (customized, not default theme) integrated with next-themes for dark mode, including a documented decision on which components were customized and why. Domain: SaaS/internal tools.

**"Multi-Brand Theming Demo" (Hard)**

One codebase that can render 3 completely different visual brands (different logos, colors, radii, fonts) at runtime by swapping a theme provider — combining Radix primitives, next-themes, and CSS variables. Domain: white-label SaaS / multi-tenant products.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a Radix Dialog from scratch styled with your own CSS, verifying focus trap and Escape-to-close work.  

* Add dark mode to an existing project with next-themes, including a flash-of-wrong-theme fix.  

* Customize 3 shadcn/ui components beyond their default styling and document each change.

# **STAGE 5 — Forms & Validation**

*Forms are placed after component libraries (Stage 4\\) because real form UIs are typically built from shadcn/Radix inputs, not raw HTML elements — though the underlying form-state logic is library-agnostic.*

## **▌ 12\. react-hook-form**

**Core Concepts**

* Uncontrolled-by-default philosophy (register() vs controlled useState-per-field)  

* useForm hook — register, handleSubmit, formState  

* Why uncontrolled inputs reduce re-renders compared to a useState-per-field approach (ties back to Phase 02's performance hooks)

**Validation**

* Built-in HTML validation rules (required, min, max, pattern) via register options  

* Custom validation functions  

* Displaying field-level errors from formState.errors

**Advanced Patterns**

* Controller component — bridging RHF with controlled third-party components (e.g. Radix Select, which isn't a native input)  

* useFieldArray for dynamic field lists  

* Watching field values (watch()) vs subscribing for performance  

* Form submission state (isSubmitting, isSubmitSuccessful)

## **▌ 13\. Zod (react-hook-form Integration)**

*Zod's general type-system usage was already covered in Phase 01, Stage 5, Topic 66\. This section is scoped specifically to its role as a validation resolver for react-hook-form (12) — not a re-teach of Zod itself.*

* @hookform/resolvers/zod — connecting a Zod schema as RHF's validation source  

* Deriving TypeScript types from a Zod schema for full form type-safety (z.infer, tying back to Phase 01's type system)  

* Schema reuse between client-side form validation and server-side validation (Server Actions, ties back to Phase 02, Stage 2, Topic 11\\)

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Forms & Validation  (7 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| react-hook-form | 5 |
| Zod (react-hook-form Integration) | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Multi-Field Signup Form with Live Validation" (Easy–Medium)**

A signup form using react-hook-form with field-level and cross-field validation (password confirm), inline error messages, and disabled-until-valid submit. Domain: general SaaS.

**"Schema-Driven Dynamic Form Builder" (Hard)**

A form renderer that takes a Zod schema as input and dynamically renders the correct react-hook-form-powered fields (text, number, select, date) with validation derived entirely from the schema — no hardcoded field JSX per form. Domain: internal tooling / low-code platforms.

**Practice Exercises**

* Add async validation (e.g., "email already taken") to a react-hook-form field with proper loading/error states.  

* Build a Zod schema with a refined cross-field rule (e.g., end date after start date) and wire it into react-hook-form.  

* Handle a deeply nested form (array of objects, e.g., "add another team member") with useFieldArray.

# **STAGE 6 — General-Purpose Utilities**

*These three libraries are independent of styling and forms — placed after Stage 5 simply because they're commonly reached for while building the form/data-display logic that comes right after this phase, not because of any real dependency.*

## **▌ 14\. date-fns**

* Immutable, modular date utility functions (format, parse, addDays, differenceInDays, etc.)  

* Why date-fns over native Date manipulation for anything non-trivial  

* Tree-shaking benefit — only importing the functions actually used

## **▌ 15\. lodash-es**

* Common utilities: debounce, throttle, cloneDeep, groupBy, pick, omit  

* ES module build (lodash-es) vs CommonJS lodash — why the ES version is preferred in a modern bundler context  

* When to reach for lodash vs writing the equivalent natively (many array/object methods now exist natively)

## **▌ 16\. nanoid**

* Generating compact, URL-safe unique IDs  

* nanoid vs uuid — size and performance tradeoffs  

* Common use: client-generated temporary IDs before a record is persisted (e.g. optimistic UI list items)

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — General-Purpose Utilities  (3.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| date-fns | 1.5 |
| lodash-es | 1.5 |
| nanoid | 0.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Event Countdown & Scheduling Widget" (Easy)**

A small widget showing "time until" and formatted relative dates using date-fns, correctly handling timezones. Domain: events/scheduling.

**"Deduplicating Data Import Tool" (Medium)**

A CSV import utility that uses lodash-es for deep merging/grouping/deduplication logic on messy imported records, plus nanoid for generating stable client-side temp IDs before server save. Domain: data tooling/back-office.

**Practice Exercises**

* Format the same date 5 different ways with date-fns (relative, ISO, localized) and explain when to use each.  

* Use lodash-es groupBy and uniqBy to clean a deliberately messy dataset.  

* Explain (in writing) why nanoid was chosen over UUID or Math.random for a given use case, with tradeoffs.

# **STAGE 7 — Design Tokens & Alternative Styling Systems**

*Placed last deliberately — both topics here are either an extension of concepts already covered (Style Dictionary formalizes what CSS Custom Properties \+ theming already introduced) or a full alternative to the Tailwind approach (vanilla-extract), most meaningful as a comparison once Tailwind is already second nature.*

## **▌ 17\. Style Dictionary**

*Requires CSS Custom Properties and theming (Stage 1, Topic 1; Stage 4, Topic 11\\) — Style Dictionary formalizes design tokens into a single source of truth that can generate CSS variables, Tailwind config, and other platform outputs.*

* Design tokens as a concept — single source of truth for colors, spacing, typography values  

* Token definition format (JSON/YAML)  

* Build transforms — generating CSS custom properties, Tailwind config, or platform-specific outputs (iOS/Android) from one token set  

* Where this fits in a design-system workflow (design tool exports → tokens → code)

## **▌ 18\. vanilla-extract**

*Placed last — a zero-runtime CSS-in-JS alternative to Tailwind's utility-first approach. Most valuable as a comparison once Tailwind (Stage 2\\) is deeply understood, so the tradeoffs are meaningful rather than abstract.*

* Zero-runtime philosophy — styles compiled to static CSS at build time, not generated at runtime like traditional CSS-in-JS  

* style() function and typed style objects  

* Theming via createTheme() / createThemeContract() — contrasted with Tailwind \+ CSS Custom Properties theming (Stage 4, Topic 11\\)  

* Sprinkles (atomic CSS utilities within vanilla-extract) as a Tailwind-like layer  

* When vanilla-extract is preferable to Tailwind: component-scoped complex styles, type-safe design tokens, teams wanting CSS-in-JS ergonomics without runtime cost

### **Stage 7 Projects & Exercises — Design Tokens & Alternative Styling Systems  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Style Dictionary | 2 |
| vanilla-extract | 4 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Cross-Platform Design Token Pipeline" (Medium–Hard)**

Define design tokens once in Style Dictionary source format and generate outputs for CSS variables, a Tailwind config, and a JSON format usable by a hypothetical mobile app — demonstrating single-source-of-truth theming. Domain: design systems / platform engineering.

**"Zero-Runtime Styled Component Library" (Hard)**

Rebuild 4 components from Stage 3/4 using vanilla-extract instead of Tailwind/CVA, compare bundle output and runtime cost, and write a comparison report (DX, performance, type safety) between utility-first and zero-runtime CSS-in-TS approaches. Domain: performance engineering / design systems.

**Practice Exercises**

* Define 10 design tokens in Style Dictionary and generate both CSS variable and Tailwind config outputs from the same source.  

* Build one component in vanilla-extract with typed style variants.  

* Write a short comparison (bundle size, DX, runtime cost) between a Tailwind version and a vanilla-extract version of the same component.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Atlas" — A Production-Grade, Multi-Brand Design System**

Build a documented, installable component library (published as a local/private npm package) combining: a token pipeline (Style Dictionary) as the single source of truth, Tailwind and vanilla-extract both wired to consume those tokens, Radix-based accessible primitives styled with CVA, dark mode and multi-brand theming via next-themes/CSS variables, a schema-driven dynamic form system using react-hook-form \+ Zod, and a hand-built documentation/catalog page showing every component in every variant and theme. Domain: design systems / platform engineering. Why it matters: design systems are one of the highest-leverage, most portfolio-impressive artifacts a front-end engineer can build — this project proves you can architect one, not just consume one.

*Band: Core   ·   Track: Frontend Core   ·   Realistic hours: 153h (header said 169h)*

| 4 WEEKS · 169 HOURS · MONTH 4 |

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 04 — State, Data Fetching & Advanced React, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 04 budget (169 core hours), the same way Phases 01–03 deep versions expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**OVERLAP NOTICE: Four topics here were already touched in Phase 02's deep React stage — React Context (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 3), React.lazy and Suspense (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 7), and createPortal (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 6). Each is scoped narrowly below to what's genuinely new at this phase (Context's limits as a segue into external stores; Suspense for data-driven loading with TanStack Query; createPortal in real modal/toast implementations) rather than re-taught from scratch.**

Every topic from Phase 04, resequenced by real dependency: client state (Context's limits → Zustand) → server state (TanStack Query → tRPC, since tRPC's hooks are built on Query) → state machines (XState → Stately Studio) → rendering/resilience primitives (lazy/Suspense/error boundaries/portals, now applied at real-project depth) → browser observation APIs → virtualization and profiling as the capstone, since both require a real, populated app to be meaningful.

# **STAGE 1 — Client State: Context's Limits, Then Zustand**

*React Context was introduced generally in Phase 02\. This stage starts from where Context breaks down, then introduces the tool that fixes it.*

## **▌ 1\. React Context — Revisited: Where It Breaks Down**

*Not a re-teach of Context mechanics (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 3\\) — this section is specifically about Context's failure modes at scale, which motivates Zustand.*

* Recap: Provider/Consumer, useContext  

* The re-render problem: every consumer re-renders on any context value change, even unrelated fields  

* Splitting contexts to limit blast radius — and why this gets unwieldy with many independent pieces of state  

* Context is not a state manager — it's a dependency-injection mechanism; conflating the two causes the scaling problem  

* When Context alone is still the right tool (rarely-changing values: theme, auth session, locale)

## **▌ 2\. Zustand**

*Directly motivated by Topic 1 — Zustand solves Context's re-render problem via selective subscriptions.*

**Core API**

* create() — defining a store as a hook  

* Reading state via selectors (subscribing to only the slice you need)  

* Updating state — set() and functional updates  

* Why Zustand doesn't cause the Context re-render problem: components only re-render when their selected slice changes

**Patterns**

* Store slicing — splitting a large store into composable slices  

* Middleware: persist (localStorage sync, ties back to Phase 02, Stage 4, Topic 23), devtools, immer middleware for mutable-style updates  

* Actions colocated with state vs separate action creators  

* Combining Zustand stores with Context for dependency injection (e.g. multiple instances of the same store shape)

**When to Reach for Zustand vs Context vs Server State (Stage 2\\)**

* Client-only UI state (modals open/closed, form wizard step) → Zustand or local state  

* Rarely-changing app-wide values → Context  

* Anything that originates from the server → belongs in Stage 2, not Zustand (the most common state-management mistake)

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Client State: Context's Limits, Then Zustand  (7 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| React Context — Revisited: Where It Breaks Down | 2 |
| Zustand | 5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Context Meltdown Demo" (Easy)**

Deliberately build a deeply nested component tree relying only on Context for frequently-changing state, profile the re-render cascade with React DevTools, then document exactly where and why it breaks down. Domain: educational / performance engineering.

**"Multi-Store Zustand Task Manager" (Medium)**

A task/project manager using multiple focused Zustand stores (not one giant store), with selectors to prevent unnecessary re-renders, persisted to localStorage via Zustand middleware. Domain: productivity tools.

**"Undo/Redo Design Tool State Engine" (Hard)**

The state layer for a simple design/whiteboard tool (shapes, position, color) using Zustand with a custom undo/redo middleware (command history), demonstrating advanced store composition. Domain: creative tools.

**Practice Exercises**

* Profile a Context-based counter nested 10 components deep and count unnecessary re-renders; then fix it with memoization and explain the limits of that fix.  

* Split one monolithic Zustand store into 3 focused stores and update all consumers.  

* Add a persist middleware to a Zustand store and handle a schema-migration scenario (old shape to new shape).

# **STAGE 2 — Server State: TanStack Query, Then tRPC**

*Server state (data that lives on a server and is merely cached on the client) is a fundamentally different problem from client state (Stage 1\\) — it needs caching, revalidation, and request deduplication, which is what this stage's tools provide.*

## **▌ 3\. TanStack Query v5**

*Builds directly on the HydrationBoundary/dehydrate() concepts already covered in Phase 02, Stage 5 — this is the full treatment of the library that mechanism serves.*

**Core Concepts**

* Server state vs client state — why they need different tools  

* Query keys — structuring them for correct caching and invalidation  

* useQuery — status states (pending, error, success), data/error/isLoading  

* staleTime vs gcTime (formerly cacheTime) — the two most misunderstood options

**Mutations**

* useMutation — mutate/mutateAsync, onSuccess/onError/onSettled callbacks  

* Invalidating queries after a mutation (queryClient.invalidateQueries)  

* Optimistic updates — updating the cache before the server confirms, with rollback on error

**Advanced Patterns**

* Dependent queries (enabled option)  

* Pagination: offset-based vs cursor-based, keepPreviousData/placeholderData  

* Infinite queries (useInfiniteQuery) — foundation for Stage 6's virtualization use case  

* Prefetching (queryClient.prefetchQuery) — connects back to Phase 02's Server Component \+ HydrationBoundary pattern  

* Query invalidation strategies at scale (broad vs targeted invalidation)

**DevTools**

* React Query DevTools — inspecting cache state, query status, stale/fresh boundaries

## **▌ 4\. tRPC v11**

*Placed after TanStack Query (3), not before — tRPC's React hooks (@trpc/react-query) are built directly on top of TanStack Query, so understanding Query first means tRPC's client API requires learning almost nothing new mechanically, only the type-safety layer on top.*

**Core Concepts**

* End-to-end type safety — types flow from server router to client without code generation  

* Defining a router: procedures (query vs mutation), input validation (typically Zod, ties back to Phase 01/03)  

* Context (tRPC's server-side request context, not to be confused with React Context from Stage 1\\) — auth, DB client injection

**Client Integration**

* @trpc/react-query — tRPC's hooks are TanStack Query hooks with typed inputs/outputs  

* trpc.useQuery / trpc.useMutation — same mental model as Topic 3, now fully typed  

* Batching requests automatically (httpBatchLink)

**Advanced Patterns**

* Middleware — auth checks, logging, applied per-procedure or router-wide  

* Nested routers — organizing large APIs  

* Error handling — TRPCError and typed error codes  

* Server-side calling (calling a tRPC procedure directly from a Server Component, without an HTTP round-trip)

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Server State: TanStack Query, Then tRPC  (15 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| TanStack Query v5 | 8 |
| tRPC v11 | 7 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Paginated \+ Infinite Product Catalog" (Medium)**

A product listing using TanStack Query with both classic pagination and infinite scroll variants, optimistic updates for "add to wishlist," and proper cache invalidation on mutation. Domain: e-commerce.

**"End-to-End Type-Safe Todo API with tRPC" (Medium–Hard)**

A full-stack todo app where the client calls the backend via tRPC with zero manual type duplication — renaming a backend field breaks the frontend build immediately, proving end-to-end type safety. Domain: full-stack tooling demonstration.

**"Real-Time-Feeling Dashboard via Query Polling \+ Optimistic UI" (Hard)**

A support-ticket dashboard where status changes feel instant via optimistic updates, with automatic rollback on server rejection, background refetching, and stale-time tuning documented per query type. Domain: customer support / SaaS.

**Practice Exercises**

* Implement optimistic updates for a "like" button including correct rollback on simulated server failure.  

* Configure staleTime and cacheTime differently for 3 different query types and justify each choice in writing.  

* Build a tRPC router with input validation (Zod) and trace a type error from a backend rename all the way to a frontend compile failure.

# **STAGE 3 — State Machines**

*Conceptually independent of Stages 1–2, but placed after both because state machines are usually reached for once you've felt the pain of complex state logic that plain useState/Zustand/useReducer can't cleanly express — that pain is easier to appreciate with Stage 1–2 experience already in hand.*

## **▌ 5\. XState v5**

**Core Concepts**

* Finite state machines — states, events, transitions  

* Why explicit state machines prevent "impossible states" that boolean-flag-based state allows (e.g. isLoading && isError simultaneously)  

* createMachine — defining states and transitions declaratively  

* Actions (side effects on transition), guards (conditional transitions)

**Advanced Machine Features**

* Context (XState's own typed extended state, not to be confused with React Context from Stage 1 or tRPC Context from Stage 2\\)  

* Hierarchical (nested) states  

* Parallel states  

* Actors model — spawning and communicating between machines (XState v5's actor-based architecture)

**React Integration**

* useMachine / useActor hooks (@xstate/react)  

* Deciding when a machine belongs in a component vs a Zustand store (Stage 1\\) vs plain useReducer

## **▌ 6\. Stately Studio**

*Directly depends on XState (5) — Stately Studio is a visual editor/inspector for XState machines, not a standalone tool.*

* Visualizing machines defined in code  

* Editing machines visually and syncing back to code  

* Using it for design/engineering collaboration on complex flows

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — State Machines  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| XState v5 | 6 |
| Stately Studio | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Traffic Light & Turnstile State Machines" (Easy)**

Two classic finite state machines implemented in XState v5 to build fluency with the model before tackling something real. Domain: educational / systems modeling.

**"Multi-Step Checkout State Machine" (Medium–Hard)**

A checkout flow (cart → shipping → payment → confirm) modeled explicitly as a state machine with guards (e.g., can’t proceed to payment without valid address) and visualized in Stately Studio, catching impossible states that a boolean-flag approach would allow. Domain: e-commerce.

**"Video Upload Pipeline State Machine" (Hard)**

Model a video upload/processing pipeline (idle → uploading → processing → ready/failed, with retry and cancel) as a state machine with parallel states for "upload progress" and "processing progress" tracked independently. Domain: media platforms.

**Practice Exercises**

* Model a traffic light as an XState machine and add a pedestrian-crossing parallel state.  

* Diagram a real UI flow from a previous project as a state chart in Stately Studio before implementing it.  

* Add a guard condition to a machine that prevents an invalid transition, and write a test proving the invalid transition is blocked.

# **STAGE 4 — Rendering & Resilience Primitives (Applied Depth)**

*React.lazy, Suspense, and createPortal were already introduced at a conceptual level in Phase 02, Stage 1\. This stage doesn't re-teach the mechanics — it applies them at real-project depth, now that Stage 2's data-fetching layer exists to make Suspense-for-data meaningful.*

## **▌ 7\. React.lazy — Applied**

*Mechanically covered already (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 7). Here: real code-splitting decisions.*

* Route-level vs component-level code splitting decisions  

* Combining with Suspense fallback UI (Topic 8\\) for a real loading experience  

* Measuring the actual bundle-size impact of a lazy boundary

## **▌ 8\. Suspense — For Data**

*Phase 02 covered Suspense for lazy-loaded components and previewed Suspense-for-data conceptually. Now that TanStack Query (Stage 2\\) exists, this is the full data-driven treatment.*

* useSuspenseQuery — TanStack Query's Suspense-integrated query hook  

* Suspense boundaries around data-dependent UI instead of manual isLoading checks  

* Waterfalls — why naive nested Suspense boundaries can serialize requests, and how to prevent it (parallel fetching, prefetching)  

* Combining with Error Boundaries (Topic 9\\) for the full loading/error/success triad

## **▌ 9\. react-error-boundary**

*Phase 02 covered hand-written error boundary classes. This is the library that replaces that boilerplate — a natural upgrade once the manual version is understood.*

* FallbackComponent / fallbackRender props  

* resetErrorBoundary — retry logic without a full page reload  

* Combining with TanStack Query's error handling (Stage 2\\) for a full data-fetching error UI  

* Placement strategy: one boundary per independently-failing UI region, not one global boundary

## **▌ 10\. ReactDOM.createPortal — Applied**

*Mechanically covered already (Phase 02, Stage 1, Topic 6). Here: real modal/toast system implementation, now that Zustand (Stage 1\\) exists to manage which portals are open.*

* Building a real modal system: portal target, focus trapping, scroll locking  

* Toast/notification system architecture using a Zustand store (Stage 1\\) to queue and dismiss toasts  

* Accessibility requirements for portal content (focus management, aria-modal, returning focus on close)

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Rendering & Resilience Primitives (Applied Depth)  (6.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| React.lazy — Applied | 1.5 |
| Suspense — For Data | 2 |
| react-error-boundary | 1.5 |
| ReactDOM.createPortal — Applied | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Route-Level Code Splitting Audit" (Medium)**

Take a multi-page app and apply React.lazy \+ Suspense at route boundaries, measuring bundle size and load time before/after with real numbers. Domain: performance engineering.

**"Resilient Widget Dashboard" (Medium–Hard)**

A dashboard of independent widgets where each widget has its own Suspense boundary and its own react-error-boundary fallback, so one failing widget (simulated) never crashes the whole page — with a portal-based "widget detail" modal on click.

**Practice Exercises**

* Lazy-load 3 heavy components and measure the bundle size difference with a bundle analyzer.  

* Force an error inside one of several sibling components and prove only that component’s error boundary catches it.  

* Build a data-fetching component wrapped in Suspense and simulate a slow network to observe the fallback correctly.

# **STAGE 5 — Browser Observation APIs**

*Independent of the state/data/rendering stages above — these are browser-native APIs for observing layout and viewport changes, placed here because their most common React use case (infinite scroll, responsive components) is more meaningful with the data-fetching layer (Stage 2\\) already available.*

## **▌ 11\. Intersection Observer**

* Observing when an element enters/exits the viewport  

* Threshold and rootMargin configuration  

* Common use: infinite scroll — triggering useInfiniteQuery's fetchNextPage (Stage 2\\) when a sentinel element becomes visible  

* Common use: lazy-loading images/components on scroll into view  

* Wrapping in a custom hook (useIntersectionObserver) — ties back to Phase 02's custom hook patterns

## **▌ 12\. ResizeObserver**

*Same API family as Intersection Observer (11) — observes size instead of visibility.*

* Observing element size changes without window resize listeners  

* Common use: responsive components that adapt to container size, not just viewport size (container queries' JS-based precursor)  

* Performance considerations — avoiding observer callback loops

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Browser Observation APIs  (4.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Intersection Observer | 2.5 |
| ResizeObserver | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Scroll-Triggered Storytelling Page" (Medium)**

A scrollytelling landing page (like a data-journalism piece) where sections animate/reveal using Intersection Observer as the user scrolls. Domain: media/journalism.

**"Responsive Chart Container System" (Medium)**

A set of chart containers that resize their internal SVG/canvas rendering correctly using ResizeObserver, avoiding the common bug of charts not resizing on sidebar collapse. Domain: analytics/dashboards.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a "reading progress" indicator using Intersection Observer on section headings.  

* Fix a chart that fails to resize when its container changes size (e.g., sidebar toggle) using ResizeObserver.

# **STAGE 6 — Virtualization & Profiling (Capstone)**

*Both topics require a real, data-populated, multi-feature app to be meaningful — placed last because they're applied to everything built in Stages 1–5, not new concepts in isolation.*

## **▌ 13\. TanStack Virtual**

*Requires Intersection Observer concepts (11) as a mental model, and TanStack Query's infinite queries (Stage 2, Topic 3\\) as the typical data source for a virtualized list of unknown/large length.*

* Why virtualization exists — rendering only visible rows for large lists instead of the entire dataset  

* useVirtualizer — core API, item size estimation (fixed vs dynamic)  

* Combining with useInfiniteQuery (Stage 2\\) for infinite-scroll virtualized lists  

* Overscan configuration — rendering a buffer beyond the visible area for smoother scrolling

## **▌ 14\. React DevTools Profiler**

*Placed last deliberately — profiling is most valuable once there's a real app with the patterns from every prior stage (Context, Zustand, Query, Suspense, virtualization) to actually measure and compare.*

* Recording a profiling session  

* Flamegraph vs ranked chart views  

* Identifying unnecessary re-renders — tying back to Phase 02's memoization concepts (React.memo, useMemo, useCallback)  

* Committed vs rendered — understanding what the Profiler actually measures  

* Using profiling data to decide where memoization, Zustand selector-splitting (Stage 1), or virtualization (Topic 13\\) would actually help, vs premature optimization

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — Virtualization & Profiling (Capstone)  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| TanStack Virtual | 3 |
| React DevTools Profiler | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"100,000-Row Virtualized Table" (Hard)**

A data table that renders 100,000 rows smoothly using TanStack Virtual, with sorting/filtering that stays performant, profiled and documented with React DevTools Profiler flame charts before/after virtualization. Domain: enterprise data tools / fintech dashboards.

**"Chat App Virtualized Message List" (Hard)**

A chat interface with a virtualized, variable-height message list (some messages have images, some are one line) that maintains correct scroll position when new messages arrive at the bottom. Domain: messaging/social.

**Practice Exercises**

* Render 10,000 unvirtualized rows, profile the frame rate, then virtualize and profile again — document the numeric difference.  

* Handle variable-height virtualized items correctly (not all rows are the same height).  

* Use React DevTools Profiler to find and eliminate the single most expensive re-render in one of your Phase 2–4 projects.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Pulse" — A Real-Time Support Ticket Dashboard**

A support-ticket triage dashboard combining every Phase 4 concept: Zustand for UI-local state (filters, selected ticket), TanStack Query \+ tRPC for fully type-safe server state with optimistic status updates and rollback, an XState machine governing the ticket lifecycle (new → assigned → in-progress → resolved → reopened) with guards preventing invalid transitions, Suspense \+ error boundaries per panel so one broken widget never takes down the dashboard, Intersection/ResizeObserver-driven responsive charts, and a virtualized ticket list handling 50,000+ rows without jank, profiled and documented. Domain: customer support / SaaS operations tooling. Why it matters: this is a realistic "senior front-end engineer" artifact — the kind of dashboard that separates people who know individual libraries from people who can architect a resilient, performant system out of them.

# **Summary: The Full Sequence at a Glance**

| Stage | Focus | Topics |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | Client State | React Context (limits) → Zustand |
| 2 | Server State | TanStack Query v5 → tRPC v11 |
| 3 | State Machines | XState v5 → Stately Studio |
| 4 | Rendering & Resilience (Applied) | React.lazy → Suspense (data) → react-error-boundary → createPortal (applied) |
| 5 | Browser Observation APIs | Intersection Observer → ResizeObserver |
| 6 | Virtualization & Profiling | TanStack Virtual → React DevTools Profiler |
