# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 05 — Backend \+ Database \+ Auth, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 05 budget (229 core hours), the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–04 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**ORDERING CORRECTION: The official topic list places HMAC-SHA256 (Topic 21\\) after JWT (Topic 17). This is backwards for real dependency order — HS256, one of JWT's standard signing algorithms, is HMAC-SHA256 applied to the token. Teaching JWT before HMAC means teaching "the token is signed" without the mechanism, then circling back. HMAC-SHA256 is moved ahead of JWT below, the same correction applied to Type Narrowing → Discriminated Unions in Phase 01\.**

Every topic from Phase 05, resequenced by real dependency: server runtime → framework → API design → middleware/hardening → API testing → database → ORM → hosted Postgres platforms → query performance → password/token cryptography → JWT auth → cookie-based token storage. Six weeks of official schedule, expanded here to genuine working depth at each step.

# **STAGE 1 — Server Runtime & Framework**

*Everything in this phase runs on top of Node.js and, for routing, Express — both must be solid before anything else here makes sense.*

## **▌ 1\. Node.js**

**Runtime Fundamentals**

* The event loop in a server context (contrasted with the browser event loop from Phase 01\\)  

* CommonJS vs ES Modules in Node — require vs import, package.json "type" field  

* Global objects: process, \\\_\\\_dirname/\\\_\\\_filename (or import.meta.url in ESM), Buffer  

* Module system: built-in modules (fs, path, http, crypto, os)

**File System & Streams**

* fs module — sync vs async vs promise-based APIs  

* Reading/writing files, directories  

* Streams — readable, writable, duplex, transform  

* Piping streams; why streams matter for large files/responses

**The http Module (Before Express)**

* Creating a raw HTTP server with http.createServer  

* Request/response objects at the Node level  

* Why frameworks like Express exist — what raw Node http makes tedious (routing, body parsing, middleware chaining)

**Process & Environment**

* Environment variables in Node (process.env), dotenv pattern  

* Process signals and graceful shutdown (SIGTERM, SIGINT)  

* Child processes (conceptual — spawn, exec)  

* Error handling: uncaughtException, unhandledRejection

**Package Management (Node Context)**

* npm/package.json recap in a backend context — engines field, scripts for dev/build/start  

* Dependency vs devDependency distinctions relevant to backend (nodemon, ts-node as dev deps)

## **▌ 2\. Express.js**

*Requires Node's http model (Topic 1\\) to appreciate what Express abstracts away.*

**Core Routing**

* app.get/post/put/patch/delete — route handlers  

* Route parameters (:id) and query strings  

* Request and Response objects — req.body, req.params, req.query, res.json/send/status

**Middleware**

* Middleware function signature (req, res, next)  

* Application-level vs router-level vs error-handling middleware  

* Built-in middleware: express.json(), express.urlencoded()  

* Middleware execution order — why it matters

**Routers & Structure**

* express.Router() for modular route files  

* Structuring a real backend: routes/controllers/services layering  

* Route-level middleware chains

**Error Handling**

* Express's default error handler  

* Custom error-handling middleware (4-arg signature)  

* Synchronous vs asynchronous error propagation — the gap that motivates Topic 4

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Server Runtime & Framework  (17 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Node.js | 10 |
| Express.js | 7 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Raw Node HTTP File Server" (Easy)**

A file server built with zero frameworks using only the Node.js http module — routing, static file serving, and JSON responses hand-rolled — to understand what Express actually abstracts away. Domain: systems/infra fundamentals.

**"Bookstore REST API with Express" (Medium)**

A clean Express-based REST API (CRUD for books, authors, categories) with proper middleware layering, route organization, and centralized error handling. Domain: retail/inventory.

**"Pluggable Middleware Pipeline Framework" (Hard)**

Build a tiny Express-compatible middleware pipeline from scratch (your own mini-framework mimicking app.use()/next()) to fully understand the middleware pattern, then reimplement one real feature (logging) both in your framework and in real Express side-by-side. Domain: framework/systems engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a route handler using only Node’s http module that parses a JSON body without any body-parsing library.  

* Write custom Express middleware that logs request duration and attaches a request ID to every request.  

* Recreate Express’s next()-based middleware chaining from scratch in under 40 lines.

# **STAGE 2 — API Design & Hardening**

*Requires Express (Stage 1\\) — REST conventions are applied through Express routes, and every hardening middleware in this stage attaches to that same Express app.*

## **▌ 3\. REST APIs**

**REST Principles**

* Resources and URIs — nouns, not verbs, in endpoint paths  

* HTTP methods mapped to CRUD operations  

* Statelessness — why REST APIs shouldn't rely on server-side session state (motivates token-based auth in Stage 5\\)  

* HATEOAS (concept, rarely fully implemented — worth knowing why)

**API Design Practice**

* Status codes — using them correctly (200 vs 201 vs 204, 400 vs 401 vs 403 vs 404 vs 422, 500\\)  

* Request/response body design — consistent envelope shapes, pagination metadata  

* Versioning strategies (URL path, header-based)  

* Idempotency — which methods must be idempotent and why (PUT/DELETE vs POST)

**Validation**

* Input validation at the API boundary — connects back to Zod (Phase 01/03)  

* Validating request bodies, params, and query strings before they reach business logic

## **▌ 4\. express-async-errors**

*Directly addresses the gap flagged at the end of Stage 1's Express error-handling section — async route handlers that throw don't automatically reach Express's error middleware without this.*

* The problem: unhandled promise rejections in async route handlers bypass Express's error handler by default  

* How express-async-errors patches this (or the manual try/catch-and-next(err) alternative, for understanding what it automates)

## **▌ 5\. CORS**

*Requires Express middleware model (Stage 1, Topic 2\\) — CORS is applied as middleware.*

* Same-Origin Policy recap (from Phase 02's security stage) and why CORS exists to relax it selectively  

* Configuring the cors middleware — origin, credentials, allowed methods/headers  

* CORS and cookies (credentials: true) — foreshadows httpOnly cookie auth in Stage 5

## **▌ 6\. Helmet.js**

* Setting secure HTTP headers by default (CSP, X-Frame-Options, HSTS, etc.)  

* Which headers matter most and why  

* Customizing Helmet's defaults for an app's specific needs

## **▌ 7\. express-rate-limit**

*Requires Express middleware (Stage 1\\) — applied per-route or app-wide to prevent abuse.*

* Configuring windowMs and max request limits  

* Per-route vs global rate limiting  

* Rate limiting login endpoints specifically — foreshadows brute-force protection relevant to Stage 5's auth work

## **▌ 8\. DOMPurify (Server-Side Sanitization)**

*Requires request body handling (Stage 1, Topic 2\\) — sanitizing user-submitted content before storage or before it's ever rendered as HTML.*

* Sanitizing user-generated HTML/rich text input server-side before persistence  

* XSS prevention at the data layer, complementing Helmet's header-based protections (Topic 6\\)  

* When sanitization belongs server-side vs client-side (defense in depth — both, not either/or)

## **▌ 9\. compression**

* gzip/brotli response compression middleware  

* Tradeoffs: CPU cost of compression vs bandwidth savings  

* When to skip compression (already-compressed assets, small responses)

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — API Design & Hardening  (15 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| REST APIs | 6 |
| express-async-errors | 1 |
| CORS | 2 |
| Helmet.js | 1.5 |
| express-rate-limit | 2 |
| DOMPurify (Server-Side Sanitization) | 1.5 |
| compression | 1 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Public API with Rate Limiting & CORS Policy" (Medium)**

A small public-facing API (e.g., a quotes/jokes API) with a documented CORS policy, express-rate-limit tuned per endpoint tier (free vs authenticated), and async error handling via express-async-errors so no unhandled rejection crashes the server. Domain: public API products.

**"User-Generated Content Sanitization Pipeline" (Medium–Hard)**

A comment/review submission API where all HTML input is sanitized server-side with DOMPurify (server-side variant) before storage, with a deliberate XSS payload test suite proving the sanitization actually works. Domain: content platforms / trust & safety.

**"Hardened Production API Checklist Implementation" (Hard)**

Take an existing API and harden it end-to-end: Helmet.js security headers, response compression, tuned CORS, rate limiting by IP and by API key, and a written security checklist with before/after header comparison (via curl \-I). Domain: security engineering / SRE.

**Practice Exercises**

* Configure CORS to allow exactly 2 specific origins and prove a 3rd origin is blocked.  

* Send a deliberate XSS payload through an unsanitized field, confirm the exploit, then fix it with DOMPurify and reconfirm it’s blocked.  

* Compare response sizes and timing with and without compression middleware on a large JSON payload.  

* Configure express-rate-limit with different limits for authenticated vs anonymous requests.

# **STAGE 3 — Testing the API**

## **▌ 10\. Supertest**

*Requires a real Express API with routes and middleware (Stages 1–2) — there's nothing to test until then.*

* HTTP assertion testing — making real requests against an Express app in tests  

* Integration with a test runner (Jest/Vitest, from Phase 01's testing stage)  

* Testing status codes, response bodies, headers  

* Testing middleware behavior — rate limits, CORS rejections, validation errors  

* Setting up a test database or mocking the DB layer (bridges to Stage 4\\)

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Testing the API  (3 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Supertest | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Full Supertest Suite for the Bookstore API" (Medium)**

A comprehensive Supertest test suite for the Stage 1 Bookstore API covering happy paths, validation failures, auth-protected routes, and edge cases (empty body, malformed JSON, huge payloads). Domain: QA engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a Supertest test that verifies a 401 is returned for a protected route with no token.  

* Write a test that verifies pagination metadata (total count, page, limit) is correct for a seeded dataset.  

* Simulate a database error and verify the API returns a proper 500 with no leaked stack trace in the response body.

# **STAGE 4 — Database Layer**

*Independent of Express/Node conceptually, but placed here because a real backend needs persistence, and the API/testing layer built in Stages 1–3 is what will consume this data.*

## **▌ 11\. PostgreSQL**

*The largest single topic in this phase — genuine database fundamentals, not just SQL syntax (which Phase 01 already covered at a basic level).*

**Beyond Phase 01's SQL Basics**

* Recap: Phase 01 covered basic SELECT/JOIN/GROUP BY — this goes into PostgreSQL specifically

**Data Types & Schema Design**

* PostgreSQL-specific types: UUID, JSONB, ARRAY, ENUM  

* Normalization — 1NF/2NF/3NF in practice, and when to deliberately denormalize  

* Constraints: foreign keys, unique, check constraints in depth  

* Indexes — B-tree by default, when to add one, composite indexes

**Transactions**

* BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK in practice  

* ACID properties applied concretely  

* Isolation levels (read committed, repeatable read, serializable) and what anomalies each prevents  

* Row-level locking, deadlocks

**Advanced Querying**

* Window functions (ROW\\\_NUMBER, RANK, PARTITION BY)  

* Common Table Expressions (WITH clauses), recursive CTEs  

* Subqueries vs joins vs CTEs — when each is clearer or faster

**JSONB**

* Storing and querying semi-structured data in a relational database  

* JSONB operators and indexing (GIN indexes)  

* When JSONB is appropriate vs a proper relational schema

## **▌ 12\. Prisma**

*Requires PostgreSQL fundamentals (11) — an ORM only makes the SQL you already understand more convenient; learning it first would hide what's actually happening.*

**Schema & Migrations**

* schema.prisma — models, fields, relations  

* prisma migrate dev / deploy workflow  

* Migration history and rollback considerations

**Prisma Client**

* CRUD operations via the generated client  

* Relations — include vs select, nested writes/reads  

* Type safety — how Prisma generates types from the schema (ties to Phase 01's TypeScript stage)

**Advanced Prisma**

* Transactions in Prisma ($transaction)  

* Raw queries when the query builder isn't enough ($queryRaw)  

* Middleware/extensions for cross-cutting concerns (soft deletes, audit logs)  

* N+1 query problems and how Prisma's include avoids or causes them depending on usage

## **▌ 13\. Supabase**

*Requires PostgreSQL fundamentals (11) — Supabase is hosted Postgres plus additional services layered on top.*

* Supabase as hosted PostgreSQL \+ auth \+ storage \+ realtime, in one platform  

* Row Level Security (RLS) policies — PostgreSQL-native access control enforced at the database layer  

* Supabase's client libraries vs going through Prisma/Express directly  

* When Supabase's built-in auth/storage make sense vs building your own with Stage 5's tools

## **▌ 14\. Neon**

*Same family as Supabase (13) — another hosted Postgres platform, placed immediately after for direct comparison.*

* Serverless Postgres — branching databases like Git branches  

* Connection pooling considerations for serverless/edge environments (ties to Phase 02's Edge Runtime)  

* Neon vs Supabase — when to choose one over the other (Neon: pure Postgres hosting; Supabase: Postgres \+ platform features)

## **▌ 15\. EXPLAIN ANALYZE**

*Requires real queries to analyze — needs PostgreSQL (11) and ideally Prisma (12) or raw SQL usage already in place, since this topic is about diagnosing queries you've already written.*

* Reading query plans — seq scan vs index scan vs index-only scan  

* Understanding cost estimates vs actual execution time (the ANALYZE part)  

* Identifying missing indexes from a plan  

* Common fixes: adding an index, rewriting a query, denormalizing a hot path

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Database Layer  (26.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| PostgreSQL | 12 |
| Prisma | 7 |
| Supabase | 3 |
| Neon | 1.5 |
| EXPLAIN ANALYZE | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Normalized Multi-Tenant Schema Design" (Medium)**

Design and implement (via Prisma migrations) a normalized PostgreSQL schema for a multi-tenant SaaS (organizations, users, projects, roles) with correct foreign keys and cascade rules. Domain: B2B SaaS.

**"Serverless Postgres Comparison Project" (Medium)**

Deploy the same schema/API to both Supabase and Neon, documenting connection pooling differences, cold-start behavior, and pricing/scaling tradeoffs from hands-on testing. Domain: infrastructure decision-making.

**"Slow Query Detective Agency" (Hard)**

Given a seeded database with 1M+ rows and a set of deliberately slow endpoints, use EXPLAIN ANALYZE to diagnose each bottleneck, apply the correct fix (index, query restructure, N+1 fix via Prisma include), and document before/after query plans and timings. Domain: database performance engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Design a schema for a multi-tenant app and justify every foreign key’s ON DELETE behavior.  

* Reproduce and fix a classic Prisma N+1 query problem, proving the fix with query-count logging.  

* Run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on a slow query, add an index, rerun, and document the plan change and speedup.  

* Migrate a schema change (adding a non-nullable column to a populated table) without downtime, documenting the multi-step migration strategy.

# **STAGE 5 — Authentication & Token Security**

*Requires the database layer (Stage 4\\) — auth needs somewhere to store users and their credentials. Internally reordered from the official list: HMAC-SHA256 moved ahead of JWT, since HS256 (HMAC-SHA256) is one of JWT's standard signing algorithms — teaching JWT first would mean explaining "the token is signed" without the mechanism underneath it.*

## **▌ 16\. bcrypt**

* Why passwords are never stored in plaintext or with reversible encryption  

* Hashing vs encryption — a critical distinction  

* Salt — why bcrypt generates one automatically per hash  

* Cost factor (rounds) — the time/security tradeoff  

* Hashing on signup, comparing on login (bcrypt.compare)

## **▌ 17\. HMAC-SHA256**

*Moved ahead of JWT (18) from the official ordering — this is the cryptographic primitive JWT's HS256 algorithm is built on.*

* Hash functions recap (SHA-256) vs keyed hash functions (HMAC)  

* Why a shared secret key changes a hash function into a message authentication code  

* HMAC's role: proving a message wasn't tampered with and came from someone holding the secret  

* Where this shows up directly in JWT's HS256 signing (Topic 18\\)

## **▌ 18\. JWT (JSON Web Tokens)**

*Requires bcrypt (16) for the login flow that precedes token issuance, and HMAC-SHA256 (17) to understand what HS256 signing actually does.*

**Structure & Signing**

* Header.Payload.Signature structure  

* Claims — registered (exp, iat, sub) vs custom claims  

* Signing algorithms — HS256 (HMAC-SHA256, Topic 17\\) vs RS256 (asymmetric, public/private key pairs)  

* Why the signature prevents tampering but does NOT encrypt the payload (JWTs are readable, not secret)

**Auth Flow**

* Login → verify password (bcrypt) → issue JWT  

* Access tokens vs refresh tokens — short-lived vs long-lived, and why both exist  

* Token verification middleware in Express (Stage 1\\)  

* Token expiry and refresh flow implementation

**Security Considerations**

* Where NOT to store JWTs (localStorage — vulnerable to XSS) — motivates httpOnly cookies (Topic 20\\)  

* Token revocation problem — JWTs are stateless, so revoking one before expiry requires extra infrastructure (blocklists, short expiry \+ refresh rotation)

## **▌ 19\. jose**

*Requires JWT concepts (18) — jose is a modern, standards-compliant implementation library, relevant here specifically because it's Edge-Runtime compatible (ties to Phase 02's Middleware/Edge Runtime, unlike some older JWT libraries that depend on Node-only crypto APIs).*

* Why jose over jsonwebtoken for Edge-compatible code (Middleware, Edge Functions)  

* Signing and verifying tokens with jose's API  

* Key generation and management with jose

## **▌ 20\. httpOnly Cookies**

*Requires JWT (18) — this is where the token actually gets stored, addressing the localStorage/XSS problem flagged in Topic 18's security considerations.*

* Setting cookies with the httpOnly flag — inaccessible to JavaScript, mitigating XSS-based token theft  

* Secure flag — cookie only sent over HTTPS  

* Cookie-based token flow in Express (res.cookie, reading from req.cookies)  

* CSRF implications of cookie-based auth — sets up SameSite (Topic 21\\) as part of the mitigation

## **▌ 21\. SameSite=Lax**

*Requires httpOnly cookies (20) — SameSite is a cookie attribute, meaningless without a cookie-based auth flow already in place.*

* SameSite=Strict vs Lax vs None — what cross-site request scenarios each allows or blocks  

* Why Lax is the practical default for auth cookies (allows top-level navigation, blocks cross-site form/fetch requests)  

* SameSite as CSRF mitigation, completing the cookie security picture started in Topic 20

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Authentication & Token Security  (16 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| bcrypt | 2 |
| HMAC-SHA256 | 2.5 |
| JWT (JSON Web Tokens) | 6 |
| jose | 2 |
| httpOnly Cookies | 2 |
| SameSite=Lax | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"From-Scratch Password Auth" (Medium)**

A signup/login system built without an auth-as-a-service library: bcrypt for hashing, JWT access tokens, httpOnly refresh-token cookies with SameSite=Lax, and a documented token-rotation strategy. Domain: security engineering / any product needing custom auth.

**"Webhook Signature Verification Service" (Medium–Hard)**

A small service that receives webhooks (simulate a payment provider) and verifies HMAC-SHA256 signatures before trusting the payload, including a deliberate test proving a tampered payload is rejected. Domain: payments/fintech infrastructure.

**"jose-Based Multi-Audience Token System" (Hard)**

A token system using the jose library supporting multiple audiences (web client, mobile client, internal service-to-service) with different expiry/claims per audience, and a key-rotation mechanism (JWKS-style) so old tokens gracefully expire during a key rotation. Domain: platform/identity engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Hash and verify a password with bcrypt, then time-attack-test two different wrong passwords to confirm constant-time comparison behavior.  

* Build HMAC-SHA256 signature generation and verification from scratch (no library shortcuts) and prove a 1-byte payload change invalidates the signature.  

* Implement JWT refresh-token rotation and prove a stolen, already-used refresh token is rejected on reuse.  

* Explain in writing (with a diagram) why httpOnly \+ SameSite=Lax cookies mitigate XSS-based token theft, and what they do NOT protect against.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Ledger" — A Secure Multi-Tenant Expense-Approval API**

A production-grade backend for a B2B expense-approval SaaS: hand-rolled Express architecture with layered middleware, a normalized multi-tenant PostgreSQL schema (via Prisma) supporting organizations/roles/approval chains, hardened with Helmet/CORS/rate-limiting/sanitization, a from-scratch JWT \+ httpOnly refresh-token auth system with rotation, HMAC-signed webhook endpoints for a simulated payment provider, comprehensive Supertest coverage (happy paths, auth failures, tampered webhooks), and a documented EXPLAIN ANALYZE-driven performance pass on the top 3 slowest endpoints under seeded load. Domain: fintech / B2B SaaS. Why it matters: this is a genuinely defensible "I built a secure backend" portfolio piece — covering real auth, real multi-tenancy, real hardening, not a tutorial CRUD app.

# **Summary: The Full Sequence at a Glance**

| 1 | Server Runtime & Framework | Node.js → Express.js |
| 2 | API Design & Hardening | REST APIs → express-async-errors → CORS → Helmet.js → express-rate-limit → DOMPurify → compression |

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 06 — Testing \+ CI/CD \+ Deployment, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 06 budget (125 core hours), the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–05 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

This is the phase that ends at Exit Point A — your first job-ready milestone (₹6–10 LPA, junior full-stack, early-stage startups/agencies). Exit A's checklist is: a live demo URL, green CI badge, Docker deployment, conventional commits. Every stage below builds toward one of those four things. Resequenced by real dependency: unit/component testing → E2E testing → CI → containerization → deployment, with the CI badge (shields.io) deliberately last since it depends on everything before it actually working.

# **STAGE 1 — Unit & Component Testing**

*The foundation of the testing pyramid. Requires a real app with components and logic (Phases 01–05) to have anything meaningful to test.*

## **▌ 1\. Vitest**

*Test runner — must exist before anything else in this stage, since every other tool here either extends it or runs inside it.*

* Test file conventions and running tests (vitest run, watch mode)  

* describe/it/test, expect assertions  

* Setup/teardown: beforeEach, afterEach, beforeAll, afterAll  

* Mocking: vi.fn(), vi.mock(), spying on functions  

* Vitest vs Jest — why Vitest is the modern default for Vite/Next.js projects (native ESM, faster, shared config with Vite)

## **▌ 2\. React Testing Library**

*Requires Vitest (1) as the runner — RTL provides the rendering and query APIs that run inside Vitest's test blocks.*

* render() — mounting a component in a test environment  

* Query methods: getBy, queryBy, findBy, and their variants (ByRole, ByText, ByLabelText)  

* Why RTL queries prioritize accessibility-based queries (ByRole) over implementation details (class names, test IDs)  

* Testing philosophy: "test behavior, not implementation" — what this actually means in practice  

* screen object vs destructured render() queries

## **▌ 3\. @testing-library/user-event**

*Requires RTL (2) as the base — user-event replaces RTL's built-in fireEvent with more realistic event sequences.*

* userEvent.click(), .type(), .selectOptions() and how they differ from fireEvent (simulating real browser event sequences, not just dispatching one event)  

* Async nature of user-event — why interactions must be awaited  

* Testing forms end-to-end within a component test (ties back to Phase 02's forms, Phase 03's react-hook-form)

## **▌ 4\. MSW (Mock Service Worker)**

*Requires component tests with user interactions (2, 3\\) — MSW exists to mock the network calls those interactions trigger (TanStack Query/tRPC calls from Phase 04).*

* Intercepting requests at the network level (service worker in browser, request interception in Node for tests)  

* Defining request handlers — mocking REST and GraphQL responses  

* Why MSW is preferred over mocking fetch/axios directly — tests exercise real request code paths  

* Using MSW in both tests and local development (mocking a backend that isn't ready yet)

## **▌ 5\. v8 Coverage**

*Requires actual tests to exist (1–4) — coverage reports on what those tests do and don't exercise.*

* Coverage metrics: statements, branches, functions, lines  

* Configuring Vitest's built-in v8 coverage provider  

* Reading a coverage report — where gaps actually matter vs where 100% coverage is a vanity metric  

* Coverage thresholds — failing CI if coverage drops below a set percentage (bridges to Stage 3\\)

## **▌ 6\. Codecov**

*Requires v8 coverage output (5) — Codecov is a hosted service for tracking and visualizing that output over time.*

* Uploading coverage reports from CI (bridges to Stage 3's GitHub Actions)  

* Coverage badges (precursor to the CI badge work in Stage 5\\)  

* Coverage diffs on pull requests — seeing whether a PR increases or decreases coverage

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Unit & Component Testing  (15.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Vitest | 4 |
| React Testing Library | 4 |
| @testing-library/user-event | 2 |
| MSW (Mock Service Worker) | 3 |
| v8 Coverage | 1.5 |
| Codecov | 1 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Fully-Tested Utility Library" (Easy)**

Take a utility library from an earlier phase and bring it to 100% meaningful coverage with Vitest, including edge cases, with a coverage report published via Codecov. Domain: dev tooling.

**"Mocked API Component Test Suite" (Medium)**

A React component suite (forms, data tables) tested with React Testing Library \+ @testing-library/user-event for realistic interactions, and MSW to mock network requests so tests never hit a real backend. Domain: frontend QA.

**"Regression-Proof Checkout Flow Tests" (Hard)**

A full test suite for a multi-step checkout component (from Phase 4’s state machine work) covering every state transition, error path, and MSW-simulated payment failure, with coverage thresholds enforced in CI and tracked over time in Codecov. Domain: e-commerce QA engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write Vitest tests achieving full branch coverage for a function with 4 conditional paths.  

* Test a form component with @testing-library/user-event simulating real typing, tabbing, and paste events.  

* Use MSW to simulate a 500 error and verify the component shows the correct error UI, with no real network call made.  

* Set a minimum coverage threshold in CI config and demonstrate a build failing when coverage drops below it.

# **STAGE 2 — End-to-End Testing**

## **▌ 7\. Playwright**

*Conceptually distinct from Stage 1 — E2E tests exercise the whole running app (frontend \+ backend \+ database) rather than isolated components, so this naturally follows once the pieces being tested end-to-end already exist across Phases 01–05.*

**Core Concepts**

* Browser automation model — Playwright drives real browsers (Chromium, Firefox, WebKit)  

* page.goto(), locators, and Playwright's auto-waiting (vs manual waits/sleeps)  

* Assertions with expect() in a Playwright context

**Writing Real E2E Flows**

* Testing full user journeys: signup → login → core app action (ties directly to the JWT auth flow from Phase 05\\)  

* Handling authentication state across tests (storageState, reusing a logged-in session)  

* Testing against a real or seeded test database

**Tooling**

* Playwright Test Runner — test organization, fixtures  

* Trace viewer and debugging failed tests  

* Running Playwright in CI (headless mode, bridges to Stage 3\\)

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — End-to-End Testing  (7 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Playwright | 7 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Critical User Journey E2E Suite" (Medium)**

Playwright tests covering the 3 most critical user journeys of any earlier full-stack project (signup → login → core action), running against a real (test) database. Domain: QA engineering.

**"Cross-Browser Visual Regression Suite" (Hard)**

Playwright tests that run across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, include visual snapshot comparisons for key pages, and are wired to fail CI on unexpected visual diffs. Domain: QA / design systems.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a Playwright test that logs in, performs an action, and verifies a database side-effect (via a test API endpoint).  

* Add a visual regression snapshot test and intentionally break the UI to confirm the test catches it.  

* Run the same Playwright suite across 3 browser engines and document any browser-specific failure found.

# **STAGE 3 — Continuous Integration**

## **▌ 8\. GitHub Actions**

*Requires tests to exist (Stages 1–2) — CI's entire purpose is running those tests automatically; there's nothing to automate before they exist.*

**Workflow Fundamentals**

* Workflow YAML structure — on, jobs, steps  

* Triggers: push, pull\\\_request, schedule, workflow\\\_dispatch  

* Runners — GitHub-hosted vs self-hosted (conceptual)  

* Actions marketplace — using pre-built actions (checkout, setup-node)

**Building a Real CI Pipeline**

* Running Vitest (Stage 1\\) and Playwright (Stage 2\\) in CI  

* Caching dependencies (node\\\_modules, npm cache) for faster runs  

* Matrix builds — running against multiple Node versions (concept)  

* Uploading coverage to Codecov (Stage 1, Topic 6\\) as a CI step

**Secrets & Environments**

* GitHub Secrets — storing credentials (DB URLs, API keys) securely for CI  

* Environment-specific workflows (staging vs production deploys)  

* Branch protection rules requiring CI to pass before merge

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Continuous Integration  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| GitHub Actions | 8 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Full CI Pipeline for a Full-Stack App" (Medium–Hard)**

A GitHub Actions workflow that on every PR: installs deps with caching, runs lint, typecheck, unit tests, and E2E tests in parallel jobs, and blocks merge on any failure — applied to one of your earlier full-stack projects. Domain: DevOps/engineering process.

**"Matrix-Tested Cross-Version CI" (Hard)**

A CI workflow using a build matrix to test a library across multiple Node.js versions and operating systems simultaneously, with a status badge reflecting real pass/fail state. Domain: open-source maintenance / library engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a GitHub Actions workflow with dependency caching and measure the CI time saved vs. no caching.  

* Split lint/typecheck/test into parallel jobs and measure total pipeline time improvement vs. sequential.  

* Build a matrix CI job testing 3 Node versions and intentionally break compatibility with one version to confirm the matrix catches it.

# **STAGE 4 — Containerization**

*Independent of testing/CI conceptually, but placed here because Docker images are what CI will build and what deployment (Stage 5\\) will run — this stage sits at the intersection.*

## **▌ 9\. Docker**

**Core Concepts**

* Images vs containers — the class/instance analogy  

* Dockerfile syntax: FROM, WORKDIR, COPY, RUN, CMD/ENTRYPOINT  

* Layers and layer caching — why instruction order in a Dockerfile affects build speed  

* Multi-stage builds — separating build dependencies from the final runtime image

**Building for a Real App**

* Dockerizing a Next.js app — the standalone output mode and its Dockerfile implications  

* Dockerizing an Express/Node backend (Phase 05\\)  

* Environment variables in Docker — build-time (ARG) vs runtime (ENV)  

* .dockerignore — excluding node\\\_modules, .git, etc.

**Running Containers**

* docker build, docker run, port mapping (-p)  

* Volumes — persisting data outside the container (relevant for local Postgres, Phase 05\\)  

* docker exec — shelling into a running container for debugging  

* docker logs

## **▌ 10\. Docker Compose**

*Requires Docker fundamentals (9) — Compose orchestrates multiple containers that Docker alone runs one at a time.*

* docker-compose.yml structure — services, networks, volumes  

* Running a full local stack: app \+ Postgres (Phase 05\\) \+ Redis (later phase) in one command  

* depends\\\_on and service startup ordering  

* Local development workflow — hot-reloading inside containers vs running natively

## **▌ 11\. GitHub Container Registry (GHCR)**

*Requires Docker images to exist (9) — GHCR is where those images get pushed to be pulled by a deployment target.*

* Authenticating to GHCR from the CLI and from CI  

* Tagging images correctly (semantic versions, latest, commit SHA)  

* Pulling images from GHCR in a deployment context (bridges to Stage 5's Render)

## **▌ 12\. docker/build-push-action**

*Sits at the direct intersection of GitHub Actions (8) and Docker/GHCR (9, 11\\) — this action automates building and pushing a Docker image as a CI step.*

* Configuring the action: context, tags, push conditions  

* Building and pushing on merge to main, not on every PR (deployment discipline)  

* Connecting this to a deployment trigger on Render (Stage 5\\)

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Containerization  (18 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Docker | 10 |
| Docker Compose | 4 |
| GitHub Container Registry (GHCR) | 2 |
| docker/build-push-action | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Multi-Stage Dockerized Full-Stack App" (Medium)**

Dockerize a full-stack app (Phase 5 backend \+ a frontend build) using multi-stage builds to keep the final image small, with a documented image-size before/after comparison. Domain: DevOps.

**"Local Dev Environment via Docker Compose" (Medium)**

A docker-compose.yml that spins up the app, a Postgres database, and Redis together with one command, including healthchecks and proper service dependency ordering. Domain: developer experience / infra.

**"Automated Image Publishing Pipeline" (Hard)**

A GitHub Actions workflow using docker/build-push-action to build and publish a versioned, tagged image to GitHub Container Registry on every merge to main, including a documented rollback procedure using a previous tag. Domain: DevOps / release engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a multi-stage Dockerfile that reduces final image size by at least 50% vs. a naive single-stage version, and document the measurement.  

* Add healthchecks to a docker-compose service and prove dependent services wait correctly.  

* Publish an image to GHCR from CI and pull/run it locally to confirm it matches the CI-built artifact.

# **STAGE 5 — Deployment & Exit A Polish**

*Deliberately last. Deployment needs a tested, containerized, CI-integrated app (Stages 1–4) to deploy. The CI badge (shields.io) is the final topic since it's a status indicator for a pipeline that must already be working.*

## **▌ 13\. Render**

*Requires Docker (9) — Render deploys directly from a Dockerfile or a pushed image (GHCR, Topic 11).*

* Connecting a GitHub repo or GHCR image to a Render service  

* Web Services vs Background Workers vs Static Sites on Render  

* Environment variables and secrets in Render's dashboard  

* Auto-deploy on push vs manual deploys  

* Health checks — Render's mechanism for verifying a deploy succeeded before routing traffic to it

## **▌ 14\. HTTP/2**

*Placed alongside deployment since it's a hosting/transport-layer concern most relevant once a real app is being served in production.*

* HTTP/1.1 limitations HTTP/2 addresses: head-of-line blocking, connection overhead  

* Multiplexing — multiple requests over a single connection  

* Server push (concept, largely deprecated in practice) and header compression (HPACK)  

* How this relates to what Render/Vercel (Phase 02\\) provide automatically — usually nothing to configure manually, but worth understanding what's happening

## **▌ 15\. shields.io**

*Last topic in the phase — this is literally the "green CI badge" from Exit A's checklist, and it depends on GitHub Actions (8) already passing reliably before there's anything honest to badge.*

* Generating dynamic badges from GitHub Actions workflow status  

* Coverage badges sourced from Codecov (Stage 1, Topic 6\\)  

* Adding badges to the README — the actual artifact a recruiter or hiring manager sees first

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Deployment & Exit A Polish  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Render | 2.5 |
| HTTP/2 | 2 |
| shields.io | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Production Deployment with README Badges" (Easy–Medium)**

Deploy a full-stack project to Render with a properly configured production environment, and add live status/build/coverage badges via shields.io to the README. Domain: developer presentation / DevOps.

**"HTTP/2 Performance Verification" (Medium)**

Deploy the same app behind both HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 configurations (or verify Render’s default) and document real measured differences in multiplexed asset loading via browser waterfall charts. Domain: web performance / infra.

**Practice Exercises**

* Deploy a project to Render end-to-end from a fresh repo, documenting every configuration step.  

* Add 4 different shields.io badges (build status, coverage, license, version) to a README and verify they update live.  

* Capture a network waterfall chart and explain, in writing, where HTTP/2 multiplexing is visibly helping.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Shipwright" — A Fully CI/CD-Automated, Containerized SaaS Deployment Pipeline**

Take any earlier full-stack project (Phase 5’s "Ledger" is a good candidate) and build the complete professional delivery pipeline around it: Vitest \+ React Testing Library \+ MSW unit/component coverage with enforced thresholds, a Playwright E2E suite covering critical journeys across 3 browsers with visual regression checks, a multi-stage Dockerfile and docker-compose local dev environment, a full GitHub Actions pipeline (parallel lint/typecheck/test/E2E jobs, matrix-tested, cached, auto-publishing tagged images to GHCR on merge), and a live production deployment on Render with real status/coverage badges in the README. Domain: DevOps / release engineering. Why it matters: this is the artifact that proves you can ship software professionally, not just write it — the single most common gap between bootcamp-level and production-level engineers.

# **Exit Point A — What You Have When This Phase Is Done**

*Restated from the official curriculum, mapped to where each piece was built in this resequenced version.*

* Next.js 15 App Router \+ tRPC \+ Zustand (Phases 02, 04\\)  

* PostgreSQL \+ Prisma (Phase 05, Stage 4\\)  

* JWT auth (Phase 05, Stage 5\\)  

* Docker deployment (this phase, Stage 4\\)  

* GitHub Actions CI (this phase, Stage 3\\)  

* Playwright E2E tests (this phase, Stage 2\\)  

* Deployed on Vercel/Render with a green CI badge (this phase, Stage 5\\)  

* Conventional commits (Phase 01, Stage 7\\) — already in place from early on

Target: early-stage startups, dev agencies. ₹6–10 LPA. This is the point where applying makes sense — you don't need to wait for later phases.

# **Phase 06b — React Native (Mobile)**

*Band: Core   ·   Track: Mobile (Optional)   ·   Realistic hours: 111h (header said no total stated)   ·   Exit A2: Mobile-Capable Add-On (₹8–12 LPA)   ·   Optional phase*

**CONCURRENT WITH PHASE 06/07 · MOBILE TRACK**

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 06b — React Native & Mobile (Optional), Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 06b budget (123 core hours), the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–08 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**OPTIONAL PHASE: Per the official curriculum, this phase runs concurrently with the start of Phase 07, not sequentially after Phase 06\. It's optional — skipping it does not block progress toward Exit B or Exit ★1. Build it only if mobile-capable roles are actually part of your target company list.**

Every topic from Phase 06b, resequenced by real dependency: React Native fundamentals → tooling/navigation/layout → client-side storage and secure token handling → native device capabilities → performance/internals → app store shipping, with OTA updates last since they only make sense for an app that's already published.

# **STAGE 1 — React Native Foundation**

*Requires React (Phase 02\\) as a direct prerequisite — React Native uses the same component/hooks model, with a different rendering target (native views instead of the DOM).*

## **▌ 1\. React Native Fundamentals**

**Core Model**

* Native components (View, Text, Image, ScrollView) as RN's equivalent to HTML elements  

* Why there's no DOM — RN renders to native platform views, not a browser  

* StyleSheet API — CSS-like styling, but a real subset of CSS properties, not the full web spec  

* Same React mental model: components, props, state, hooks all transfer directly from Phase 02

**Touch & Interaction**

* Touchable components (Pressable, TouchableOpacity) — RN's equivalent to onClick handlers  

* Gesture basics — press, long-press, swipe (conceptual introduction)

**Lists & Scrolling**

* ScrollView vs FlatList — when each is appropriate (FlatList's virtualization is covered fully in Stage 4\\)  

* Basic list rendering before the performance-focused deep dive later

## **▌ 2\. Expo \+ EAS Build**

*Requires RN fundamentals (1) — Expo is the tooling and managed workflow built around React Native; there's nothing to build until basic RN concepts exist.*

* Expo managed workflow vs bare React Native — why Expo is the recommended starting point  

* Expo Go — running an app on a physical device instantly during development  

* EAS Build — cloud-based native builds without needing local Xcode/Android Studio setup  

* expo-cli commands for scaffolding and running a project  

* app.json/app.config.js — app metadata, icons, splash screens

## **▌ 3\. React Navigation**

*Requires RN fundamentals (1) — React Navigation is RN's routing library, conceptually parallel to Phase 02's App Router but for a screen-stack model instead of URLs.*

* Stack navigator — push/pop screen transitions  

* Tab navigator, drawer navigator  

* Passing params between screens  

* Deep linking (conceptual — connecting a URL scheme to a specific screen)  

* Contrasting with Phase 02's file-based App Router — RN navigation is configured, not file-system-driven

## **▌ 4\. Flexbox Layout Differences (RN vs Web)**

*Requires Flexbox from Phase 03's CSS stage as the point of comparison — this section is entirely about where RN's implementation diverges from web CSS.*

* Default flexDirection: column in RN (vs row on web) — the single most common source of layout confusion for web developers  

* Which CSS properties don't exist in RN (no CSS Grid, no float, limited selector model)  

* Percentage-based vs flex-based sizing in RN  

* Platform differences in flexbox behavior between iOS and Android (minor but real)

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — React Native Foundation  (16 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| React Native Fundamentals | 6 |
| Expo \+ EAS Build | 4 |
| React Navigation | 4 |
| Flexbox Layout Differences (RN vs Web) | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Cross-Platform Habit Tracker" (Easy)**

Rebuild Phase 2’s habit tracker as a React Native \+ Expo app, documenting layout differences (Flexbox defaults differ from web) and navigation using React Navigation (stack \+ tabs). Domain: personal wellness / mobile.

**"Recipe Browser with Deep Linking" (Medium)**

A recipe browsing app with nested navigation (stack inside tabs), deep linking support (opening a specific recipe from a shared URL), and platform-aware layout adjustments for notches/safe areas. Domain: lifestyle/food.

**"EAS-Built Multi-Environment App" (Hard)**

An app configured with separate dev/staging/production build profiles via EAS Build, each pointing to a different API environment, with a documented build-and-distribute workflow for internal testers. Domain: mobile release engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Port one web component to React Native and document every styling difference encountered.  

* Implement nested navigation (tabs containing stacks) with a shared header pattern.  

* Configure and trigger 3 separate EAS build profiles (dev/staging/prod) and compare their outputs.

# **STAGE 2 — Storage & Secure Token Handling**

*Requires RN fundamentals and navigation (Stage 1\\) — a real app with multiple screens is what makes persistent storage meaningful.*

## **▌ 5\. AsyncStorage \+ Offline-First Sync**

*RN's equivalent to Phase 02's localStorage/sessionStorage, but asynchronous by design since RN has no synchronous storage API.*

* AsyncStorage API — get/set/remove, all promise-based  

* Why RN has no synchronous storage equivalent to web's localStorage  

* Offline-first architecture — queuing writes locally when offline, syncing when connectivity returns  

* Conflict resolution strategies for offline sync (last-write-wins vs merge strategies)  

* Connecting offline sync to the TanStack Query patterns from Phase 04 — persisting query cache for offline access

## **▌ 6\. expo-secure-store**

*Requires Expo (2) specifically — this is an Expo module. Placed directly after AsyncStorage (5) since both are storage mechanisms, but this one is for sensitive data specifically.*

* Why AsyncStorage is unencrypted and unsuitable for tokens — the specific gap this fills  

* Platform-backed secure storage (Keychain on iOS, Keystore on Android)  

* Storing JWT access/refresh tokens (Phase 05\\) securely on-device  

* API parity with AsyncStorage but with encryption guarantees

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Storage & Secure Token Handling  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| AsyncStorage \+ Offline-First Sync | 4 |
| expo-secure-store | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Offline-First Grocery List" (Medium)**

A grocery list app that works fully offline using AsyncStorage, queues changes made offline, and syncs to a backend when connectivity returns, with conflict resolution for simultaneous edits. Domain: productivity / offline-first apps.

**"Secure Mobile Auth Session" (Medium–Hard)**

A mobile login flow storing tokens in expo-secure-store (not AsyncStorage) with biometric-gated access to sensitive actions, and a documented threat-model comparison of AsyncStorage vs. secure-store. Domain: mobile security.

**Practice Exercises**

* Implement an offline write queue that syncs automatically on reconnect, tested by toggling airplane mode.  

* Store a token in expo-secure-store vs. AsyncStorage and explain (with evidence) why one is safe for secrets and one isn’t.  

* Handle a sync conflict (same item edited offline on two devices) with a documented resolution strategy.

# **STAGE 3 — Native Device Capabilities**

*Requires Expo (Stage 1, Topic 2\\) — most native capabilities are exposed through Expo's SDK modules.*

## **▌ 7\. Native APIs (Camera, Push, Permissions)**

**Permissions Model**

* Requesting permissions at runtime (camera, notifications, location)  

* Handling permission denial gracefully — a required UX pattern, not optional

**Camera**

* expo-camera — capturing photos/video  

* Common use case: document/ID capture for onboarding flows (ties to ClientSync's onboarding domain)

**Push Notifications**

* Expo push notification service — registering a device token  

* Sending a push notification (server-side trigger, ties back to Phase 05's backend)  

* Handling notifications when the app is foregrounded vs backgrounded vs closed

## **▌ 8\. Platform-Specific Code (iOS vs Android)**

*Requires native API usage (7) — platform differences most often surface exactly where native capabilities are involved.*

* Platform.OS and Platform.select() for conditional logic  

* File naming conventions for platform-specific implementations (.ios.tsx, .android.tsx)  

* Common divergences: safe area handling, status bar behavior, back button handling on Android

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Native Device Capabilities  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Native APIs (Camera, Push, Permissions) | 5 |
| Platform-Specific Code (iOS vs Android) | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Receipt Scanner Utility" (Medium)**

An app using the camera API to photograph receipts, request the correct permissions gracefully (with a fallback UI if denied), and save images locally. Domain: personal finance / utility apps.

**"Push Notification Reminder App" (Medium–Hard)**

A reminders app sending real push notifications (via Expo push service) with platform-specific permission flows for iOS vs Android documented and handled distinctly. Domain: productivity.

**Practice Exercises**

* Request camera permission, handle both grant and deny paths with appropriate UI in each case.  

* Send a test push notification end-to-end and document the iOS vs Android permission prompt differences.  

* Write platform-specific code (Platform.select or .ios./.android. files) for one component that must look different per OS.

# **STAGE 4 — Performance & Internals**

*Requires a real app with lists and navigation (Stages 1–3) to have anything meaningful to profile or optimize.*

## **▌ 9\. FlatList Performance \+ Virtualization**

*RN's equivalent to Phase 04's TanStack Virtual — the same underlying problem (don't render offscreen items), solved with RN's built-in list component.*

* getItemLayout for fixed-height items — skipping expensive layout measurement  

* keyExtractor and why stable keys matter as much here as in web React (Phase 02\\)  

* windowSize, initialNumToRender, maxToRenderPerBatch — tuning virtualization aggressiveness  

* When to reach for a dedicated virtualization library vs FlatList's built-in behavior

## **▌ 10\. Hermes Engine**

*Placed alongside FlatList as a performance topic — Hermes is the JS engine RN apps run on, optimized specifically for mobile startup time and memory.*

* What Hermes is — a JS engine built specifically for React Native (vs JavaScriptCore)  

* Bytecode precompilation — why this improves app startup time  

* Enabling/verifying Hermes is active in a project

## **▌ 11\. New Architecture (Fabric \+ TurboModules)**

*The most advanced/internals-focused topic in the phase — requires general RN architecture familiarity (Stages 1–3) and Hermes (10) as context, since the New Architecture changes how JS communicates with native code.*

* The old bridge architecture and its performance limitations (async, serialized JSON messaging)  

* Fabric — the new rendering system, enabling synchronous layout measurement  

* TurboModules — lazy-loaded native modules with direct JS-to-native calls, replacing the bridge  

* Practical implication: most apps don't need to think about this directly, but library compatibility increasingly assumes it

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Performance & Internals  (6.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| FlatList Performance \+ Virtualization | 3 |
| Hermes Engine | 1.5 |
| New Architecture (Fabric \+ TurboModules) | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"10,000-Item Contact List" (Hard)**

A contacts app rendering a very large list using FlatList performance best practices (getItemLayout, windowing tuning) profiled before/after optimization. Domain: productivity / performance engineering.

**"Hermes vs JSC Benchmark Report" (Medium)**

Build a CPU-intensive screen (e.g., client-side image filtering) and benchmark its performance under the Hermes engine vs JSC, documenting startup time and runtime differences with real numbers. Domain: mobile performance engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Profile a slow FlatList, apply at least 2 optimizations, and document the measured improvement.  

* Explain in writing what the New Architecture (Fabric \+ TurboModules) changes about the JS-to-native bridge and why it matters for performance.  

* Benchmark app startup time with Hermes enabled vs disabled and record the difference.

# **STAGE 5 — Shipping**

*Deliberately last. Requires a working, tested app (Stages 1–4) and EAS Build (Stage 1, Topic 2\\) as the build pipeline that makes submission possible.*

## **▌ 12\. App Store \+ Play Store Submission**

**Preparing for Submission**

* App signing — certificates and provisioning profiles (iOS), keystores (Android)  

* App Store Connect setup, Google Play Console setup  

* Required assets: icons, screenshots, privacy policy, app description

**Submission Process**

* Building a production binary with EAS Build (Stage 1, Topic 2\\)  

* Submitting via EAS Submit or manual upload  

* Review process expectations — Apple's stricter review vs Google's faster turnaround  

* Common rejection reasons and how to avoid them

## **▌ 13\. EAS Update (OTA Updates)**

*Genuinely last — over-the-air updates only make sense for an app that's already published (Topic 12\\) and installed on real devices.*

* What EAS Update enables — pushing JS/asset changes without a full app store review cycle  

* What CANNOT be updated OTA — native code changes still require a full resubmission  

* Update channels — staging vs production update tracks  

* Rollback strategy if a bad update ships

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Shipping  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| App Store \+ Play Store Submission | 5 |
| EAS Update (OTA Updates) | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"App Store & Play Store Submission Dry Run" (Medium–Hard)**

Prepare a complete submission package (icons, screenshots at required sizes, privacy policy, store listing copy) for one of your built apps and go through the actual submission flow (to internal/closed testing track if not publishing publicly). Domain: mobile release / product.

**"Zero-Downtime OTA Update Demo" (Hard)**

Ship a bug fix to a live-installed app using EAS Update (over-the-air) without requiring users to redownload from the store, with a before/after demonstration and a documented rollback plan if the OTA update itself is broken. Domain: mobile release engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Generate all required icon/screenshot sizes for both App Store and Play Store submission from a single source asset.  

* Push an OTA update via EAS Update to a running app and verify it applies without a store redownload.  

* Write a rollback plan for a bad OTA update and simulate executing it.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Nomad" — An Offline-First Field Reporting App**

A mobile app for field workers (e.g., inspectors, delivery drivers) who often lack connectivity: photo capture with camera permissions handled gracefully, offline-first data entry via AsyncStorage with a sync queue and conflict resolution on reconnect, secure token storage via expo-secure-store, push notifications for assignment updates, a FlatList-based large history view tuned for performance, multi-environment EAS build profiles, and a full store-submission-ready asset package plus at least one OTA update shipped to a test build. Domain: field service / logistics mobile tooling. Why it matters: offline-first, secure, store-ready mobile apps are a distinct and valuable skill set from web development — this proves end-to-end mobile shipping competence.

# **Exit Point A2 — What You Have When This Phase Is Done**

*Restated from the official curriculum, mapped to where each piece was built in this resequenced version.*

* Same Phase 05 backend — no backend changes required for mobile  

* A working ClientSync Mobile client running on Expo Go (Stage 1–3)  

* A signed build submitted, or ready to submit, to at least one app store (Stage 5, Topic 12\\)

Target: roles explicitly requiring or valuing React Native experience, alongside your existing Exit A/B web positioning. ₹8–12 LPA. This is an optional branch — it broadens the roles you're eligible for without replacing the core web-focused path.

|  | **PHASE TOTAL (incl. ClientSync build)** | 37h | 6h | 9h | **57h** |

**B  EXIT POINT B**

*After Phase 07 · Month \~6.5 · Junior-to-Mid Full-Stack*

**WHAT YOU HAVE**

All of Exit A \+ OpenAPI-documented API with Swagger UI, GraphQL knowledge, Postman collections, Mermaid architecture diagrams.

**JOB TITLES TO USE ON APPLICATIONS**

Software Engineer  ·  Full-Stack Engineer  ·  Backend Developer  ·  Node.js Developer

**ROLES TO APPLY FOR**

SDE-1 at API-first or developer-tool companies

Backend Engineer at fintech, edtech, or SaaS startups

Full-Stack Engineer where API design is called out in the JD

**COMPANIES TO TARGET (INDIA)**

API-first companies: Postman (Bangalore HQ), Hasura, DhiWise

Fintech: Juspay, M2P Fintech, Razorpay (junior), Setu, Open Financial

Developer tools: Appsmith, Retool India, Tooljet

SaaS with documented APIs: Chargebee, Freshworks (junior track)

**WHAT TO HIGHLIGHT**

Swagger UI link in your portfolio (live, interactive, not a screenshot)

OpenAPI spec committed to repo with versioning

GraphQL schema as evidence of API paradigm breadth

Postman collection exported and linked in README

**WHERE TO APPLY**

Same as Exit A \+ specifically target companies whose product is an API or dev tool

Cold email/DM to backend leads at API companies — they respect people who show up with a Swagger link

**WHAT THEY'LL TEST**

REST vs GraphQL tradeoffs (you can now answer this properly)

API versioning strategies

HTTP methods, status codes, idempotency

Auth flows (JWT, OAuth basics)

Intermediate SQL

Possibly: design a REST API for a given problem (live)

**COMPENSATION**

India: ₹8–12 LPA  ·  US remote: $60–80k

**CAUTION**  *Exit B is close to Exit A in time (\~2 weeks). Don't stop at Exit A if you can push through Phase 07 — the OpenAPI spec alone adds credibility disproportionate to the effort.*

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 07 — API Documentation & Developer Tooling, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 07 budget (57 core hours), the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–06 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**ORDERING NOTE: curl is moved ahead of Postman/Insomnia from the official list order. Postman and Insomnia are GUI wrappers around exactly what curl does at the command line — understanding curl first means the GUI tools are recognized as a convenience layer over a concept you already have, not a new concept themselves.**

This is the short phase between Exit A and Exit B — 2 weeks in the official schedule, and per the curriculum's own caution: "don't stop at Exit A if you can push through Phase 07 — the OpenAPI spec alone adds credibility disproportionate to the effort." Resequenced by real dependency: manual HTTP tooling → API documentation → GraphQL as a deliberate contrast to REST, since Exit B explicitly expects you to answer REST vs GraphQL tradeoffs in interviews.

# **STAGE 1 — Manual HTTP Tooling**

*Requires a real API to hit (Phase 05\\) — these tools exist to inspect, test, and debug HTTP requests/responses by hand, before any documentation layer is added on top.*

## **▌ 1\. curl**

*The most primitive tool in this stage — a command-line HTTP client. Placed first because Postman and Insomnia are graphical abstractions over exactly what curl does directly.*

* Basic syntax — curl \\\<url\\\>, \-X for method, \-H for headers, \-d for body  

* Sending JSON payloads (-H "Content-Type: application/json" \-d '{...}')  

* Inspecting response headers (-i, \-I) and verbose output (-v) for debugging  

* Authentication — sending a Bearer token (ties back to Phase 05's JWT), Basic auth  

* Saving/loading cookies (-c, \-b) — relevant given Phase 05's httpOnly cookie auth flow  

* Why curl remains the lowest-common-denominator tool: works in CI scripts, SSH sessions, anywhere without a GUI

## **▌ 2\. Postman**

*Requires curl (1) conceptually — Postman is the same request/response model with a GUI, collections, and environment management layered on top.*

* Building and sending requests — the GUI equivalent of curl's flags  

* Collections — organizing related requests together  

* Environments and variables — swapping base URLs/tokens between dev/staging/prod without editing every request  

* Scripting: pre-request scripts and tests (JavaScript) for automated checks on responses  

* Exporting a Postman collection — this is the literal artifact Exit B's checklist wants linked in your README

## **▌ 3\. Insomnia**

*Same category as Postman (2) — a lighter-weight alternative GUI HTTP client, placed immediately after for direct comparison.*

* Core workflow parity with Postman — requests, environments, collections (called "workspaces")  

* Where Insomnia differs: simpler interface, native GraphQL support (foreshadows Stage 3\\)  

* When to reach for Insomnia over Postman — personal/lightweight use vs Postman's team-collaboration features

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Manual HTTP Tooling  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| curl | 2 |
| Postman | 3 |
| Insomnia | 1 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Zero-GUI API Debugging Toolkit" (Easy)**

A personal collection of curl scripts (with a Makefile or shell aliases) that fully exercise every endpoint of an earlier project’s API, usable by teammates with no GUI tool installed. Domain: developer tooling.

**"Shared Postman Collection with Environments" (Medium)**

A Postman collection for a full-stack project with dev/staging/prod environments, pre-request scripts for auth token refresh, and automated test assertions per request, exported and version-controlled alongside the code. Domain: API QA/tooling.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a curl one-liner that authenticates, extracts a token with jq, and uses it in a second authenticated request.  

* Build a Postman collection with a pre-request script that auto-refreshes an expired token.  

* Reproduce a bug report using only curl, without opening any GUI tool.

# **STAGE 2 — API Documentation**

*Requires a real, stable API (Phase 05\\) and familiarity with inspecting requests/responses (Stage 1\\) — you can't document an API accurately without having manually explored its behavior first.*

## **▌ 4\. OpenAPI 3.0**

**Specification Fundamentals**

* The OpenAPI spec as a language-agnostic contract for describing a REST API  

* Structure: paths, operations, parameters, requestBody, responses  

* Schema objects — describing request/response shapes (conceptually parallel to the Zod schemas from Phases 01/03/05)  

* Reusable components — $ref for shared schemas, parameters, responses

**Writing a Real Spec**

* Documenting authentication (JWT Bearer scheme, ties to Phase 05\\)  

* Documenting status codes and error responses accurately (ties to Phase 05's REST status code conventions)  

* Generating an OpenAPI spec from code (annotations/decorators) vs hand-writing YAML/JSON — tradeoffs  

* Validating a spec against the actual API behavior — specs drift from reality if not enforced

## **▌ 5\. Swagger UI**

*Directly depends on OpenAPI 3.0 (4) — Swagger UI renders a spec as an interactive, browsable documentation page; it has nothing to render without a spec already written.*

* Serving Swagger UI from your API (as a route, e.g. /docs)  

* Interactive "Try it out" — making real requests from the docs page itself  

* Why a live Swagger UI link (not a screenshot) is what Exit B's checklist specifically wants in your portfolio  

* Customizing Swagger UI branding/appearance (minor, but relevant for portfolio polish)

## **▌ 6\. Mermaid**

*Independent of OpenAPI/Swagger mechanically, but placed here since architecture diagrams naturally complement API documentation for a portfolio README.*

* Mermaid syntax basics — flowcharts, sequence diagrams, ER diagrams  

* Sequence diagrams for documenting an auth flow (login → JWT issuance → protected route, ties to Phase 05\\)  

* ER diagrams for documenting a database schema (ties to Phase 05's PostgreSQL/Prisma schema)  

* Rendering Mermaid directly in GitHub READMEs — no external tooling needed

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — API Documentation  (9 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| OpenAPI 3.0 | 5 |
| Swagger UI | 2 |
| Mermaid | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Auto-Generated OpenAPI Docs Site" (Medium)**

Write a complete OpenAPI 3.0 spec for an earlier project’s API by hand, render it with Swagger UI, and verify every documented example actually matches real API behavior (no doc drift). Domain: API developer experience.

**"Architecture Diagrams as Code" (Medium)**

Document a full-stack project’s system architecture (request flow, database relationships, deployment topology) entirely using Mermaid diagrams embedded in markdown, version-controlled alongside the code. Domain: technical documentation / architecture.

**"Public-Facing Developer Portal" (Hard)**

A polished, standalone documentation site for a public API combining OpenAPI-generated Swagger UI, hand-written getting-started guides, and Mermaid sequence diagrams showing common integration flows (e.g., OAuth handshake). Domain: developer relations / API products.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write an OpenAPI 3.0 spec for 5 endpoints including request/response schemas and error responses.  

* Render your OpenAPI spec with Swagger UI and use its "Try it out" feature against a real running server.  

* Create a Mermaid sequence diagram showing the exact request flow of an authenticated API call, from client to database and back.

# **STAGE 3 — GraphQL (Alternative API Paradigm)**

*Placed last deliberately. Exit B's own interview-prep checklist expects you to answer "REST vs GraphQL tradeoffs" — that comparison is only meaningful once REST (Phase 05\\) and its documentation ecosystem (Stage 2\\) are already solid. Learning GraphQL first would remove the contrast that makes it click.*

## **▌ 7\. GraphQL**

**Core Concepts**

* Single endpoint, client-specified queries — the central contrast with REST's multiple resource endpoints  

* Schema Definition Language (SDL) — types, queries, mutations, resolvers  

* Over-fetching and under-fetching — the specific REST pain points GraphQL addresses  

* N+1 query problem in GraphQL resolvers (ties back to the same problem flagged for Prisma in Phase 05\\) and DataLoader as the standard fix

**Building a GraphQL API**

* Defining a schema — types, Query, Mutation root types  

* Writing resolvers — connecting schema fields to actual data (ties to Phase 05's Prisma/PostgreSQL layer)  

* Apollo Server or a comparable server library — setup and integration with Express (Phase 05, Stage 1\\)

**Client-Side GraphQL**

* Querying a GraphQL API — comparing the request shape to REST calls from Phase 04's TanStack Query/tRPC  

* Apollo Client or urql — caching model differences vs TanStack Query's REST-oriented caching (Phase 04\\)  

* GraphQL Playground / GraphiQL — the GraphQL-native equivalent of Swagger UI (Stage 2, Topic 5\\) for exploring a schema interactively

**REST vs GraphQL — The Interview-Ready Comparison**

* When REST's simplicity and cacheability win (public APIs, simple CRUD, HTTP-level caching)  

* When GraphQL's flexibility wins (complex nested data, multiple client types with different data needs, mobile bandwidth constraints)  

* Why most production systems use both rather than picking one exclusively  

* Articulating this tradeoff clearly — this is explicitly one of the things Exit B expects you to be able to answer live

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — GraphQL (Alternative API Paradigm)  (12 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| GraphQL | 12 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Blog GraphQL API" (Medium)**

A GraphQL API (posts, authors, comments) with proper resolver structure, demonstrating the N+1 problem occurring naturally, then fixed with DataLoader-style batching. Domain: publishing/content.

**"REST vs GraphQL Head-to-Head" (Medium–Hard)**

Implement the identical feature (a nested product/reviews/author data fetch) as both a REST endpoint and a GraphQL query, measuring over-fetching/under-fetching and request-count differences with real network data, and write a decision-framework document for when to choose each. Domain: API architecture.

**"Federated Multi-Service GraphQL Gateway" (Hard)**

Split a schema across 2 independent services (e.g., Users service and Orders service) and compose them behind a single gateway, demonstrating schema federation concepts. Domain: microservices/API architecture.

**Practice Exercises**

* Reproduce the N+1 problem in a GraphQL resolver on purpose, then fix it and prove the query count dropped.  

* Write a GraphQL query that fetches deeply nested data in one round-trip and compare it to the equivalent REST call count.  

* Add input validation and proper error formatting to a GraphQL mutation.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "OpenGateway" — A Fully Documented, Dual-Paradigm Public API Product**

Take an existing backend (Phase 5’s "Ledger" works well) and ship it as a genuine API product: a complete hand-written OpenAPI 3.0 spec rendered via Swagger UI, Mermaid-diagrammed architecture and auth-flow documentation, a parallel GraphQL interface over the same data exposing at least one N+1-fixed resolver, a curated Postman collection with environments and auto-refreshing auth, and a written REST-vs-GraphQL decision document explaining which consumers should use which interface and why. Domain: developer platforms / API products. Why it matters: documenting and exposing an API well is a distinct, high-value skill from building it — this proves you can make your work usable by other engineers, not just functional.

# **Exit Point B — What You Have When This Phase Is Done**

*Restated from the official curriculum, mapped to where each piece was built in this resequenced version.*

* Everything from Exit A (Phase 06\\) — deployed app, green CI badge, Docker, tests  

* OpenAPI-documented API (Stage 2, Topic 4\\)  

* Live Swagger UI link, not a screenshot (Stage 2, Topic 5\\)  

* Postman collection exported and linked in your README (Stage 1, Topic 2\\)  

* Mermaid architecture/auth-flow/ER diagrams in your README (Stage 2, Topic 6\\)  

* GraphQL knowledge — schema, resolvers, and the REST-vs-GraphQL tradeoff comparison (Stage 3\\)

Target: SDE-1 at API-first or developer-tool companies, backend roles at fintech/edtech/SaaS startups. ₹8–12 LPA. The official curriculum flags this explicitly: Exit B is only \~2 weeks past Exit A — worth pushing through rather than stopping applications at Exit A alone.

# **Summary: The Full Sequence at a Glance**

| Stage | Focus | Topics |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | Manual HTTP Tooling | curl → Postman → Insomnia |
| 2 | API Documentation | OpenAPI 3.0 → Swagger UI → Mermaid |
| 3 | Alternative API Paradigm | GraphQL |

**MILESTONE · EXIT POINT 1**

*After Phase 08 · Month \~7 · Junior-to-Mid Full-Stack (Interview-Ready)*

**WHAT YOU HAVE**

All of Exit B \+ algorithmic problem-solving (can pass DSA rounds), system design fundamentals, interview frameworks. First exit where you're competitive in the full interview loop — not just portfolio review.

**JOB TITLES TO USE ON APPLICATIONS**

Software Engineer (SDE-1)  ·  Full-Stack Engineer  ·  Product Engineer  ·  Software Developer

**ROLES TO APPLY FOR**

SDE-1 at funded startups (Series A/B)

Software Engineer I at mid-size product companies

Associate Product Engineer at PLG SaaS companies

Full-Stack Engineer at companies explicitly using Next.js, tRPC, or PostgreSQL

**COMPANIES TO TARGET (INDIA · BANGALORE)**

Series A/B product startups: Slintel, Porter, Slice, Groww (junior), Niyo, Jupiter

B2B SaaS: Leadsquared, WebEngage, CleverTap, MoEngage (junior track)

Developer tools: Tooljet, Appsmith, Pieces.app

HR/Fintech: Darwinbox, Keka, Kredivo, Fi Money

Bangalore-based YC startups (filter Wellfound: India \+ Series A \+ eng team \\\< 50\\)

Calsoft vendor network via your aunt — referral converts at 3–5x application rate

**WHAT TO HIGHLIGHT**

Portfolio project (ClientSync) with live URL, auth, real-time feature, CI badge

GitHub streak / recent commit activity (last 30 days matters to reviewers)

2–3 dev.to posts written during the curriculum (shows communication ability)

tRPC \+ Next.js in tech stack — many JDs now explicitly list these

DSA: LeetCode profile showing solved Easy/Medium problems in the last month

**WHERE TO APPLY**

Wellfound — primary for startups, filter by stack (Next.js, TypeScript, PostgreSQL)

Instahyre — Indian product companies, strong filter options

LinkedIn Easy Apply — volume play, 10–15 applications/day

Referrals — highest conversion, use aunt's Calsoft network \+ any contacts from dev.to readers

Twitter/X dev community — founders often hire from replies and threads

**WHAT THEY'LL TEST — ROUND 1: DSA (45–60 MIN)**

1–2 LeetCode Medium problems (arrays, hashmaps, two pointers, sliding window). Time complexity \+ space complexity explanations required. Target: solve Easy in \\\<15 min, Medium in \\\<30 min.

**ROUND 2 — TECHNICAL (60–90 MIN)**

React: hooks, reconciler, when to use Context vs Zustand

Next.js: App Router vs Pages Router, Server Components, when to use each

**ROUND 3 — SYSTEM DESIGN (30–45 MIN, SOME COMPANIES SKIP AT SDE-1)**
