# **STAGE 2 — Embeddings & Vector Storage**

*Requires OpenAI API access (Stage 1\\) for the embedding model itself, and Phase 05/11's PostgreSQL depth as the storage layer these vectors live in.*

## **▌ 4\. text-embedding-3-small**

*Requires the OpenAI API (Stage 1, Topic 1\\) as the access mechanism — embeddings are just another API call, conceptually, but the output (a vector) is a fundamentally different kind of data than a chat completion.*

* What an embedding actually is — a fixed-length vector representing semantic meaning  

* Why "small" — the cost/dimensionality tradeoff vs larger embedding models  

* Cosine similarity as the standard way to compare two embeddings for semantic closeness  

* Batching embedding requests for efficiency

## **▌ 5\. pgvector**

*Requires embeddings (4) to have vectors worth storing, and Phase 05/11's PostgreSQL depth as the database these vectors are stored in.*

* The vector column type — storing embeddings directly in PostgreSQL rows alongside relational data  

* Distance operators — L2, cosine distance, inner product — and which matches text-embedding-3-small's intended usage  

* Why storing vectors in PostgreSQL (vs a dedicated vector database) is often the right call — no new infrastructure, joins with existing relational data (ties back to Phase 05's schema design)  

* Basic similarity search — ORDER BY embedding \\\<=\\\> query\\\_vector LIMIT n

## **▌ 6\. IVFFlat Index**

*Requires pgvector (5) to exist first — this is one of two index types pgvector supports for making similarity search fast at scale.*

* Why a sequential scan becomes too slow for similarity search as a table grows — the same motivation as B-Tree indexes in Phase 11, applied to vectors  

* IVFFlat's approach — clustering vectors into lists, searching only the nearest clusters  

* Tuning the lists parameter — the recall/speed tradeoff  

* When IVFFlat needs to be rebuilt as data grows

## **▌ 7\. HNSW Index**

*Same family as IVFFlat (6) — an alternative vector index type, placed immediately after for direct comparison.*

* HNSW's approach — a navigable graph structure, vs IVFFlat's clustering approach  

* Build time and memory cost — HNSW is typically more expensive to build than IVFFlat  

* Query performance and recall — why HNSW is often preferred despite the build cost  

* Choosing between IVFFlat and HNSW for a given dataset size and update frequency

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Embeddings & Vector Storage  (9.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| text-embedding-3-small | 2.5 |
| pgvector | 3 |
| IVFFlat Index | 2 |
| HNSW Index | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Semantic Duplicate Detector" (Medium)**

A tool that embeds a set of support tickets or forum posts (text-embedding-3-small) and finds semantic duplicates/near-duplicates using cosine similarity in pgvector, catching duplicates that keyword search would miss. Domain: content moderation / support tooling.

**"HNSW vs IVFFlat Benchmark" (Hard)**

Index the same 100k-vector embedding set with both IVFFlat and HNSW in pgvector, benchmarking query latency and recall accuracy tradeoffs at different index parameters, with a written recommendation document. Domain: vector search infrastructure.

**Practice Exercises**

* Generate embeddings for 20 sentences and manually verify cosine similarity ranks semantically related pairs highest.  

* Set up a pgvector column with an HNSW index and query for nearest neighbors.  

* Compare IVFFlat and HNSW query latency at the same recall target on a mid-size dataset (10k+ vectors).

# **STAGE 3 — RAG (The Synthesis)**

## **▌ 8\. RAG Pattern**

*The largest single topic in the phase (24h official) — genuinely requires everything in Stages 1–2: an LLM API to generate from, embeddings to represent text semantically, and pgvector with a real index to retrieve efficiently.*

**Core Pipeline**

* Chunking strategy — fixed-size, semantic, and hierarchical chunking, and the tradeoffs between them  

* Embedding chunks and storing them via pgvector (Stage 2\\)  

* Retrieval — similarity search against the query's embedding to find relevant chunks  

* Augmentation — inserting retrieved chunks into the LLM prompt as context  

* Generation — the LLM (Stage 1\\) producing a response grounded in retrieved context

**Retrieval Quality**

* Hybrid search — combining vector similarity with keyword search (ties back to Phase 11's PostgreSQL Full Text Search) for better recall  

* Re-ranking — a second pass to reorder retrieved chunks by relevance before sending to the LLM  

* Chunk overlap and metadata — preserving enough context at chunk boundaries

**Production Concerns**

* Latency budgeting across the pipeline — embedding time \+ retrieval time \+ generation time  

* Handling retrieval misses — what the system does when nothing relevant is found  

* Grounding and citation — pointing back to source chunks in the final answer

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — RAG (The Synthesis)  (12 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| RAG Pattern | 12 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Documentation Q\\\&A Bot" (Medium–Hard)**

A RAG system that answers questions about a real technical documentation set (e.g., your own roadmap or an open-source project’s docs) by retrieving relevant chunks via pgvector and grounding the LLM’s answer in them, with citations back to source sections. Domain: developer tools / knowledge management.

**"Multi-Document Research Assistant with Chunking Strategy Comparison" (Hard)**

A RAG pipeline over a corpus of long-form documents (e.g., legal or research PDFs) that compares 3 different chunking strategies (fixed-size, semantic, sliding-window-with-overlap) on retrieval quality for the same question set, with a written evaluation report. Domain: legal-tech / research tooling.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a basic RAG pipeline: chunk a document, embed the chunks, retrieve top-k on a query, and pass them as context to an LLM call.  

* Add citation tracking so the RAG answer references which specific source chunk each claim came from.  

* Compare retrieval quality (precision of retrieved chunks) across 2 different chunk sizes on the same document set.

# **STAGE 4 — SDK & Background Jobs**

*Requires RAG (Stage 3\\) to exist as real work worth simplifying (Vercel AI SDK) and queuing (BullMQ) — introducing these tools before there's a real pipeline to apply them to would make them abstract.*

## **▌ 9\. Vercel AI SDK**

*Deliberately placed after raw API usage (Stage 1\\) and RAG (Stage 3), not before — understanding the raw mechanics first means the SDK is recognized as a convenience wrapper unifying OpenAI/Anthropic calls, not treated as unexplained magic.*

* Unified interface across OpenAI and Anthropic (Stage 1\\) — one API surface for multiple providers  

* Streaming helpers — simplifying the token-by-token streaming already understood from Stage 1  

* useChat and related React hooks (ties back to Phase 02\\) for wiring streaming responses into a UI  

* Tool calling support built into the SDK (foreshadows Stage 6\\)

## **▌ 10\. BullMQ**

*Requires Redis (Phase 09\\) as its backing store, and a real async workload — RAG's embedding/indexing pipeline (Stage 3\\) is exactly the kind of long-running, retry-needing work that justifies a job queue.*

* Why embedding generation and document indexing belong in a background job, not an inline request/response cycle  

* Queue, worker, and job concepts — producer/consumer model  

* Job priority, concurrency limits, and rate limiting API calls to OpenAI/Anthropic (Stage 1\\) to avoid hitting provider rate limits  

* Retry strategies and dead-letter queues (DLQ) for jobs that fail repeatedly  

* Idempotency in job processing — ties back to Phase 09's Razorpay webhook idempotency discussion, same underlying problem

## **▌ 11\. Bull Board**

*Directly depends on BullMQ (10) — a visual dashboard for a queue system that must already exist.*

* Visualizing queue state — active, waiting, completed, failed jobs  

* Manually retrying or removing failed jobs from the dashboard  

* Using this for production AI job monitoring — a concrete artifact showing "production AI, not toy AI," per the official curriculum's own framing

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — SDK & Background Jobs  (8 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Vercel AI SDK | 3 |
| BullMQ | 4 |
| Bull Board | 1 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Streaming AI Chat UI" (Medium)**

A chat interface using the Vercel AI SDK with true token-by-token streaming responses, stop/regenerate controls, and conversation history persistence. Domain: AI product UX.

**"Async AI Batch Processing Pipeline" (Medium–Hard)**

A system where users submit a batch of documents for AI summarization; jobs are queued in BullMQ (not processed synchronously in the request), with a Bull Board dashboard for monitoring job status, retries, and failures. Domain: AI infrastructure / batch processing.

**Practice Exercises**

* Implement streaming AI responses in a chat UI using the Vercel AI SDK and verify tokens appear incrementally, not all at once.  

* Queue an AI processing job in BullMQ, monitor it in Bull Board, and implement a retry policy for transient failures.  

* Implement a "stop generation" button that actually cancels an in-flight streaming AI response.

# **STAGE 5 — Claude-Specific Depth**

*Requires the Anthropic API (Stage 1, Topic 3\\) as the foundation — these three topics are all Claude-specific behavior on top of the basic Messages API already covered.*

## **▌ 12\. Claude Model Tier Strategy**

* Haiku vs Sonnet vs Opus — the cost/capability/speed tradeoff across Anthropic's model tiers  

* Matching a task to the right tier — classification/extraction (cheaper tier) vs complex generation (higher tier)  

* This is the first concrete decision-point that Stage 7's cost tracking will later measure

## **▌ 13\. Extended Thinking**

*Requires the Anthropic API (Stage 1, Topic 3\\) — a Claude-specific reasoning feature.*

* What extended thinking does — allowing the model additional reasoning tokens before producing a final answer  

* When it measurably improves output quality — complex multi-step reasoning tasks  

* Cost and latency tradeoff of enabling it

## **▌ 14\. Prompt Caching**

*Requires API fundamentals from both providers (Stage 1\\) to be understood — prompt caching applies to both OpenAI and Anthropic, though it's frequently discussed in an Anthropic context.*

* What gets cached — typically large, repeated portions of a prompt (system prompts, RAG context from Stage 3\\)  

* Cache hit economics — the cost reduction ROI for repeated context across requests  

* Cache invalidation — when cached content stops being reusable  

* Designing prompts specifically to maximize cacheable portions (structuring stable content before dynamic content)

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Claude-Specific Depth  (5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Claude Model Tier Strategy | 1.5 |
| Extended Thinking | 1.5 |
| Prompt Caching | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Cost-Optimized Multi-Tier Support Bot" (Medium)**

A support bot that routes simple questions to a smaller/cheaper Claude model tier and escalates complex questions to a larger tier, with prompt caching applied to the large, static system prompt/knowledge base portion to cut repeated-request cost. Domain: AI cost engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Measure and document the cost/latency difference between using extended thinking versus not, on a genuinely hard reasoning prompt.  

* Implement prompt caching for a large static system prompt and measure the token-cost reduction on repeated calls.  

* Write a one-page model-tier selection policy (which requests go to which tier and why) for a hypothetical production app.

# **STAGE 6 — Structured Output & Multi-Model Routing**

*Both topics require both providers (Stage 1\\) to be genuinely understood — tool calling is commonly needed across either provider, and routing is meaningless with only one model to route to.*

## **▌ 15\. Tool Calling for Structured Extraction**

*Requires LLM API fundamentals (Stage 1\\) — typically simplified in practice by the Vercel AI SDK (Stage 4, Topic 9), though understanding the raw mechanism first matters.*

* Defining tool/function schemas — parameter types, descriptions the model uses to decide when and how to call a tool  

* The extraction pattern — using tool calling not to actually call a function, but to force the model into producing structured JSON output  

* Validating tool call outputs — ties back to Zod (Phases 01/03/05) for runtime validation of what the model returns  

* Multi-turn tool use — handling a model's tool call, executing it, and returning results for the model to continue reasoning with

## **▌ 16\. Multi-Model Routing**

*Requires both OpenAI (Stage 1, Topics 1–2) and Anthropic (Stage 1, Topic 3; Stage 5\\) to be understood well enough to make a real routing decision between them — this cannot come before both provider tracks exist.*

* Routing logic — sending a request to the model best suited for it, not defaulting to one provider for everything  

* Common routing signals: task complexity, cost sensitivity, latency requirements, specific capability needs (e.g. Claude's extended thinking, Stage 5, vs GPT-4o's multimodal input, Stage 1\\)  

* Fallback strategies — routing to a secondary provider if the primary is rate-limited or down  

* This is the decision-making skill the official curriculum explicitly flags as what separates a real AI engineer from someone who can only call one API

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — Structured Output & Multi-Model Routing  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Tool Calling for Structured Extraction | 3 |
| Multi-Model Routing | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Receipt-to-Structured-Data Extractor" (Medium)**

A tool that takes a photo/text of a receipt and uses tool calling to extract guaranteed-valid structured JSON (line items, totals, tax) validated against a schema, with a test suite of malformed/tricky receipts. Domain: fintech / expense automation.

**"Cost-Aware Multi-Model Router" (Hard)**

A routing layer that sends each incoming request to the cheapest model capable of handling it correctly (classified by a lightweight pre-check), falling back to a stronger model on low-confidence responses, with logged cost savings versus an always-use-the-best-model baseline. Domain: AI infrastructure / cost engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Use tool calling to force an LLM response into a strict JSON schema and validate it with Zod, handling the case where the model still returns malformed output.  

* Build a simple classifier (rule-based or lightweight-model-based) that decides which of 2 models should handle a given request.  

* Log and compare total cost between an always-best-model baseline and your routed approach over the same 50 test requests.

# **STAGE 7 — Cost & Governance**

*Requires real multi-model usage (Stage 6\\) to have actual costs across providers worth tracking and governing — introducing cost tracking before there's real usage would have nothing to measure.*

## **▌ 17\. AI Cost Tracking**

* Token counting — input vs output tokens, and why output tokens typically cost more  

* Per-request cost calculation across different models/providers (Stage 1, Stage 5's tier strategy, Stage 6's routing)  

* Building a cost dashboard — the concrete artifact the official curriculum calls out as something to highlight in a portfolio  

* Attributing cost to specific features/users for accurate reporting

## **▌ 18\. Token Budget Enforcement**

*Requires cost tracking (17) as the data source — you can't enforce a budget without first measuring actual usage.*

* Setting per-user or per-feature token budgets  

* Enforcing budgets before a request is sent (pre-flight estimation) vs after (post-hoc alerting)  

* Graceful degradation when a budget is exceeded — routing to a cheaper model (Stage 6\\) rather than simply failing

### **Stage 7 Projects & Exercises — Cost & Governance  (4 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| AI Cost Tracking | 2 |
| Token Budget Enforcement | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"AI Spend Dashboard with Budget Enforcement" (Medium–Hard)**

A dashboard tracking AI API costs per user/feature in real time, with a hard token-budget enforcement layer that blocks or downgrades requests once a user/org exceeds their allocated monthly budget. Domain: SaaS cost governance / FinOps.

**Practice Exercises**

* Track token usage per request and aggregate it into a per-user daily cost report.  

* Implement a hard budget cap that rejects further AI requests once exceeded, with a clear user-facing message.  

* Write a policy document describing how your app would handle a sudden 10x spike in AI costs.

# **STAGE 8 — Prompt Engineering & Evaluation**

*Requires real AI features to exist (RAG from Stage 3, tool calling from Stage 6\\) — both prompt engineering and evaluation are meaningfully practiced against real, built systems, not in the abstract. Evals are placed genuinely last since you need something built before you can evaluate it.*

## **▌ 19\. System Prompt Engineering**

*Could arguably be introduced earlier, but real prompt engineering skill benefits from having already built RAG (Stage 3\\) and tool calling (Stage 6\\) — there's more to actually engineer prompts around once those systems exist.*

* Structuring system prompts — role definition, constraints, output format instructions  

* Few-shot examples within a prompt — when they measurably improve output vs adding noise  

* Prompt injection awareness — treating user input as untrusted within a prompt (security parallel to Phase 02's XSS/sanitization concerns)  

* Iterating on prompts systematically rather than by trial and error — setting up the mindset Stage 8's evals (20) will formalize

## **▌ 20\. Eval-Driven AI Development**

*Genuinely last — requires real, built AI features (RAG from Stage 3, tool calling from Stage 6, prompts from Topic 19\\) to evaluate. Evaluation is meaningless without something to measure.*

* Building an eval set — representative test cases with expected outputs or grading criteria  

* Metrics that matter beyond "it works": precision/recall for retrieval (Stage 3), exact-match or rubric-based scoring for generation  

* LLM-as-judge — using a model to grade another model's output, and its limitations  

* Regression testing for prompts — catching quality drops when a prompt or model version changes (ties back to Phase 06's CI/CD, running evals as part of a pipeline)  

* This is the practice the official curriculum frames as the actual differentiator in interviews — showing how you measure AI quality, not just that a feature works

### **Stage 8 Projects & Exercises — Prompt Engineering & Evaluation  (9 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| System Prompt Engineering | 3 |
| Eval-Driven AI Development | 6 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Prompt Version Control & Regression Suite" (Medium)**

A system prompt library with versioned prompts and an automated eval suite (a fixed set of test inputs with expected-quality criteria) that runs on every prompt change to catch regressions before deploying a new prompt version. Domain: AI engineering / MLOps.

**"Eval-Driven Support Bot Improvement Cycle" (Hard)**

Take the Stage 5 support bot, build a 30-example golden eval dataset with graded expected outputs, establish a baseline score, then iterate on the system prompt 3 times, re-running the eval each time and documenting the score progression and specific failure modes fixed. Domain: AI quality engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a system prompt for a specific task, then write 10 adversarial test inputs designed to break it, and fix the prompt until all 10 pass.  

* Build a simple automated eval script that scores AI outputs against expected criteria (exact match, contains-keyword, or LLM-graded).  

* Version 3 iterations of the same prompt and document exactly what changed and why, with eval scores for each version.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Sage" — A Cost-Governed, Eval-Tested RAG Support Platform**

A complete production-pattern AI support platform: a documentation-grounded RAG pipeline (pgvector, chunking strategy chosen via comparison testing, citation tracking), a cost-optimized multi-tier Claude routing layer with prompt caching on the static knowledge base, tool-calling-based structured ticket extraction validated with Zod, BullMQ-queued background processing for bulk document ingestion with a Bull Board monitoring dashboard, a real-time AI cost dashboard with hard budget enforcement per organization, and a versioned prompt library backed by an automated golden-dataset eval suite that must pass before any prompt change ships. Domain: enterprise AI / support automation. Why it matters: this is what separates "called an LLM API" from "built a production AI system" — grounding, cost control, and evaluation discipline are the actual hard parts.

# **Exit Point D — What You Have When This Phase Is Done**

*Restated from the official curriculum, mapped to where each piece was built.*

* Everything from Exit ★2 (Phase 11\\)  

* Full RAG pipeline — pgvector \+ BullMQ \+ job queues (this phase, Stages 2–4)  

* Multi-model AI routing — OpenAI GPT-4o \+ Anthropic Claude (this phase, Stages 1, 5–6)  

* Prompt caching (this phase, Stage 5\\)  

* Tool calling for structured extraction (this phase, Stage 6\\)  

* Eval-driven AI development (this phase, Stage 8\\)  

* AI cost tracking and token budget enforcement (this phase, Stage 7\\)  

* Can own the AI feature surface of a product end-to-end

Target: Senior SDE / SDE-2 to SDE-3 transition at AI-native companies, AI Engineer at companies building LLM-powered products, Full-Stack Engineer with AI focus at Series A/B AI startups, Applied Engineer at AI infrastructure companies. Job titles to use: Senior Software Engineer, AI Engineer, Full-Stack AI Engineer, Product Engineer (AI), Applied AI Engineer. India comp: ₹20–30 LPA · US remote: $100–140k.

**CAUTION (from the official curriculum, restated): AI Engineer roles hire on demonstrated judgment about model tradeoffs, not just the ability to call an API. Your portfolio must show a decision — why pgvector over Pinecone (Stage 2), why Claude Haiku for classification and GPT-4o for generation (Stage 5's tier strategy, Stage 6's routing), why BullMQ and not inline processing (Stage 4). Document the decisions, not just the implementation.**

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 13 — Advanced Browser APIs \+ Collaboration, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 13 budget, the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–12 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**SOURCE DISCREPANCY: The official document header states "4 WEEKS · 152 HOURS," but the topic table's own PHASE TOTAL row sums to 110 hours (70h learn \+ 7h problems \+ 17h project \+ 16h ClientSync \\= 110h, not 152h). Consistent with the same kind of header/table mismatch already flagged in Phases 09–12 — noted here rather than silently picking one number.**

**ORDERING CORRECTIONS: Two topics are reordered from the official list. (1) requestAnimationFrame is moved ahead of View Transitions API — rAF is the lower-level timing primitive; View Transitions is a higher-level browser feature that assumes the same rendering-pipeline timing concepts. (2) ProseMirror is moved ahead of Tiptap — Tiptap is a wrapper built directly on top of ProseMirror, the same raw-before-wrapper pattern already applied elsewhere in this series (Node's http module before Express, curl before Postman, the OpenAI API before the Vercel AI SDK).**

This phase does not end at a named Exit Point in the official curriculum — it sits between Exit D (Phase 12\\) and Exit E (later). Resequenced by real dependency: client-side storage → parallelism (Web Workers) → cross-tab communication → browser rendering/animation internals → the Yjs real-time collaboration stack (the phase's centerpiece, and the official Build in Public moment) → rich text editors, with the low-level toolkit (ProseMirror) taught before the high-level wrapper built on it (Tiptap).

# **STAGE 1 — Client-Side Storage**

*Requires Phase 02's localStorage/sessionStorage as the point of comparison — IndexedDB is what those simpler APIs can't handle: large, structured, queryable client-side data.*

## **▌ 1\. IndexedDB**

**Core Concepts**

* Why IndexedDB exists — localStorage's synchronous, string-only, size-limited model breaks down for real offline-capable apps  

* Databases, object stores, and transactions — IndexedDB's structural model  

* Keys and key paths — auto-incrementing keys vs application-defined keys

**Working with Data**

* CRUD operations via the raw IndexedDB API — verbose, callback/event-based by design  

* Indexes — querying by a field other than the primary key  

* Cursors — iterating over large result sets without loading everything into memory  

* Versioning and schema migrations (onupgradeneeded)

**Practical Use**

* Offline-first data caching — storing API responses locally for offline access (parallels Phase 06b's AsyncStorage discussion, browser equivalent)  

* Storage limits and eviction — what happens when the browser needs to reclaim space

## **▌ 2\. idb**

*Directly depends on IndexedDB (1) — idb is a small Promise-based wrapper around the same raw API, removing the callback/event boilerplate.*

* Why idb exists — IndexedDB's raw API is unusually verbose and callback-heavy for a modern codebase  

* Promise-based CRUD operations — the same concepts as Topic 1, with async/await instead of event listeners  

* When to use the raw API directly vs idb — idb covers the vast majority of real use cases

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Client-Side Storage  (5.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| IndexedDB | 4 |
| idb | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Offline Recipe Box" (Easy–Medium)**

A recipe-saving app that stores full recipe data in IndexedDB (via the idb wrapper) so it works completely offline after first load, including full-text search over locally stored recipes. Domain: lifestyle / offline-first apps.

**"Local-First Bookmark Manager with Sync" (Hard)**

A bookmark manager storing data primarily in IndexedDB with a background sync-to-server layer, handling the case where local and server data diverge while offline, including a documented conflict-resolution strategy. Domain: productivity tools.

**Practice Exercises**

* Store 1,000+ structured records in IndexedDB via idb and build an indexed query (not a full table scan) for a specific field.  

* Build an app that fully functions with the network disabled (DevTools offline mode) using only IndexedDB-stored data.  

* Implement a sync queue that flushes pending local changes to a server once connectivity returns.

# **STAGE 2 — Parallelism**

## **▌ 3\. Web Workers**

*Independent of Stage 1 — background thread execution, foundational for offloading expensive work off the main thread.*

* Why the main thread matters — JavaScript's single-threaded execution model and what blocks it (ties back to Phase 01's event loop)  

* Creating a worker, postMessage/onmessage communication — the structured-clone messaging model  

* What workers can and can't access — no DOM access, separate global scope  

* Use cases: expensive computation (parsing, image processing), keeping the UI responsive during heavy work

## **▌ 4\. comlink**

*Directly depends on Web Workers (3) — comlink is an RPC-style wrapper that removes the manual postMessage/onmessage boilerplate.*

* Why raw postMessage-based worker communication gets unwieldy for anything beyond trivial message-passing  

* comlink's proxy-based API — calling worker functions as if they were local async functions  

* When the raw API (3) is still clearer vs when comlink's abstraction is worth adopting

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Parallelism  (4.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Web Workers | 3 |
| comlink | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"CPU-Heavy Data Processing Without UI Freeze" (Medium)**

A tool that processes a large CSV/JSON file (parsing, aggregation) entirely inside a Web Worker using comlink for clean async communication, keeping the UI thread responsive throughout — with a visible before/after (UI freezes without the worker, doesn’t with it).

**Practice Exercises**

* Move a CPU-intensive function into a Web Worker using comlink and prove the main thread stays responsive during execution.  

* Benchmark the same heavy computation on the main thread vs. in a worker and document the UI responsiveness difference (e.g., input lag measurement).

# **STAGE 3 — Cross-Context Communication**

## **▌ 5\. Broadcast Channel API**

*Grouped after Workers (Stage 2\\) since both are fundamentally about communication between separate execution contexts — workers talk to the main thread, Broadcast Channel lets tabs/windows talk to each other.*

* Creating a named channel and posting messages to it  

* All same-origin tabs/windows/workers subscribed to the same channel receive the message — the key difference from postMessage's point-to-point model  

* Use cases: syncing auth state across tabs (logging out in one tab logs out all tabs, ties back to Phase 05's JWT/session handling), syncing UI state across multiple open windows of the same app

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Cross-Context Communication  (1.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Broadcast Channel API | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Cross-Tab Session Sync" (Medium)**

A demo app where logging out in one browser tab immediately logs the user out in all other open tabs of the same app, using the Broadcast Channel API. Domain: security/session management UX.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a cross-tab "you have unsaved changes in another tab" warning system using Broadcast Channel.  

* Sync a simple piece of UI state (e.g., dark mode toggle) instantly across all open tabs.

# **STAGE 4 — Browser Rendering & Animation Internals**

*A cluster of APIs concerned with how and when the browser paints — ordered from the lowest-level timing primitive (rAF) to the most advanced/niche (CSS Houdini).*

## **▌ 6\. requestAnimationFrame**

*Moved ahead of View Transitions API (7) from the official ordering — rAF is the foundational timing primitive that browser rendering concepts build on, including conceptually informing what View Transitions does under the hood.*

* Why requestAnimationFrame exists — syncing JavaScript-driven animation with the browser's actual repaint cycle, instead of guessing with setTimeout (Phase 01\\)  

* The callback timing guarantee — running right before the next repaint  

* Canceling a scheduled frame (cancelAnimationFrame)  

* Common pattern: using rAF for smooth, manually-driven animations (scroll-linked effects, custom easing)

## **▌ 7\. requestIdleCallback**

*Same timing-API family as rAF (6) — scheduling work during idle time instead of every animation frame.*

* The difference from rAF — rIC runs when the browser is otherwise idle, not on every frame  

* Use cases: low-priority background work (analytics batching, prefetching) that shouldn't compete with rendering or user input  

* The timeout option — forcing execution even if the browser never reports idle time  

* Browser support caveats and Safari's lack of support (practical consideration)

## **▌ 8\. View Transitions API**

*Requires the rendering-pipeline timing concepts from rAF (6) as context, even though View Transitions is a much higher-level, declarative API.*

* document.startViewTransition() — the core API for triggering a transition between two DOM states  

* How the browser captures before/after snapshots and animates between them automatically  

* Named transitions (view-transition-name) for controlling which elements get their own distinct animation  

* Use cases: page transitions in an SPA (Phase 02's App Router), smooth state changes without hand-rolled animation logic

## **▌ 9\. CSS Houdini**

*The most advanced and niche topic in this cluster — placed last since it requires comfort with the browser's rendering pipeline (6–8) to be meaningful.*

* What Houdini exposes — low-level access to the CSS rendering pipeline (paint, layout, properties) that was previously entirely opaque to developers  

* CSS Paint API — defining custom paint worklets for background-image-style custom rendering  

* Properties and Values API — registering custom CSS properties with actual types (not just strings), enabling smooth animation of previously non-animatable values  

* Realistic scope — Houdini's browser support and practical adoption level today, versus its role as a forward-looking spec

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Browser Rendering & Animation Internals  (7.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| requestAnimationFrame | 2 |
| requestIdleCallback | 1.5 |
| View Transitions API | 2 |
| CSS Houdini | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"60fps Custom Physics Animation" (Medium–Hard)**

A hand-built physics-based animation (e.g., a bouncing/dragging card with momentum) using requestAnimationFrame correctly (delta-time based, not frame-count based) and requestIdleCallback for non-critical background updates, profiled to confirm consistent 60fps. Domain: creative/interactive UI.

**"Native Page Transition Gallery" (Hard)**

A multi-page image gallery using the View Transitions API for smooth native page-to-page morph animations (image expanding into a detail view), with a custom CSS Houdini paint worklet used for one decorative element (e.g., a custom-drawn border or background pattern). Domain: creative/media portfolios.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a requestAnimationFrame loop that is correctly delta-time-based so animation speed is independent of frame rate.  

* Use requestIdleCallback to defer a non-critical UI update and prove (via performance trace) it doesn’t block user interaction.  

* Implement a View Transitions API page navigation and document the browsers where it degrades gracefully vs. natively works.  

* Write one simple CSS Houdini paint worklet and use it as a custom background on an element.

# **STAGE 5 — Real-Time Collaboration: The Yjs Stack**

*The centerpiece of this phase, and the official curriculum's designated Build in Public moment. Requires WebSockets and WebRTC (Phase 11\\) as transport context, and IndexedDB (Stage 1\\) as a persistence context — Yjs itself is transport- and storage-agnostic, and only makes sense once you understand what it's being connected to.*

## **▌ 10\. Yjs**

**CRDT Fundamentals**

* What a CRDT (Conflict-free Replicated Data Type) is — data structures that can be modified concurrently by multiple users and always converge to the same state without central coordination  

* Why this matters for real-time collaboration — no server-side operational transform logic needed, unlike older collaborative editing approaches  

* Shared types — Y.Text, Y.Array, Y.Map — the building blocks for collaborative documents

**Working with Yjs**

* Documents (Y.Doc) as the root container for shared state  

* Observing changes — subscribing to updates from remote peers  

* Awareness — sharing ephemeral state (cursor position, user presence) separately from document content, exactly what powers the "two cursors editing live" demo the official curriculum calls out as the portfolio moment

**Transport-Agnostic Design**

* Yjs itself doesn't handle networking or persistence — it's a pure CRDT implementation, requiring a provider (11–13) to actually sync or persist

## **▌ 11\. y-websocket**

*Directly depends on Yjs (10) and WebSockets (Phase 11\\) — one of two transport providers for syncing a Y.Doc between peers.*

* Setting up a y-websocket server for centralized sync  

* Client-side connection and automatic reconnection  

* When a centralized WebSocket relay is the right transport choice — simpler infrastructure, works behind NATs without STUN/TURN

## **▌ 12\. y-webrtc**

*Same category as y-websocket (11) — the second transport provider, using WebRTC (Phase 11\\) for peer-to-peer sync instead of a central relay.*

* Peer-to-peer document sync — no central server required for the actual data flow, only for signaling (ties directly back to Phase 11's WebRTC signaling discussion)  

* Tradeoffs vs y-websocket: no server infrastructure cost, but NAT traversal reliability and scaling to many peers become harder

## **▌ 13\. y-indexeddb**

*Requires both Yjs (10) and IndexedDB (Stage 1, Topic 1\\) — this is a persistence provider, not a transport provider, storing the CRDT state locally for offline access.*

* Persisting a Y.Doc to IndexedDB (Stage 1\\) so document state survives a page reload or offline period  

* Combining with a transport provider (11 or 12\\) — local persistence and network sync are independent, composable concerns in Yjs's design  

* Offline-first collaborative editing — making local edits while offline, syncing automatically once a transport reconnects

## **▌ 14\. Hocuspocus**

*Requires Yjs and its transport providers (10–13) to be understood — Hocuspocus is a production-ready backend server framework built specifically around Yjs, wrapping the concepts from y-websocket (11) into something deployable.*

* Why a dedicated server framework — running y-websocket directly works for prototypes, but production use needs auth, persistence hooks, and scaling considerations Hocuspocus provides  

* Authentication hooks — connecting document access to your existing auth system (Phase 05's JWT)  

* Persistence hooks — connecting Hocuspocus to a real database (PostgreSQL, Phase 05\\) instead of relying solely on in-memory or IndexedDB (13) state  

* Extensions — Hocuspocus's plugin model for logging, webhooks, and custom persistence backends

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Real-Time Collaboration: The Yjs Stack  (12.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Yjs | 6 |
| y-websocket | 2 |
| y-webrtc | 1.5 |
| y-indexeddb | 1 |
| Hocuspocus | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Collaborative Sticky-Note Board" (Medium–Hard)**

A multi-user sticky-note board (like a mini Miro) with real-time collaborative editing via Yjs \+ y-websocket, showing live cursors/presence and conflict-free concurrent edits to note position and text. Domain: collaborative productivity tools.

**"Offline-Capable Collaborative Doc with Peer Fallback" (Hard)**

A collaborative text document combining y-indexeddb (offline persistence), y-websocket (primary sync via a Hocuspocus server), and y-webrtc as a peer-to-peer fallback when the server is unreachable, demonstrating that edits made offline merge correctly once any sync path reconnects. Domain: collaborative software infrastructure.

**Practice Exercises**

* Set up a Yjs document synced via y-websocket between two browser tabs and prove concurrent edits merge without data loss.  

* Add y-indexeddb persistence and prove a Yjs document survives a full page reload with pending offline changes intact.  

* Set up a Hocuspocus server and connect a Yjs client to it, documenting the difference from a bare y-websocket server.

# **STAGE 6 — Rich Text Editors**

*Moved from the official ordering — ProseMirror is taught before Tiptap, not after, since Tiptap is a wrapper built directly on top of ProseMirror. This follows the same raw-before-wrapper pattern used throughout this series.*

## **▌ 15\. ProseMirror**

*The low-level toolkit — placed first because Tiptap (16) is built directly on top of it, and understanding the primitive first means Tiptap is recognized as a convenience layer, not treated as the whole picture.*

* Document schema — ProseMirror's strict, defined-in-advance model of what content is valid (a deliberate contrast to contentEditable's anything-goes DOM)  

* Node views and marks — block-level content vs inline formatting  

* Transactions and plugins — ProseMirror's model for making and intercepting document changes  

* Why ProseMirror is famously powerful but low-level — most teams don't use it directly, which motivates Tiptap (16)

## **▌ 16\. Tiptap**

*Directly built on ProseMirror (15) — a higher-level, extension-based API over the same underlying document model.*

* Extension-based architecture — composing an editor from discrete extensions (Bold, Heading, BulletList, etc.), each ultimately a ProseMirror node/mark/plugin under the hood  

* React integration (@tiptap/react) — wiring Tiptap into a component-based UI (Phase 02\\)  

* Collaborative editing extension — connecting Tiptap directly to Yjs (Stage 5), the practical convergence point of this entire phase: a rich text editor with live multi-cursor collaboration  

* Custom extensions — building your own when Tiptap's built-in set isn't enough, which requires the ProseMirror concepts (15) underneath

# **Build in Public — This Phase's Portfolio Moment**

*Restated from the official curriculum.*

* Post: a demo of real-time collaboration (Yjs) — two cursors editing the same document live. This is the direct payoff of Stage 5's Awareness feature (Topic 10\\) combined with Stage 6's Tiptap collaboration extension (Topic 16).  

* GitHub: this is called out as a strong portfolio moment specifically — lead the repo README with this feature, not bury it.  

* Where \+ when: LinkedIn, as a video demo, at the end of this phase.

### **Stage 6 Projects & Exercises — Rich Text Editors  (9 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| ProseMirror | 4 |
| Tiptap | 5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Notion-Style Block Editor" (Hard)**

A rich text editor built with Tiptap (on ProseMirror) supporting headings, lists, code blocks, and a custom block type you build yourself (e.g., an embeddable "callout" box), with full keyboard shortcuts and markdown-style input rules (typing "\# " converts to a heading). Domain: productivity / content tools.

**"Collaborative Rich Text Editor" (Hard, combines Stage 5 \+ 6\\)**

Integrate Tiptap with Yjs for real-time collaborative rich-text editing with live cursors showing collaborator names/colors — a genuine Google-Docs-style editing experience built from open primitives. Domain: collaborative software.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a custom Tiptap node/extension (e.g., a mention @user feature or a custom callout block).  

* Implement a markdown input rule (e.g., typing "\\\* " or "1. " auto-converts to a list) in a ProseMirror/Tiptap editor.  

* Wire Tiptap to a Yjs document and verify two browser tabs editing the same document show live, correctly-attributed cursors.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Confluence Lite" — A Real-Time Collaborative Document Workspace**

A Notion/Google-Docs-style collaborative workspace: a Tiptap-based rich text editor with custom block extensions, fully wired to Yjs for real-time collaborative editing (y-websocket primary sync via a Hocuspocus server, y-indexeddb for offline persistence, y-webrtc as a peer-to-peer fallback), live cursor presence, cross-tab session sync via Broadcast Channel, CPU-heavy operations (e.g., full-document search indexing) offloaded to a Web Worker via comlink so typing never lags, and smooth View-Transitions-powered navigation between documents. Domain: collaborative productivity software. Why it matters: real-time collaborative editing is one of the most technically demanding categories of web application — building a working version from open primitives (not a SaaS embed) is a genuine senior-level achievement.

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 14 — Load Testing \+ Security Deep Dive, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 14 budget, the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–13 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**SOURCE DISCREPANCY: The official document header states "3 WEEKS · 119 HOURS," but the topic table's own PHASE TOTAL row sums to 72 hours (47h learn \+ 10h problems \+ 4h project \+ 11h ClientSync \\= 72h, not 119h). Consistent with the same kind of header/table mismatch already flagged in Phases 09–13 — noted here rather than silently picking one number.**

**OVERLAP NOTICE: GDPR (Topic 15 here) was already touched in Phase 10 as "GDPR Consent Mode" — scoped narrowly to analytics/ tracking consent. This phase's GDPR topic is broader (data subject rights, breach notification, lawful basis for processing) and is not a re-teach of Phase 10's consent-banner mechanics. Treated here as a distinct extension, flagged rather than silently duplicated.**

This phase does not end at a named Exit Point — it sits between Exit D (Phase 12\\) and Exit E (later), refining production readiness rather than unlocking a new tier. Resequenced by real dependency: load testing → the security threat model (OWASP Top 10\\) before the tools that scan for or mitigate specific items on it → secret management (detecting existing leaks before setting up prevention) → lightweight automated security monitoring → compliance, which needs the technical security work above as context for what's actually being attested to.

# **STAGE 1 — Load Testing**

*Requires a real, deployed app (Phase 06\\) — load testing has nothing to test against before then.*

## **▌ 1\. k6**

**Core Concepts**

* Virtual users (VUs) — simulating concurrent traffic, not just sequential requests  

* Writing test scripts in JavaScript — k6's scripting model  

* Stages — ramping traffic up and down over the course of a test, rather than a flat constant load

**Test Types**

* Load testing — expected normal-to-peak traffic  

* Stress testing — pushing past expected capacity to find the breaking point  

* Spike testing — sudden traffic bursts (ties back to real scenarios: a product launch, a viral post)  

* Soak testing — sustained load over a long duration, surfacing memory leaks (ties back to Phase 02's memory leak topic) that only show up over time

**Analyzing Results**

* Reading k6's output — response times (p95/p99, not just average), error rates, throughput  

* Correlating load test results with the observability stack from Phase 10 — watching Grafana/Sentry while the load test runs, not just reading k6's own summary  

* Identifying the actual bottleneck — application code, database (Phase 05/11), connection pool (PgBouncer, Phase 09), or infrastructure

## **▌ 2\. k6 Cloud**

*Directly depends on k6 (1) — the hosted platform for running the same k6 scripts at larger scale with better result visualization.*

* Running k6 scripts from multiple geographic load zones — testing latency for real-world distributed users, not just from one machine  

* Result dashboards and historical trend comparison across test runs  

* When local k6 is sufficient vs when cloud-scale load generation is actually needed

## **▌ 3\. Artillery**

*Same category as k6 (1) — an alternative load testing tool, placed immediately after for direct comparison.*

* YAML-based test definitions vs k6's JavaScript scripting — a different authoring philosophy  

* Artillery's plugin ecosystem for protocol support beyond plain HTTP (WebSockets, ties back to Phase 11/13's real-time work)  

* When Artillery's approach fits better than k6's — teams preferring declarative config over scripted tests

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Load Testing  (8.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| k6 | 5 |
| k6 Cloud | 1.5 |
| Artillery | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"API Breaking Point Report" (Medium)**

Use k6 to load-test an earlier project’s API, ramping virtual users until it breaks, and produce a written report identifying the exact bottleneck (CPU, DB connections, memory) with graphs. Domain: performance engineering / SRE.

**"Cloud-Scale Load Test with k6 Cloud" (Medium–Hard)**

Run a distributed load test from multiple geographic regions using k6 Cloud, comparing latency experienced by users in different regions and documenting a CDN/edge-caching recommendation based on findings.

**"Realistic User-Behavior Load Simulation with Artillery" (Hard)**

Model a realistic multi-step user journey (browse → search → add to cart → checkout) as an Artillery scenario with weighted random behavior (not just hammering one endpoint), simulating a realistic traffic spike (e.g., a flash sale) and documenting system behavior under it.

**Practice Exercises**

* Write a k6 script that ramps from 0 to 500 virtual users over 2 minutes and identify the exact user count where error rate exceeds 1%.  

* Compare p50/p95/p99 latency under load and explain why p99 matters more than average for user experience.  

* Build an Artillery scenario with at least 3 weighted request types simulating realistic traffic mix.

# **STAGE 2 — Security Threat Model & Scanning**

*OWASP Top 10 is placed first in this stage deliberately — it's the threat model everything else in this stage addresses. Learning ZAP, CSP, or JWT pinning before understanding what threats they mitigate would teach tool operation without the reasoning behind it.*

## **▌ 4\. OWASP Top 10**

*The conceptual foundation for the rest of this stage — every other topic here is a specific tool or mitigation for one or more items on this list.*

* Broken Access Control — the most common real-world vulnerability, and how it shows up in practice (ties back to Phase 05's auth/authorization patterns)  

* Cryptographic Failures — weak hashing, exposed secrets (foreshadows Stage 3\\)  

* Injection — SQL injection specifically (ties back to Phase 05's Prisma usage, which parameterizes queries by default — understanding why that matters)  

* Insecure Design — a threat category about architecture decisions, not just code bugs  

* Security Misconfiguration — default credentials, verbose error messages, unnecessary exposed endpoints  

* Vulnerable and Outdated Components — foreshadows Stage 4's Dependabot/npm audit  

* Identification and Authentication Failures — connects directly to Phase 05's JWT/bcrypt/session work  

* Software and Data Integrity Failures — insecure deserialization, unsigned updates  

* Security Logging and Monitoring Failures — connects back to Phase 10's structured logging and Sentry work  

* Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) — a newer addition to the list, relevant to any backend that fetches URLs on a user's behalf

## **▌ 5\. OWASP ZAP**

*Requires OWASP Top 10 (4) as the conceptual framework it scans against — ZAP's findings only make sense once you know what category of issue each one maps to.*

* Automated vulnerability scanning — passive scanning (observing traffic) vs active scanning (actively probing for issues)  

* Running ZAP against your deployed app (Phase 06\\) or a staging environment — never production without deliberate care  

* Interpreting ZAP's findings — triaging real vulnerabilities against false positives  

* Integrating ZAP into CI (Phase 06's GitHub Actions) for continuous scanning

## **▌ 6\. Nonce-Based CSP**

*A specific mitigation for XSS (an OWASP Top 10 item, 4\\) — requires that context, and builds directly on Phase 05's Helmet.js, which sets basic CSP headers without the nonce refinement covered here.*

* Content Security Policy recap from Phase 05's Helmet.js — the baseline this topic extends  

* Why 'unsafe-inline' defeats much of CSP's protection, and why nonces exist to allow specific inline scripts without that blanket exception  

* Generating a per-request nonce and threading it through to both the CSP header and the script tags that need it (a real implementation challenge in a Next.js/Server Component context, Phase 02\\)  

* Testing that a nonce-based policy actually blocks unauthorized inline scripts

## **▌ 7\. JWT Algorithm Pinning**

*A specific mitigation for an authentication vulnerability class (OWASP Top 10, 4\\) — requires Phase 05's JWT knowledge directly, since this topic is about a specific flaw in naive JWT verification.*

* The algorithm confusion attack — a token signed with a weaker or different algorithm than the server expects, and how naive verification code can be tricked into accepting it  

* Explicitly pinning the expected algorithm (e.g. only ever accepting HS256, never letting the token's own header dictate which algorithm to verify with) — directly extends Phase 05's HMAC-SHA256/JWT signing discussion  

* Why this matters specifically for the jose/jsonwebtoken libraries introduced in Phase 05 — checking that the algorithm allowlist is actually being enforced in your own auth code

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Security Threat Model & Scanning  (12.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| OWASP Top 10 | 6 |
| OWASP ZAP | 2.5 |
| Nonce-Based CSP | 2.5 |
| JWT Algorithm Pinning | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"OWASP Top 10 Self-Audit" (Medium–Hard)**

Take an earlier full-stack project and systematically test it against every applicable OWASP Top 10 category, documenting findings and fixes for at least 3 real vulnerabilities discovered (not hypothetical). Domain: application security.

**"Automated Vulnerability Scanning Pipeline" (Medium)**

Integrate OWASP ZAP into a CI pipeline to automatically scan a staging deployment on every merge, failing the build on high-severity findings. Domain: DevSecOps.

**"Nonce-Based CSP Hardening" (Hard)**

Implement a strict, nonce-based Content Security Policy on a real app (no unsafe-inline), correctly generating a fresh nonce per request and wiring it through server-rendered script tags, verified with a CSP evaluator and a deliberate XSS-attempt test that gets blocked.

**Practice Exercises**

* Find and document one real OWASP Top 10 vulnerability in a project you built, then fix it and re-verify.  

* Run OWASP ZAP against a staging environment and triage the findings by real severity (not just accepting the raw report).  

* Implement JWT algorithm pinning (explicitly rejecting "alg: none" and unexpected algorithms) and prove an algorithm-confusion attack is blocked.

# **STAGE 3 — Secret Management**

*Ordered detection-before-prevention — establishing whether a leak has already happened (trufflehog) before setting up a mechanism to stop future ones (git-secrets), the same threat-first-then-tool logic used in Stage 2\.*

## **▌ 8\. trufflehog**

* Scanning git history — not just the current state of a repo, but every commit ever made, since a secret removed in a later commit is still recoverable from history  

* What trufflehog looks for — high-entropy strings, known credential patterns (API keys, private keys)  

* Running trufflehog against your own repos (all phases so far) as a real audit, not a hypothetical exercise  

* What to do if a real leak is found — rotating the credential immediately, not just removing it from the file (removing from a file does not remove it from git history)

## **▌ 9\. git-secrets**

*The preventive counterpart to trufflehog (8) — placed after it since establishing whether a leak already exists matters before setting up prevention going forward.*

* Pre-commit hook that blocks a commit containing a pattern matching known secret formats  

* Configuring patterns — built-in AWS key patterns plus custom patterns for your own project's secret formats  

* Combining with Husky (a natural extension of Phase 01's Git tooling work) to enforce this automatically for every commit, not just when remembered manually

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Secret Management  (3 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| trufflehog | 1.5 |
| git-secrets | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Secret Leak Prevention Pipeline" (Medium)**

Configure trufflehog and git-secrets to scan every commit (locally via pre-commit hook and in CI) for accidentally committed secrets, with a deliberate test commit containing a fake API key that gets correctly blocked.

**Practice Exercises**

* Intentionally commit a fake secret, then use trufflehog to find it in git history, then purge it and verify removal.  

* Configure git-secrets as a pre-commit hook and prove it blocks a commit containing a fake AWS key pattern.

# **STAGE 4 — Automated Security Monitoring**

*A cluster of small, lightweight tools — most are single-purpose checks that build on context established earlier in this phase.*

## **▌ 10\. securityheaders.com**

*Requires Nonce-Based CSP (Stage 2, Topic 6\\) and Phase 05's Helmet.js — there's nothing meaningful to check until real security headers exist to grade.*

* Running your deployed app's URL (Phase 06\\) through the scan  

* Reading the grade and understanding which specific headers are missing or misconfigured  

* Iterating — fixing flagged headers and re-scanning to confirm

## **▌ 11\. CSP Evaluator**

*A narrower, CSP-specific tool — directly follows securityheaders.com (10) and the Nonce-Based CSP work (Stage 2, Topic 6).*

* Google's CSP Evaluator — analyzing a CSP policy specifically for bypasses and weaknesses, more detailed than securityheaders.com's general header check  

* Common CSP mistakes it catches — overly permissive source lists, missing directives

## **▌ 12\. Dependabot**

* Automated pull requests for outdated/vulnerable dependencies — directly addresses the OWASP Top 10's Vulnerable and Outdated Components category (Stage 2, Topic 4\\)  

* Configuring update frequency and which dependency types to monitor  

* Reviewing and merging Dependabot PRs safely — not blindly auto-merging without checking for breaking changes

## **▌ 13\. npm audit**

*Same category as Dependabot (12) — a manual/CI-triggered check rather than an always-on automated PR bot.*

* Running npm audit locally and interpreting severity levels  

* npm audit fix — automatic remediation, and its limits (not everything can be auto-fixed without a breaking change)  

* Running npm audit as a CI step (Phase 06's GitHub Actions) — failing a build on high-severity vulnerabilities

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — Automated Security Monitoring  (4.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| securityheaders.com | 1 |
| CSP Evaluator | 1 |
| Dependabot | 1 |
| npm audit | 1.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Continuous Security Posture Dashboard" (Medium)**

A small internal report generator that runs securityheaders.com checks, CSP Evaluator, npm audit, and summarizes Dependabot alert status for a project into a single weekly security posture report. Domain: DevSecOps.

**Practice Exercises**

* Run securityheaders.com against a deployed project and fix every header issue it flags.  

* Enable Dependabot on a repo, let it open a real PR for an outdated dependency, and review/merge it properly.  

* Run npm audit, triage findings by real exploitability (not just count), and fix the genuinely dangerous ones.

# **STAGE 5 — Compliance**

*Requires the practical security measures from Stages 1–4 as context — compliance frameworks attest to controls that need to actually exist first; learning the framework before the practice would be abstract.*

## **▌ 14\. SOC 2 Basics**

* What SOC 2 actually attests to — a third-party audit of an organization's security controls, not a certification of a specific product feature  

* The five Trust Service Criteria — security, availability, processing integrity, confidentiality, privacy  

* Why SOC 2 matters commercially — many B2B/enterprise customers require a vendor's SOC 2 report before signing a contract  

* Mapping this phase's practical work (Stages 1–4: load testing, vulnerability scanning, secret management, header hardening) to what a SOC 2 audit would actually examine

## **▌ 15\. GDPR**

*Broader than Phase 10's GDPR Consent Mode, which was scoped narrowly to analytics/tracking consent banners. This topic covers the wider regulatory picture that consent management is only one part of.*

* Data subject rights — right to access, right to erasure ("right to be forgotten"), right to data portability, and what implementing each actually requires technically (e.g. can your Prisma/PostgreSQL schema from Phase 05 support exporting or deleting all of a single user's data cleanly?)  

* Lawful basis for processing — consent (Phase 10\\) is only one of several lawful bases; understanding the others (contract, legitimate interest)  

* Breach notification requirements — the 72-hour reporting obligation, and why Phase 10's monitoring/alerting stack is what makes detecting a breach in time even possible  

* Data Processing Agreements (DPAs) — the contractual layer between you and any third-party service handling user data on your behalf (Resend, PostHog, Razorpay — all from Phase 09/10)

# **Build in Public — This Phase's Portfolio Moment**

*Restated from the official curriculum.*

* Post: "I ran a load test on my own app and here's what broke" — an honest post about a real weakness found and fixed. This is the direct payoff of Stage 1's k6 work (Topic 1\\) combined with whatever it actually revealed once correlated against Phase 10's observability stack.  

* GitHub: add a SECURITY.md and note your OWASP Top 10 checks (Stage 2, Topic 4\\) in the README — a concrete, checkable artifact, not just a claim.  

* Where \+ when: dev.to, one post, at the end of this phase.

### **Stage 5 Projects & Exercises — Compliance  (5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| SOC 2 Basics | 2 |
| GDPR | 3 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"SOC 2 & GDPR Readiness Checklist Implementation" (Hard)**

Produce a real, project-specific SOC 2-informed security control checklist and GDPR data-handling checklist for an earlier project, then implement the 5 highest-priority gaps found (e.g., access logging, data export/deletion endpoints) rather than just documenting them. Domain: compliance / security engineering.

**Practice Exercises**

* Implement a user data export endpoint (GDPR "right to access") that returns all personal data held about a user in a structured format.  

* Implement a user data deletion endpoint (GDPR "right to erasure") including a documented approach for data that must be retained (e.g., financial records).  

* Write a SOC 2-style access control policy for who can access production data and under what conditions.

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Bastion" — A Load-Tested, Security-Hardened Production Audit**

A full offensive-and-defensive audit of an earlier full-stack project: k6 and Artillery load tests establishing real breaking points and p99 latency under realistic traffic simulation, a documented OWASP Top 10 self-audit with real vulnerabilities found and fixed, a nonce-based strict CSP and JWT algorithm pinning implemented and verified, automated secret scanning wired into CI, a continuous security-header/dependency-audit reporting pipeline, and a GDPR-compliant data export/deletion implementation backed by a written SOC 2-informed access control policy. Domain: application security / SRE. Why it matters: this is the project that proves you don’t just build features — you can find out where your own system breaks and where it’s vulnerable, and actually fix it, which is precisely what separates confident production engineers from tutorial-followers.

# **The Perfect Learning Sequence**

*Phase 15 — Build Tooling \+ CSS-in-JS, Reordered by Dependency (Full Depth)*

**SCOPE NOTICE: This version deliberately expands beyond your official Phase 15 budget, the same way the deep versions of Phases 01–14 expanded beyond their official budgets. Treat the hour figures next to each topic name as the OFFICIAL budget reference only — the sub-topic list below each one is denser than that budget supports.**

**SOURCE DISCREPANCY: The official document header states "2 WEEKS · 70 HOURS," but the topic table's own PHASE TOTAL row sums to 34 hours (25h learn \+ 1h problems \+ 3h project \+ 5h ClientSync \\= 34h, not 70h). Consistent with the same kind of header/table mismatch already flagged in Phases 09–14 — noted here rather than silently picking one number. This is the largest proportional mismatch yet (roughly double).**

**OVERLAP NOTICE: Linaria (Topic 9 here) is a zero-runtime CSS-in-JS library, conceptually the same category as vanilla-extract which Phase 03 already covered in depth. Both compile styles to static CSS at build time rather than generating them at runtime. This phase's Linaria section is scoped as a direct comparison to vanilla-extract, not a repeat of the same zero-runtime concepts from scratch.**

This phase does not end at a named Exit Point — it sits between Exit D (Phase 12\\) and Exit E (later). Per the official curriculum, this phase also has no Build in Public component: build tooling changes aren't visible or interesting to an outside audience, and that guidance is preserved here rather than inventing a portfolio moment that doesn't fit. Resequenced by real dependency: build tools themselves → bundle analysis (which needs a real build to analyze, ordered by which build system was already known vs just learned) → performance auditing (which needs bundle-size awareness as one of its inputs) → CSS-in-JS, runtime-based libraries before the zero-runtime alternative, so the tradeoff Linaria addresses is understood rather than assumed.

# **STAGE 1 — Build Tools**

*Foundational — everything else in this phase either analyzes what these tools produce (Stage 2\\) or measures the result of using them (Stage 3).*

## **▌ 1\. Vite**

**Dev Server**

* Native ES modules in development — why Vite's dev server starts almost instantly compared to bundler-based dev servers (no need to bundle the entire app before serving the first request)  

* Hot Module Replacement (HMR) — how Vite updates modules in the browser without a full page reload  

* Plugin system — Vite's Rollup-compatible plugin API

**Production Builds**

* Vite uses Rollup under the hood for production builds — a different tool than the dev server, worth knowing explicitly since it explains Stage 2's rollup-plugin-visualizer relevance  

* Code splitting and chunk strategy in a Vite production build  

* Environment variables and mode-based configuration (dev vs production)

**Context Relative to Next.js**

* Where Vite fits for projects that aren't using Next.js's App Router (Phase 02\\) — plain React SPAs, non-Next.js tooling contexts  

* Vite as the underlying dev/build tool for many non-Next.js frameworks and library projects

## **▌ 2\. tsup**

*Conceptually distinct from Vite (1) — Vite bundles applications, tsup bundles libraries/packages for publishing (e.g. to npm). Grouped here as the other half of the build-tooling picture.*

* Why publishing a library needs different bundling concerns than shipping an app — multiple output formats (CJS, ESM), type declaration generation  

* tsup's esbuild-based speed — fast builds for package authoring  

* Configuring entry points and output formats for a real npm-publishable package  

* When you'd actually need this — publishing a shared component library or utility package, not typical for a single product application

### **Stage 1 Projects & Exercises — Build Tools  (6 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| Vite | 4 |
| tsup | 2 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Framework-Free Vite App from Scratch" (Easy)**

Configure a Vite project from an empty folder (no create-vite scaffold) with TypeScript, path aliases, and environment variable handling, understanding every config option added. Domain: build tooling literacy.

**"Dual-Format Publishable Library with tsup" (Medium)**

Take a utility library from Phase 1/4 and configure tsup to build it as both ESM and CJS output with generated type declarations, ready for real npm publishing. Domain: library authoring / open source.

**Practice Exercises**

* Configure Vite path aliases (@/components) from scratch and verify both dev server and production build resolve them correctly.  

* Build a library with tsup producing both .mjs and .cjs outputs, and write a small test importing it both ways.  

* Add an environment-variable-driven feature flag to a Vite app and verify it’s correctly excluded from production if disabled.

# **STAGE 2 — Bundle Analysis**

*Three tools, each attached to a different build system — ordered by which build system context you already have: Next.js (Phase 02, long since known) first, Vite (Stage 1, just learned) second, and raw Rollup (the most general-purpose, underlying both Vite's production build and usable standalone) last.*

## **▌ 3\. @next/bundle-analyzer**

*Attaches to Next.js (Phase 02), already deeply familiar by this point in the curriculum — the most immediately applicable of the three analyzers.*

* Generating a visual treemap of what's actually in your Next.js production bundle  

* Identifying unexpectedly large dependencies — a common discovery being an entire library imported for one small utility function  

* Connecting bundle size back to Phase 02's code-splitting and dynamic import (React.lazy) discussion — bundle analysis is what tells you where those techniques would actually help

## **▌ 4\. vite-bundle-visualizer**

*Same category as @next/bundle-analyzer (3), attached to Vite (Stage 1, Topic 1\\) instead of Next.js.*

* Same treemap visualization concept as Topic 3, applied to a Vite production build  

* Differences in what's typically visible — Vite's more granular chunk splitting compared to Next.js's conventions

## **▌ 5\. rollup-plugin-visualizer**

*The most general-purpose of the three — since Vite (Stage 1\\) uses Rollup for production builds, this tool can analyze the same output as vite-bundle-visualizer (4), and is also usable standalone for any Rollup-based build outside Vite entirely.*

* Using rollup-plugin-visualizer directly in a Rollup config (or via Vite's Rollup-compatible plugin system, Stage 1\\) for finer-grained control than the wrapper tools (3, 4\\) offer  

* When to reach for the underlying Rollup tool directly instead of a framework-specific wrapper — custom build setups, library bundling scenarios (ties back to tsup, Stage 1, Topic 2\\)

### **Stage 2 Projects & Exercises — Bundle Analysis  (3.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| @next/bundle-analyzer | 1.5 |
| vite-bundle-visualizer | 1 |
| rollup-plugin-visualizer | 1 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Bundle Size Audit & Diet" (Medium)**

Take a real project, run @next/bundle-analyzer (or vite-bundle-visualizer for non-Next projects), identify the 3 largest unnecessary dependencies or duplicated code, and reduce total bundle size by a documented, measurable percentage.

**Practice Exercises**

* Run a bundle analyzer on a real project and identify one dependency that’s larger than expected.  

* Replace one heavy dependency with a lighter alternative (or a hand-written equivalent) and measure the bundle size reduction.  

* Use rollup-plugin-visualizer on a Vite-built library to find and eliminate duplicate code across chunks.

# **STAGE 3 — Performance Auditing**

## **▌ 6\. lighthouse-ci**

*Requires Phase 06's GitHub Actions to run in as an automated CI step, and bundle-size awareness from Stage 2 as one of the contributing factors to the performance scores Lighthouse measures.*

* Running Lighthouse audits automatically on every PR/deploy, instead of manually checking scores in Chrome DevTools  

* The four Lighthouse categories — performance, accessibility, best practices, SEO — and what each actually measures  

* Setting budgets — failing CI (Phase 06\\) if a score or specific metric (e.g. bundle size, connecting back to Stage 2\\) regresses below a threshold  

* Core Web Vitals specifically — LCP, INP, CLS — and why these are the metrics that actually correlate with user-perceived performance and search ranking

### **Stage 3 Projects & Exercises — Performance Auditing  (2.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| lighthouse-ci | 2.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"CI-Enforced Performance Budget" (Medium–Hard)**

Wire lighthouse-ci into a project’s CI pipeline (tying back to Phase 6\\) so a PR fails if Lighthouse performance score drops below a set threshold, with a demonstrated failing PR that gets fixed before merge.

**Practice Exercises**

* Configure lighthouse-ci with a performance budget and prove a deliberately bloated asset triggers a CI failure.  

* Fix a real Lighthouse-flagged issue (e.g., unused CSS, unoptimized image) and confirm the score improves in a rerun.

# **STAGE 4 — CSS-in-JS**

*An independent theme from Stages 1–3. Runtime-based libraries (styled-components, Emotion) are taught before the zero-runtime alternative (Linaria) so the tradeoff Linaria addresses — CSS-in-JS ergonomics without runtime cost — is understood as a response to a real limitation, not learned in isolation.*

## **▌ 7\. styled-components**

*The original and most widely-adopted CSS-in-JS library — foundational to this cluster.*

* Tagged template literals — writing actual CSS inside JavaScript, with real syntax highlighting and autocomplete support in most editors  

* Component-scoped styles — each styled component generates its own uniquely-scoped class, avoiding the global-namespace problem of plain CSS (Phase 03\\)  

* Dynamic styling based on props — styles that change based on component state, without manually toggling classNames (Phase 02/03's clsx/CVA approach)  

* Theming via ThemeProvider — a runtime theming mechanism, contrasted with Phase 03's CSS Custom Properties \+ next-themes approach  

* The runtime cost — styled-components generates and injects CSS at runtime in the browser, which is the specific tradeoff Stage 4's later topics (Linaria) exist to avoid

## **▌ 8\. Emotion**

*Same category as styled-components (7) — a very similar API, placed immediately after for direct comparison.*

* API parity with styled-components — tagged template literals and a similar component-scoping model  

* Emotion's css prop — an alternative, slightly more flexible authoring style than styled-components' component-wrapping pattern  

* Performance differences and bundle size comparison between the two libraries  

* Why teams pick one over the other in practice — largely ecosystem/preference rather than a sharp technical divide

## **▌ 9\. Linaria**

*Zero-runtime CSS-in-JS — placed last since it's the response to the specific runtime-cost tradeoff flagged in styled-components (7). Conceptually the same category as Phase 03's vanilla-extract, and framed here as a direct comparison to it, not a repeat.*

* The zero-runtime approach — Linaria extracts styles to static CSS files at build time, using a build-time transform rather than generating styles in the browser  

* CSS-in-JS authoring syntax (similar to styled-components/Emotion) with none of their runtime cost — the specific gap this fills  

* Direct comparison to vanilla-extract (Phase 03\\) — both are zero-runtime, but Linaria keeps closer syntactic similarity to styled-components/Emotion, while vanilla-extract uses a more TypeScript-native styling API  

* Build tooling requirements — Linaria needs its own bundler integration (ties back to Stage 1's Vite, or webpack/Next.js's build pipeline) to perform the build-time extraction

### **Stage 4 Projects & Exercises — CSS-in-JS  (7.5 hrs)**

**Curriculum Topics**

| Topic | Hours |
| :---- | :---- |
| styled-components | 3 |
| Emotion | 2 |
| Linaria | 2.5 |

**Projects (Easy → Hard)**

**"Themeable Component Library, Three Ways" (Hard)**

Rebuild the same 4-component set using styled-components, Emotion, and Linaria (zero-runtime) respectively, then write a hands-on comparison of DX, runtime performance cost, and SSR complexity for each. Domain: frontend architecture decision-making.

**Practice Exercises**

* Build a themeable Button in styled-components using a ThemeProvider.  

* Build the same Button in Emotion using the css prop and compare the authoring experience.  

* Build the same Button in Linaria and measure whether any runtime CSS-in-JS cost was actually eliminated (check the compiled output).

## **▌ Phase Exit Capstone — "Featherweight" — A Bundle-Size-Obsessed Component Library**

Publish a small, genuinely optimized component library: authored and bundled with tsup (dual ESM/CJS \+ types), a documented before/after bundle-size reduction case study using bundle analyzers, a CI pipeline enforcing a Lighthouse performance budget on the accompanying demo site, and a written comparative report on styled-components vs. Emotion vs. Linaria implementations of the same components with real measured runtime cost differences. Domain: frontend performance / library engineering. Why it matters: most engineers never measure what their styling/build choices actually cost; this project builds the habit of verifying performance claims with real numbers instead of assumptions.
